ISPE Good Practice Guide: Single-Use Technology

Selection, Design, Implementation, and Qualification of Single-Use Systems in Pharmaceutical Manufacturing

Section 0: 1. Introduction & Key Concepts

p7-p12

ISPE Good Practice Guide: Single-Use Technology | p7 - p12

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the industry background of Single-Use Technology (SUT) and how it reduces risk, shortens facility build time and lowers capital investment at the same time
  • Grasp the purpose and scope of this guide, know which applications it covers (liquid storage and transfer, cell culture, fill/finish, etc.), and be clear about the prerequisites for using it
  • Become familiar with the structure of the guide's three core chapters: Chapter 2 Component Selection and Design, Chapter 3 Implementation and Use, Chapter 4 Project Management
  • Know the definitions of key SUT terms precisely and distinguish easily confused concepts such as Extractables, Leachables, SUS and SUA
  • Understand the hierarchy of the toxicological thresholds (AET, TTC, QT, SCT) as a foundation for the later compatibility assessments
  • Be able to tell the real difference between the terms "Single-Use" and "Disposable" and avoid mixing them in GMP documents
1.1 Background

Original Text

The development of drug products from the laboratory to production processes entails risks, investment, and time. Any technology that can impact one or more of these factors is worth an investigation. Conventional stainless steel process equipment has reliably served the industry for many years. With the increasing need to reduce risks and quickly start up processing plants with minimum capital costs, Single-Use Technology (SUT) has been more widely used. This technology has impacted all three factors: risks, investment, and time.

The growth of SUT has been rapid and continues to expand in the industry. The primary components of SUT are polymeric film containers, flexible tubing, and an assortment of connectors and supporting components. The evolution of SUT has prompted the industry to redefine procedures to incorporate SUT into drug manufacturing processes. The application of this Guide starts after a decision to implement SUT is made; that is, all financial assessments have been completed and the results of those assessments support the use of SUT in a process. This Guide is aimed at helping the end-users and suppliers of SUT after this decision has been made.

While SUT provides flexibility, the implementation of SUT into a process requires a well-defined plan that minimizes surprises during the later stages of implementation. This can be a challenge when dealing with a revolutionary technology that is flexible, continues to evolve, and can be customized to the end-user's requirements. To take advantage of the flexibility of SUT, it is important to understand how the single-use products can work together with manufacturing operations. This Guide intends to provide a roadmap for the efficient implementation of SUT with minimum disruption to existing operations.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Three Drivers of SUT

Single-Use Technology (SUT) has risen quickly in the biopharmaceutical industry because it addresses three pain points of traditional stainless steel equipment at once:

  • Risk: removes the cross-contamination risk tied to cleaning validation, because every batch uses brand-new equipment
  • Investment: no fixed piping to install and less CIP/SIP infrastructure, so capital expenditure drops substantially
  • Time: shorter facility build time, which suits rapid start-up of clinical batches or urgent drug production particularly well

Starting point for applying this guide: the organization has completed its financial evaluation and decided to adopt SUT. The guide does not discuss "whether to use it" but "how to use it well".

Analogy

Think of SUT as a takeout lunch box versus traditional porcelain dinnerware. Porcelain is durable and reusable, but after every use it must be washed, disinfected and checked for damage (equivalent to CIP/SIP plus cleaning validation). A takeout box is thrown away after use, which saves the cleaning effort, but if you choose the wrong box material, the food may absorb plasticizers. This is exactly SUT's biggest compliance challenge: material compatibility and control of leachables.

Key Notes

The original text stresses that SUT is a "revolutionary technology that is flexible, continues to evolve". This means regulatory guidance will always lag behind technical development, so in practice you need close communication between suppliers and users rather than simply copying regulatory text.

CDMO Application Insights

For a CDMO (contract development and manufacturing organization), the flexibility of SUT is especially valuable. Products for different clients are switched quickly in the same facility, and the "discard after use" nature of SUT naturally addresses cross-contamination concerns in a multi-product plant. It also greatly reduces the cleaning validation burden of changeover, which is the core business reason a CDMO chooses SUT.

1.2 Purpose and Objectives

Original Text

This Guide is centered around getting SUT implementation done right the first time, on schedule, and with minimal costs and few surprises. When surprises arise, this Guide aims to help address them effectively with the least amount of disruption.

This Guide aims to make it easier for single-use product suppliers and drug product manufacturers to select components and suppliers, design, and apply SUT. It intends to achieve this by aligning expectations and capabilities to minimize surprises.

Due to the inherent flexibility of SUT, the process of selecting, designing, and implementing SUT should be performed with sufficient focus while keeping the end state in clear perspective. During the design phase, there are many decisions to be made about the components to use. Focusing too closely on the decision at hand and putting the overall objective in second place can result in a decision that will need to be changed at a later date. The highly flexible nature of SUT allows this to be accomplished. However, it may result in a significant cost later in the project. If too many decisions are made in this way, the flexibility becomes its own hindrance. The objective of this Guide is to provide an efficient roadmap, based on the experience of the authors.

1.2.1 Scope

It is recognized that innovation and the fast evolution of SUT leads to a continuous expansion of available products. This Guide intends to capture the current technology, the components, and how they are designed into processes. The information presented is useful in the following applications:

  • Liquid or semisolid storage and transfer
  • Cell propagation and production of bulk biologics
  • Mixing of powders and liquids
  • Filtration and centrifugation
  • Heat transfer
  • Chromatography
  • Fill/finish

1.2.2 Benefits

The intended benefits of this Guide include:

  • Learning how to select single-use components and design functional systems
  • Learning when and how to perform effective extractables and leachables studies
  • Learning how to evaluate suppliers of SUT
  • Understanding the critical supplier and end-user (customer) interactions
  • Learning about the interrelated tasks for implementing SUT

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The "Right the First Time" Philosophy

The original text repeatedly stresses "done right the first time" and "minimize surprises". This is more than a slogan; it reflects the main ways SUT projects fail:

  • Compatibility testing skipped early on, leachables found above limits after scale-up, entire batch scrapped
  • Component design ignores the overall flow path, so the assembly drawings need major rework later
  • Supplier lead times not confirmed in advance, clinical batch delayed

The core value of this guide: learn from other people's mistakes so you do not repeat them yourself.

Analogy

Designing an SUT is like building a house. If the designer only looks at "where does this door go" each time and forgets the circulation of the whole house, you may end up with the kitchen door hitting the refrigerator. The high flexibility of SUT (every component can be swapped) is in fact a trap: the easier it is to change, the easier it is to get lost in the details and lose sight of the big picture.

Key Notes

Scope: The guide covers the full SUT application chain from liquid storage and transfer to Fill/Finish. Note that fill/finish (aseptic filling) is explicitly included in the scope. This is the most critical application for a sterile drug product CDMO and also the step under the strictest regulatory scrutiny.

Importance of E&L studies: "Learning when and how to perform effective extractables and leachables studies" is listed as the first benefit, which shows that E&L is the core technical hurdle in implementing SUT.

CDMO Application Insights

For a sterile Fill/Finish CDMO, the scope of SUT directly covers the core business: bag filling, disposable filters and single-use tubing systems. Before implementation you must establish a clear supplier audit process (Chapter 3) and E&L assessment criteria (Chapter 2); otherwise "flexibility" turns into "loss of control".

Practice Questions

  1. Which of the scenarios in the table above does SUT cover at your site today? Which scenarios have not been implemented yet, and why?
  2. In SUT implementation projects, what "surprises" have you discovered only after the fact? How can you identify them earlier in the next project?
1.3 Structure of the Guide

Original Text

This Guide is intended to be used by those new to SUT while still benefiting those experienced with SUT. While any section of the Guide can be used at any time, those new to the technology would benefit by following a logical progression through the chapters. There are three core chapters. Chapter 2 guides the reader through component selection and equipment design. Chapter 3 takes the equipment into the plant and includes validation and training to yield a successful operation. Chapter 4 deals with program management topics that occur throughout the activities discussed in Chapters 2 and 3.

An overview of the Guide is as follows:

  • Introduction: This chapter provides the overall objectives of the Guide, background information on the technology, and the intended benefits of the Guide.
  • Selection and Design: Chapter 2 is intended to help the end-user select the components, assemblies, systems, and suppliers. The various sections in the chapter focus on identifying the major criteria for selecting the components for the single-use equipment and the suppliers that can support a successful SUT operation. The chapter details how assemblies are designed and aligned with the multiple-use parts of the system. Chapter 2 deals primarily with the first phase of any SUT implementation process. These activities require strong collaboration between the suppliers and the end-users. An emphasis is placed on how these collaborations are to be maintained and what information is expected to be shared between the two groups.
  • Implementation and Use: Chapter 3 covers topics which are to follow the completion of the activities of Chapter 2. Once the single-use assemblies, systems, and suppliers have been selected as described in Chapter 2, the focus is on using the single-use products in the end-user operation. Chapter 3 deals with the transfer of the technology into the plant, meeting regulatory requirements, training, validation, and setting up a strong supply chain. The end-users would take leadership in these activities with the supplier providing support.
  • Program Management: Chapter 4 mostly addresses planning information, including risk management, change management, and project schedules.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Logic of the Three-Chapter Structure

The three chapters correspond to the three main activity streams of SUT implementation:

  • Chapter 2: Selection and Design: supplier-led, with active user participation. The focus is "what to choose" and "how to design it".
  • Chapter 3: Implementation and Use: user-led, with supplier support. The focus is "how to make it work properly in the plant", including validation, training and supply chain.
  • Chapter 4: Project Management: horizontal management activities that run through the entire project: risk management, change management and schedule control.

This structure reflects an important principle of division of responsibility: at purchase the supplier is the expert; after implementation the user must become the owner.

Analogy

The three chapters resemble the three phases of starting up a new plant: Chapter 2 is "procurement and design drawings", Chapter 3 is "construction and acceptance", and Chapter 4 is "the project manager watching the site throughout". Without any one of them, an SUT project can fail just short of success.

Key Notes

The original text stresses "strong collaboration between the suppliers and the end-users". SUT is not a product you can use as soon as you buy it; it is a collaborative relationship that needs continuous communication from both sides. This means the purchase contract must clearly set out "information sharing obligations", including notification of material changes, batch test data and E&L study results.

CDMO Application Insights

A CDMO plays a dual role in the SUT supply chain: to its clients it is a "manufacturing service provider", and to SUT suppliers it is a "demanding technical end user". We recommend establishing a Supplier Qualification procedure for SUT suppliers, turning the requirements of Chapters 2 and 3 into internal SOPs, and clearly defining the allocation of SUT-related quality responsibilities in client technical agreements.

1.4 Key Concepts/Terms: Toxicology and Compatibility Terms (Group 1)

Original Text

Analytical Evaluation Threshold (AET)

Analytical Evaluation Threshold (AET): An upper limit, at or above which, identification and quantification of an unknown extractables and leachables should be performed and reported for potential toxicological assessment. This is not applicable to special case compounds such as Polyaromatic Hydrocarbon (PAH, also known as polynuclear aromatic hydrocarbons), Mercaptobenzothiazole (MBT) and N-nitrosamine, which should be evaluated individually.

Compatibility

Compatibility: A measure of the extent to which a Primary Packaging Component (PPC), Process Contact Material (PCM), and/or proximal material will interact with a dosage form. Such interaction should not be sufficient to cause unacceptable changes in the quality of either the dosage form or the packaging component. Such interactions may include (ab)adsorption of the active drug substance, reduction in the concentration of an excipient, leachable-induced degradation, precipitation, changes in drug product pH, discoloration of the dosage form or packaging component, etc.

Container Closure System (CCS)

Container Closure System (CCS): The sum of packaging components that together contain, protect, and deliver the dosage form. This includes primary packaging components and secondary packaging components if the latter are intended to provide additional protection relative to product stability to the drug product (e.g., foil pouch).

Extractables

Extractables: Chemical compounds that are removed from a material by exertion of an artificial, exaggerated force (e.g., solvent, temperature, or time). This is a material specific characteristic and is independent of the drug product with which the material is used.

Leachables

Leachables: Chemical species that migrate from or through a contact surface into a drug product or process stream during storage or normal use conditions. These are specific to the combination of material and drug product with which the material comes in contact.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Fundamental Difference in E&L

Extractables and Leachables are the two core concepts of SUT safety assessment and must be strictly distinguished:

  • Extractables: compounds forcibly extracted from a material under "extreme conditions" (high-concentration solvents, high temperature, long duration). They are a property of the material itself and are independent of the actual product. The supplier provides the test data.
  • Leachables: compounds that migrate into the drug product under "actual use conditions" (normal storage, normal contact). They are a property of the material plus drug product combination and must be tested for the specific product.

A simple way to remember: Extractables are the "worst case" and Leachables are the "real case". Leachables are usually a subset of Extractables.

Core Concept: The Role of the AET

The Analytical Evaluation Threshold (AET) is the bridge between analytical chemistry and toxicology. Unknown compounds above the AET must be identified, quantified and reported for potential toxicological assessment; compounds below the AET can be "reported but not identified".

Exceptions (require individual assessment): PAHs (polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons), MBT (mercaptobenzothiazole) and N-nitrosamines. These three groups are known to be highly toxic or carcinogenic, so the logic that "low concentrations can be ignored" does not apply.

Analogy

Think of the CCS (container closure system) as the "home of the drug product". The primary packaging component (PPC) is the "bedroom wall" in direct contact with the drug product, and secondary packaging is the "exterior wall". If the exterior wall provides extra insulation (protecting product stability), it also counts as part of the CCS (such as an aluminum foil pouch). Compatibility assessment is about making sure the "building materials do not contaminate the occupant".

Key Notes

The scope of a compatibility assessment is very broad. It is not only whether the material leaches toxic substances, but also adsorption/absorption of the active ingredient, a drop in excipient concentration, pH change, discoloration and so on. Even when no toxicity is involved, these interactions can cause the product to fall out of specification, and they are likewise a source of CAPAs.

CDMO Application Insights

At a sterile Fill/Finish CDMO, a written compatibility assessment plan is required before each new product introduces any new SUT component. We recommend explicitly requiring, in the Technology Transfer review, that the client provide or commission E&L studies, and that Leachables confirmation be completed before commercial-scale production. This is a key control point for reducing the risk of batch loss.

1.4 Key Concepts/Terms: Components, Systems and Threshold Terms (Group 2)

Original Text

Primary Packaging Component (PPC)

Primary Packaging Component (PPC): A component of the container closure system that potentially comes into direct contact with the drug product formulation (e.g., canisters, pumps, actuators, gaskets, syringe plungers, stoppers, etc.).

Process Contact Material (PCM)

Process Contact Material (PCM): Component which is in direct contact with the process or product fluid, such as tubing, hose, filter, bag, connector, etc. Also known as process stream contact material.

Proximal Materials

Proximal Materials: All packaging materials other than the PPC, such as ink, adhesive, label, carton, protective packaging materials, etc.

Qualification Threshold (QT)

Qualification Threshold (QT): A level below which a given leachable is not considered for safety qualification (toxicological assessment) unless the leachable presents Structure-Activity Relationship (SAR) concerns.

Safety Concern Threshold (SCT)

Safety Concern Threshold (SCT): A level below which a leachable would have a dose so low as to present negligible safety concerns from carcinogenic and non-carcinogenic toxic effects.

Single-Use Assembly (SUA)

Single-Use Assembly: A combination of single-use components/assemblies designed to be in one continuous, and often closed, wetted flow path. The typical assembly is manufactured and sterilized. It primarily contains mechanical components (e.g., bioprocess container, tubing, fittings, etc.) with sensor element(s) or allowance for a sensor element to be mounted by the end-user. The end-user places the assembly on a support structure prior to filling it with fluid. The support structure may also include electromechanical elements (e.g., mixer/motor, bag lift, scale, bag carrier, etc.) that interface with the assembly and are integral for its proper use.

Single-Use System (SUS)

Single-Use System (SUS): A combination of single-use components designed to be in one continuous, and often closed, wetted flow path. The typical SUS integrates one or several single-use assemblies with multiple-use electrical/control elements operated by some level of automation. The end-user places the single-use assembly on a support structure prior to filling it with fluid.

Single-Use Technology (SUT)

Single-Use Technology (SUT): Technology based on applications that utilize single-use components individually or in assemblies and systems that are designed based on these components. Figure 1.1🔍 provides an overall perspective on the relationship between components, assemblies, and systems.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The SUT Hierarchy (Component → Assembly → System)

SUT has a clear three-level structure that must be distinguished explicitly:

  • Component: the most basic unit, such as tubing, connectors, filters and bags
  • Single-Use Assembly (SUA): multiple components combined into one complete wetted flow path, sterilized before shipment. It is the core deliverable of SUT.
  • Single-Use System (SUS): an SUA integrated with reusable electrical/control and automation elements. An SUS is a complete operating unit combining "software and hardware".
  • SUT: the broadest term, covering the technical concepts of all the levels above.

Key distinction: the SUA is the "discard after use" part; the electrical and control parts of an SUS (motors, sensors) are reusable.

Core Concept: The Hierarchy of Toxicological Thresholds

Leachables safety assessment uses three thresholds, from strictest to most lenient:

  • SCT (Safety Concern Threshold): the strictest baseline. Below this value, both carcinogenic and non-carcinogenic toxicity can be disregarded.
  • QT (Qualification Threshold): below this value, toxicological assessment is waived, unless there is a SAR (structure-activity relationship) concern.
  • AET (Analytical Evaluation Threshold): the trigger point at the analytical chemistry level; unknowns at or above this value must be identified and quantified.

Generally SCT ≤ QT ≤ AET: the lower the value, the stricter the requirement.

Analogy

PPC versus PCM: a PPC is the "container that holds the drug product" (syringe plungers, vial stoppers) and the emphasis is on packaging integrity and contact with the final product; a PCM is the "conduit that contacts the drug solution during the process" (flexible tubing, filter membranes, bioreactor bags) and the emphasis is on contact within the process flow path. Both require E&L assessment, but the regulatory frameworks differ slightly (ICH Q3D and USP <661> versus the BPOG/ASTM E55 series).

Key Notes

Proximal Materials are often overlooked: label inks, adhesives and outer carton board can also migrate indirectly and affect drug product quality, especially for highly permeable packaging materials (such as some polymer bags). ICH Q3D and USP <1664> both contain corresponding requirements for assessing proximal materials.

SAR (structure-activity relationship): even if a compound's concentration is below the QT, if its chemical structure indicates potential mutagenicity it still needs individual assessment. This is why PAHs, MBT and N-nitrosamines are excluded from the general AET rule.

CDMO Application Insights

In SUT supplier evaluation, require suppliers to provide the full basis for their AET/QT/SCT calculations and the E&L study report for the SUA (usually following the BPOG methodology). A CDMO's quality agreement should clearly state the supplier's obligation to give advance notice of material changes, because any change to a PCM material may trigger a repeat E&L assessment and affect the client's drug product launch timeline.

Practice Questions

  1. How does your site currently manage notification of material changes from SUT suppliers? Is there an SOP that explicitly requires suppliers to give advance notice before a change?
  2. When evaluating a new SUT supplier, which E&L-related documents would you ask for? How do you judge the quality of the Extractables data the supplier provides?
1.4 Key Concepts/Terms: Other Important Terms (Group 3)

Original Text

Single-Use versus Disposable

While these terms seem to be used interchangeably, there are significant differences between them. Single-use products are a subset of disposable products. Single-use products are used one time and then discarded. They are typically used for the duration of a process run. At the end of a process run, any drug product is displaced from the single-use product and then the single-use product is discarded. Disposable products are often used only once and discarded. However, disposable products may also be used more than once. If the disposable product is to be used more than once, it is cleaned and sanitized between uses. Performance should be monitored as it normally decreases with each sequential use. Exposure to cleaning chemicals and high temperatures tends to degrade the disposable products resulting in a limited useful life.

Support Equipment/Systems

Support Equipment/Systems: Support systems that are used in conjunction with single-use equipment. These include temperature control systems for heating/cooling media and restricted access barrier systems.

Threshold of Toxicological Concern (TTC)

Threshold of Toxicological Concern (TTC): The daily intake of a genotoxic impurity that is considered to be associated with an acceptable risk (excess cancer risk of < 1 in 100,000 over a lifetime) for most pharmaceuticals.

Toxicological Assessment

Toxicological Assessment: An evaluation of the estimated Total Daily Intake (TDI), which is considered similar to the Average Daily Intake (ADI) of a chemical species, in order to determine if the level of exposure will present a safety concern to the patient. These evaluations include considerations of available literature data for the specific compound or class of compounds, SAR, and any qualification data (if available).

Tubing Set

Tubing Set: An assembly composed of single-use components that is used to connect between unit operations.

Upstream/Downstream Processing

Upstream/Downstream Processing: Upstream processing includes the unit operations up to the harvest clarification and recovery from the bioreactor. Downstream processing includes the unit operations that follow such as the filtration and chromatography steps to purify and concentrate the product.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Key Difference Between Single-Use and Disposable

These are the terms most commonly mixed up in the industry, and they must be strictly distinguished in GMP documents:

  • Single-Use: used once for one process run and discarded when the run ends. No cleaning is performed and performance on reuse is not considered. This is the essential definition of SUT.
  • Disposable: a broader concept that includes single-use, but also covers products that can be used several times before being discarded. A multi-use disposable needs cleaning and disinfection, and its performance declines with each use.

Practical implication: if a GMP document says "disposable tubing set", a regulatory inspector may ask whether performance over multiple uses has been validated. Rewriting it as "single-use tubing set" avoids this ambiguity.

Core Concept: The Toxicological Definition of TTC

Threshold of Toxicological Concern (TTC): for genotoxic impurities, a daily intake below this threshold is accepted as carrying an excess lifetime cancer risk of < 1/100,000. TTC is a core concept of the ICH M7 guideline on genotoxic impurities and is directly relevant to E&L safety assessment of SUT: when a leachable is suspected to be genotoxic, it must be compared with the TTC.

Analogy

A simple way to remember upstream/downstream: picture the bioprocess as "upstream: the farm (growing cells, culturing) → downstream: the food factory (purification, concentration, formulation)". Upstream ends at harvest from the bioreactor; downstream runs from post-harvest filtration, chromatography and formulation through to aseptic filling. SUT is widely used in both stages, but each emphasizes different types of components.

Key Notes

Support Equipment/Systems are not SUT themselves but directly affect the operational reliability of SUT. For example, the interface design between a Restricted Access Barrier System (RABS) and single-use glove ports is a key integration point in sterile Fill/Finish. If a temperature control system (heating/cooling) fails, it may affect the mechanical integrity of SUT components and lead to system leaks.

CDMO Application Insights

The most critical SUT applications at a sterile Fill/Finish CDMO happen to sit at the end of downstream: sterile filtration, filling tubing and container closure systems all fall within the scope of SUT. The TTC concept reminds us that for biologics given at high injection doses (such as intravenous infusion), acceptable Leachables limits are much stricter than for oral products, and the dose assumed when calculating the AET must be based on the worst-case clinical route of administration.

Practice Questions

  1. In your site's SOPs, is it clear whether "single-use" or "disposable" is used? If both are mixed, how should the terminology be standardized at the next SOP revision?
  2. When calculating the AET for leachables, how do you determine the "worst-case" daily intake? How much does the route of administration (intravenous versus subcutaneous) change the result?

Chapter Summary

  • Three value propositions of SUT: lower risk (no cleaning validation), less investment (lighter infrastructure), shorter timelines (fast start-up). The premise of this guide: the organization has already decided to adopt SUT, and the aim is "how to do it right" rather than "whether to do it".
  • Guide structure: Chapter 2 (Selection and Design) → Chapter 3 (Implementation and Use) → Chapter 4 (Project Management). The three chapters form a complete SUT implementation lifecycle framework, with Chapter 4 running throughout.
  • Terminology precision: Single-Use ≠ Disposable; SUA (assembly) ≠ SUS (system); Extractables (forced extraction in the laboratory) ≠ Leachables (migration under actual use). The correct terms must be used strictly in GMP documents and supplier agreements.
  • Hierarchy of toxicological thresholds: SCT (strictest) → QT (next strictest) → AET (analytical trigger). TTC applies to genotoxic compounds; PAHs, MBT and N-nitrosamines need individual assessment and do not fall under the general threshold logic.
  • Key concerns for a CDMO: E&L assessment is the technical hurdle for SUT implementation, supplier change management is the main source of supply chain risk, and without systematic design thinking the flexibility of SUT becomes a root cause of project delays.
  • Supplier and user collaboration: the supplier leads at the selection stage, and after implementation the user must become the owner. Information sharing, notification of material changes and the boundaries of technical support all need to be documented in advance in the quality agreement.
1a

Section 1a: 2.1–2.2: Components & Extractables

p13-p28

ISPE Good Practice Guide: Single-Use Technology | p13 - p28

Learning Objectives

  • Distinguish the three levels of single-use components, assemblies and systems (SUS), and understand the classification logic for wetted and non-wetted components
  • Become familiar with the functions and characteristics of the main single-use components (bioprocess bags, tubing, connectors, filters, sensors, valves, etc.) and how they are applied in the sterile filling process
  • Understand the essential difference between Extractables and Leachables, and the central role of E&L studies in drug product safety assessment
  • Grasp the design space concept for extractables studies, including QbD principles, tiering by critical application, and the PCM/PPC/CCS hierarchy
  • Understand the methodology of extractables testing: model solvent selection, extraction parameter setting, analytical techniques (GC-MS, LC-MS, ICP-MS) and judging results against the AET acceptance criterion
  • Build a CDMO perspective on E&L risk management: the full workflow from reviewing supplier data and assigning responsibilities in a responsibility matrix to reaching a toxicological assessment conclusion
2.1 Single-Use Components, Assemblies, and Systems

Original Text

This section lists the single-use products that are commonly used in pharmaceutical manufacturing facilities. Aligned with Figure 1.1🔍, single-use products can be categorized as follows:

  • Single-use components represent the single-use parts that may be used individually or as a subset of an SUS
  • Single-use assemblies consist of multiple single-use components connected in various configurations to make a unit operation in the manufacturing process
  • SUSs integrate single-use components/assemblies with multiple-use parts
Note: This section is intended to cover components, assemblies, and systems most commonly used in SUT and is not intended to be a comprehensive listing.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Three Levels (Component → Assembly → System)

ISPE SUT uses a clear three-level classification to describe the scope of single-use technology:

  • Component: the smallest unit, for example a length of tubing or a connector
  • Assembly: several components combined into one process operation unit, for example a "filtration assembly" = filter + tubing + connectors
  • System (SUS): an assembly integrated with multi-use equipment (such as a stainless steel frame or pump drive) into a complete piece of equipment

This three-level structure matters a great deal for E&L studies: extraction studies usually start at the component level and then extend to leaching studies at the system level.

Analogy

Think of the three levels as building a house: components = bricks and rebar (individual materials); assemblies = a wall or a room (functional units); systems = the whole building (a complete facility including plumbing and electrical services). In material safety testing, you first test the chemical release of the bricks, then the indoor air quality of the whole building.

CDMO Application Scenario

In day-to-day CDMO operations, responsibility for managing the three levels differs:

  • Component: the supplier provides the CoA (Certificate of Analysis) and the E&L data package
  • Assembly: usually assembled to a custom design by the CDMO or the supplier according to the URS, and requires integrity testing
  • System: the CDMO is responsible for equipment qualification (IQ/OQ/PQ) and operating SOPs
2.1.1 Single-Use Components

Original Text

Components can be classified into two categories, based on whether the surface(s) will be exposed to a fluid (liquid or gas) when in service:

  • Wetted components contact the product or process fluid
  • Non-wetted components do not contact the product or process fluid but may be in contact with wetted components

Wetted components may be further classified based on their potential contact time with product or process fluid, as shown in Figure 2.1.

Bioprocess Bags

Bioprocess bags (also referred to as single-use bioprocess containers) are flexible containers that are made of polymeric material and intended to be used once and discarded. The main applications are storage, sampling, mixing, freezing, and transport of liquids. Other applications include cell culture, fermentation, and powder storage/transfer.

These containers have inlet/outlet ports with fittings and associated tubing. The smaller bioprocess bags (typically 30 mL to 50 L) are typically two-dimensional (2-D) and expand like a pillow or sack when filled. As the sizes get larger (typically 100 L to 3,000 L), the bioprocess bags are three-dimensional (3-D) and typically match the shape of the bag container they will be placed into during use (e.g., stainless steel support structures/frames or totes).

Bottles

Bottles are rigid plastic vessels with volumes that typically range from 1 L to 20 L. Applications for bottles include sampling, storage, and freezing. They may be reusable (multiple-use) for non-critical applications.

Impellers/Mixers

Impellers/mixers are used to agitate fluid in a container. Common types include:

  • Impeller with magnetic or magnetic-like drive
  • Impeller with levitating drive
  • Mechanical mixing (e.g., paddle or agitator shaft)

Tubing

Tubing is manufactured from flexible polymer material and used for fluid transfer with or without pumping. Tubing is available in various diameters, lengths, wall thickness, and shore hardness. Materials of construction vary and include fluoropolymers, thermoplastics, and silicones. Thermoplastic tubing can be heat sealed (for aseptic disconnection) and welded (for tube/tube aseptic connection). Certain types of tubing are specifically designed to be used with peristaltic pumps or to withstand high pressures.

Port Fittings

Port fittings are heat sealed to the chamber portion of a bioprocess bag and used to connect tubing, allowing for fluid to enter or exit the bags. Port fittings can also be found on bottles, and these would normally be accompanied by a seal.

Connectors

Tubing connectors contain hose barb fittings, and may be used to connect tubing of different materials of construction and/or diameter sizes:

  • Two pieces of tubing with straight connector
  • Three pieces of tubing with T or Y shape connector
  • Four pieces of tubing with + shape connector

Connectors vary depending on the type of application:

  • When ease of use is important but aseptic connections are not required: Luer-Lok® connectors, quick connects, and sanitary clamp fittings (Tri-Clamp®). These connectors may be handled within a laminar flow cabinet (ISO 5/Grade A conditions) if aseptic connections are required.
  • When aseptic connections are required, the connectors should prevent any interaction between the fluid and the outside environment:
    • Equal complementary elements (genderless connectors): two elements that are exactly alike and interchangeable
    • Matched complementary elements (gendered connectors): two elements that are matched and can only be connected together

Steam-in-place connectors may be used between single-use components and stainless steel systems.

Disconnectors

Disconnectors are used to make aseptic disconnections:

  • Fluid path aseptic disconnectors: need to be pre-assembled on the single-use assembly, usually through hose barb fittings
  • Non-fluid path disconnectors: can be pre-installed or standalone, based on an external part that is pressed and cut to allow aseptic disconnection

Filter Capsules

Filter capsules of numerous types are used in single-use assemblies. Filters may be classified by:

  • Filtration mode: dead-end filtration versus tangential flow filtration
  • Type of filtration: Sterilizing grade / Pre-filtration / Depth filtration
  • Materials of construction
  • Configuration: Parallel plate / Pleated membranes / Non-woven / Hollow fiber
  • Filter media features: Polymer type (e.g., cellulose acetate, polyamide, polyether sulfone, PVDF, PTFE, PP, PE, glass fiber), filter pore size, filter surface area, Hydrophobic/Hydrophilic
  • Connection style: Hose barb / Sanitary

Valves

Valves are used to direct the flow of fluid either as wetted components or as controlled constrictions acting on tubing. There are also simple pinch valves that have a decreasing channel that squeezes the tubing to control the flow.

Sensors

Sensors of several types have been specifically developed to be single-use and can be integrated into single-use assemblies. Currently available single-use sensor types include:

  • Conductivity / Dissolved Oxygen (DO) / Flow / pH / Pressure / Temperature

For applications where single-use sensors are not available, multiple-use sensors may be used via aseptic connections or sealed barrier. Common multiple-use sensor types include: Dissolved gases / Level / Mixing speed / Turbidity / UV absorbance / Weight (load cells).

Other Components

Pump liners are polymeric components within the pump that make up the fluid path and create pressure; replaced after each use. Centrifuge liners are polymeric liners designed for use in centrifuge systems. Gaskets and O-rings are used in connections between sanitary fittings, within valves, and between two solid components. Materials include EPDM, PTFE, Viton®, and silicone. Filling needles are used in final filling operations, primarily made of stainless steel. Chromatography components include pre-packed columns (usually not single-use) and membrane chromatography equipment (usually single-use). Clamps and fasteners are non-wetted components used to secure tubing connections.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Wetted vs Non-Wetted Components

This classification is critical for E&L studies. Wetted components contact the product or process fluid directly; they are a potential source of chemical contamination and must undergo a full extractables study. Non-wetted components such as clamps and fasteners do not touch the fluid directly, but if they can transfer substances indirectly through wetted components they also need to be assessed.

Wetted components are further tiered by contact time: the E&L risks of static long-term contact (such as a storage bag) and dynamic short-term contact (such as a bioreactor bag) are quite different and call for different extraction conditions.

Key Component: Bioprocess Bag

The bioprocess bag is the largest component in SUT by volume and the one that draws the most E&L attention. Several key parameters:

  • Material: multilayer polymer film (usually containing LDPE, EVA, etc.), with each layer serving a different function (barrier layer, strength layer, contact layer)
  • 2-D vs 3-D: 30 mL–50 L bags are 2-D pillow bags; 100 L–3,000 L bags are 3-D and need a stainless steel support frame
  • Gamma irradiation: a pre-sterilization treatment, but irradiation changes polymer chemistry and may increase extractables, so it is a key input to the E&L study

Key Component: Tubing

Tubing is the single-use component with the largest liquid contact area, and it is also the direct target of peristaltic pump wear:

  • Silicone: flexible and biocompatible, the first choice for peristaltic pumps, but carries a risk of leaching silicon oxides
  • Fluoropolymer: highly chemically inert and suited to demanding solvent resistance, but stiffer
  • Thermoplastic: can be heat-sealed or heat-welded, enabling sterile connection and disconnection

Key Component: Aseptic Connectors

Aseptic connectors are a highly competitive area of innovation in SUT. The key distinction:

  • Genderless: any half can mate with any other, which keeps inventory management simple
  • Gendered: male/female pairing, designed to prevent incorrect connections
  • Steam-in-place (SIP) connectors: used as the bridge between SUT and conventional stainless steel systems

Key Notes: Key Classification of Filters

In aseptic processing, the most critical filter is the sterilizing-grade filter, with a pore size ≤0.22 μm, validated by the ASTM F838 bubble point test and bacterial retention test.

Tangential flow filtration (TFF) is used for ultrafiltration/concentration processes. Its flow path design is complex, so it is usually supplied as a pre-designed assembly and is not suitable for ad hoc assembly.

Analogy: Single-Use vs Multi-Use Sensors

Compare a disposable rapid test kit (single-use sensor) with a laboratory analyzer (multi-use sensor): the rapid test is convenient but has limited accuracy, while the analyzer is accurate but needs cleaning and calibration. In SUT, the accuracy of single-use sensors for pH, dissolved oxygen and similar measurements has improved greatly, but some high-precision applications (such as turbidity and UV absorbance) still rely on multi-use sensors inserted through a sterile membrane.

CDMO Application Scenario: Component Selection Decision Tree

When a CDMO takes on a new client's tech transfer, component selection should consider:

  • Is the brand the client uses at its original site compatible with our suppliers? (standardization of connector interfaces)
  • Does the E&L data package for product-contact components meet ICH Q3D elemental impurity and AET requirements?
  • Does the gamma irradiation dose meet our site acceptance specification? (usually 25–45 kGy)
  • Has the peristaltic pump tubing passed pump tubing validation (particulate test for wear)?

Practice Questions

  1. In your filling plant, which components fall into the high-risk "wetted + long contact time" category? Does the supplier's E&L data package cover post-gamma-irradiation conditions?
  2. What combination of tubing materials does your plant use today? Are there cases where silicone and thermoplastic tubing are mixed? How is sterility maintained at the connection points?
2.1.2 Single-Use Assemblies

Original Text

Single-use assemblies can range from relatively simple units (such as buffer bag assemblies) to complex units (such as bioreactor assemblies). Examples of single-use assemblies include:

  • Buffer storage assemblies
  • Mixer assemblies
  • Sample manifold assemblies
  • Transfer manifold assemblies
  • Bioreactor assemblies
  • Filtration assemblies
  • Chromatography assemblies
  • Centrifuge assemblies
  • Fill/finish manifold assemblies

Single-use assemblies are available as standardized products or may be customized for specific process needs. Considerations should be made to design single-use assemblies that can be used in multiple steps within a manufacturing facility, benefiting the overall operation in terms of flexibility and minimization of inventory.

2.1.2.1 Bioprocess Bag Assemblies

Bioprocess bags are often bulky and impact space requirements for storage areas. Selecting standardized (versus customized) bags and connections can help to reduce inventory levels, since one assembly design can potentially be used in multiple steps in the process.

  • 2-D Bioprocess Bags: Standard specifications include tubing dimensions for the integral tubing and standardized connectors for sampling. Use of standard designs is recommended to minimize cost and provide flexibility between assemblies.
  • 3-D Bioprocess Bags: Most tubing entry points for the larger 3-D bioprocess bags are from the top. Care should be taken when adding auxiliary components to these entry points as they may require special handling to protect the bag surface.

During selection of the bioprocess container, attention should be taken to ensure that the minimum mixing volume is met and that ample ports are available for additions and sampling.

2.1.2.2 Manifolds

Manifolds can range from being straightforward to complex in design. They are commonly the second bulkiest portion of the assembly, after the bioprocess bag. Aspects of manifold design and selection include:

  • Sizing of tubing diameters and minimizing lengths and bends are important for proper fluid handling.
  • The pressure rating of the tubing should be matched for the application; reinforced tubing is available for applications above the pressure rating of standard tubing.
  • Welds and aseptic connections should be used as needed to maintain sterility and flexibility.
  • For processes that allow ports to be combined, manifolds can allow the use of standard bioprocess containers without modifications to the number of connections.

2.1.2.3 Filter Assemblies

Typical assemblies consist of normal flow filters (dead-end filters) that are relatively small (less than 10 inch). Depending on whether the application is an open or closed operation, vent filters may be required on bioprocess containers. Vent filters on bioreactors are typically full size capsule filters provided in a redundant pair with heated jackets. Tangential flow filters are commonly incorporated into pre-designed assemblies to allow for special flow paths required for their operation.

2.1.2.4 Filling Assemblies

Final filling assemblies are specially designed filter assemblies that account for factors such as:

  • Venting the filters in a sterile manner
  • Filter flush collection
  • Low particulate requirements
  • Peristaltic pump tubing, if required
  • Surge bags, if required
  • Single-use needles, if required

2.1.2.5 Mixer Assemblies

Mixer assemblies can also range from simple to complex in design, due to the need for different mixing requirements, probes, avoidance of dead legs, and sampling requirements. Mixer types include bottom mount agitator, top mount agitator, and pump around. There are also different options for how the single-use impeller is coupled to the motor; special attention should be made for high viscosity, high solids, or low particulate applications.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Standardized vs Custom Assembly Design

Advantages of choosing standardized assemblies: fewer inventory items, reuse across process steps, a stable supply chain and already-validated E&L data. Situations that call for custom assemblies: the process has special interface requirements, the client specifies the supplier, or the cleaning/sterilization method is unusual.

The direction recommended by the ISPE guide: standardize wherever you can. This is also one of the key strategies for a CDMO to reduce COGs (Cost of Goods).

Key Concept: Design Points for Tubing Manifolds

A manifold is the core assembly where multiple flow paths merge or split; poor design leads to:

  • Dead legs: stagnant zones where fluid does not move and microorganisms can grow; the rule of thumb is that branch length must not exceed 6 times the tube diameter
  • Pressure loss: too many elbows or sudden narrowing of the bore cause a pressure drop different from what was expected
  • Integrity risk: too many connection points; every weld or connector is a potential leak point

Key Notes: What Makes Filling Assemblies Special

The filling assembly is the highest-risk assembly in the whole aseptic process, because:

  • It is closest to the final product, with no later dilution or filtration step to reduce risk
  • Particulate requirements are the strictest: any rubber wear debris or silicone particles could become foreign matter in an injectable
  • Peristaltic pump tubing must pass pump wear validation to confirm that particle shedding is within acceptable limits

This echoes the "tiering by critical application" principle in Section 2.2.4.3: the filling step is in the highest risk class.

Analogy: Choosing the Mixer for a Mixing Assembly

Picture three kitchen mixing methods: a top-mounted mixer (like a household stand mixer) suits medium-viscosity fluids that need an open port for additions; a bottom-mounted mixer (like a coffee grinder) suits closed systems and more uniform fluids; pump-around (like an aquarium filter pump) suits large volumes and gentle mixing of shear-sensitive biologics (such as mAb drug substance). Choose the wrong mixing method and protein aggregation will occur.

CDMO Application Scenario: Risk Management of the Filling Assembly

In validating a single-use filling system, a CDMO's practical work items include:

  • Peristaltic pump tubing wear test (sample and analyze for particulates after >10 hours of simulated running)
  • Material compatibility confirmation for filling needles (316L stainless steel vs polymer filling needles)
  • Assessment of the effect of the surge bag on filling rate stability
  • Aseptic validation of the filter venting procedure (to prevent outside contamination entering during venting)

Practice Questions

  1. Are the filling line assemblies at your plant standardized or custom? Which validation items must be repeated when you change supplier?
  2. How are dead legs avoided in manifold design? What does your current SOP require for dead leg management?
2.1.3 Single-Use Systems

Original Text

SUSs, as shown in Figure 1.1🔍, are comprehensive units. SUSs are composed of single-use components/assemblies and multiple-use parts. In general, the multiple-use parts are used to:

  • Support and provide mobility to the single-use assemblies
  • Control the motion of fluids within and between single-use assemblies
  • Monitor operating conditions
  • Provide process controls for the unit operation or to the entire manufacturing process

The multiple-use parts in an SUS are often complex equipment and considerations should be made to how they interface with single-use components/assemblies. For example, wear or deformation of multiple-use parts can have disastrous impact on the integrity of the single-use components.

Multiple-use parts represent conventional equipment, which are not covered in this Guide. To help the end-user select the appropriate equipment for SUSs, this Guide highlights concepts and criteria in Section 2.3 (Process Equipment Design).

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Risks of Multi-Use Equipment in an SUS

The risk most easily overlooked in an SUS: the effect of multi-use parts on the integrity of the single-use components.

  • Wear on the peristaltic pump head → tubing deforms under stress → wall thins → burst risk
  • Sharp corners or burrs on the stainless steel support frame → bag is scratched or torn → loss of integrity
  • Excessive torque from a valve actuator → uneven pressure on the tubing → leakage

This means that validating an SUS is not only about testing the single-use assemblies; you must also confirm that the multi-use equipment, within its normal wear range, does not affect assembly integrity.

CDMO Application Scenario: Defining the Scope of SUS Validation

When drawing up the IQ/OQ/PQ plan for an SUS, you need to define clearly:

  • Which parts are "single-use assemblies" (replaced every batch; the supplier is responsible for the E&L data)
  • Which parts are "multi-use equipment" (need equipment qualification; the CDMO maintains them)
  • The frequency and method of integrity testing at the interface between the two

Practice Questions

  1. Which parts of the bioreactor systems at your plant (such as Sartorius Biostat® or Thermo Fisher HyPerforma™) are multi-use equipment? How does their wear affect the integrity of the single-use bags?
  2. When multi-use equipment is sent out for maintenance or repair, which re-qualification steps are needed after it returns to the plant?
2.2 Extractables: Overview

Original Text

Single-use products are made from polymeric materials; one of the key elements to qualifying and implementing such a material is to understand and control the impacts of using it within a defined process. Extractable and leachable (E&L) profiles are a main part of this required impact assessment.

Note: Although "extractables and leachables" is commonly used as one term, these concepts reflect two distinctly different ways of determining the chemical species that may migrate from the component:
  • Extractables are removed from the component by exaggerated force, involving high temperatures, high and low pH, and aggressive solvents; this information can be useful for assessing the risk of the component in any application.
  • Leachables, on the other hand, are a result of the interaction of the production within the equipment; therefore, related studies use actual product and process conditions to determine which chemical components will migrate from the component. When actual product is not possible, a mimic of the product might be used.

A science and risk-based approach to an extractables study is recommended due to its complexity and the need for trace level analysis. Risk factors include, but are not limited to: surface area to volume ratio, extracting potential of the process stream or formulation, and duration of exposure. For example, a storage bag may be static in process contact but long in duration, while a bioreactor bag may be dynamic in process contact but short in the duration of contact.

Because this Guide deals with SUT applied in the processing of products which are intermediates, the focus of this section is on extractables as it applies to process contact materials (PCM), and not to finished dosage forms or containers.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Extractables vs Leachables, the Fundamental Difference

This is the most important conceptual distinction in SUT and is very often confused in exams and regulatory reviews:

  • Extractables (E): chemical substances forcibly extracted under "exaggerated conditions" (high temperature, extreme pH, organic solvents). The aim is to build a "chemical fingerprint" library of the material, representing the maximum possible chemical risk. It is like a "criminal interrogation", forcing out every chemical that could possibly come out.
  • Leachables (L): chemical substances that migrate out naturally under actual process/product conditions, representing the real patient exposure risk. It is like "everyday contact", the amount actually released in a real environment.

Logical relationship: Extractables ⊇ Leachables (extractables are a superset of leachables). Do the E study first, then use the E results to predict L risk and decide whether a further L study is needed.

Key Parameters for Risk Assessment

E&L risk ∝ surface area/volume ratio (SA/V) × extraction potential × contact time

High-risk scenario: small volume + large contact area (such as filling tubing)
Low-risk scenario: large volume + short contact time (such as a transfer bag, contact ≤1 hr)

AET (Analytical Evaluation Threshold) calculation:
AET = SCT × (weight/daily dose) × (1/extraction efficiency)
SCT = Safety Concern Threshold (ICH Q3D or toxicological TTC value)

Analogy: E vs L Is Like Food Packaging Testing

Picture a plastic water bottle: an extractables study is like soaking the bottle in hot water + alcohol + acid to force extraction, testing what could come out in the worst case; a leachables study is like filling the bottle with water, leaving it at room temperature for 6 months and then testing the water, to see what you actually drink under real use conditions. The former is the safety baseline study; the latter confirms the real-world risk.

Key Notes: What Is Special About a CDMO as an Intermediate Process

The original text points out that the E&L focus of this guide is on process contact materials (PCM) rather than final dosage form containers. This matters for a CDMO:

  • A CDMO handles "drug substance" intermediates, not the final product that contacts the patient directly
  • But for some process steps (such as filling drug substance into DS storage bags), the MAH (marketing authorization holder) may require the E&L data to be included in the regulatory submission
  • Responsibility for providing E&L data must be clearly defined in the client contract

Practice Questions

  1. Which process step at your plant has the highest SA/V ratio? Does the existing E&L assessment cover the worst-case conditions of that step?
  2. When a client asks "do your single-use storage bags have E&L data?", what is your standard response process? Who provides which documents?
2.2.1 Roles and Responsibilities

Original Text

Extractables studies represent one of the most challenging aspects of pharmaceutical development. There is a large degree of complexity given by the number of factors that influence an extractables evaluation. The involvement of teams with adequate skills and subject matter expertise from both end-users and suppliers help in tackling each factor efficiently.

Table 2.1 provides a template of the typical deliverables responsibilities matrix for an extractables study. Allocation of these responsibilities varies depending on company policy and procedures.

R=Responsible, A=Accountable, C=Consulted, I=Informed

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Applying the RACI Matrix to E&L Studies

The RACI matrix (Responsible / Accountable / Consulted / Informed) is a standard tool for cross-organization collaboration. In E&L studies, the division of responsibility between the end user (CDMO/pharmaceutical company) and the supplier is the area most likely to cause disputes.

Table 2.1 shows several key principles:

  • URS definition: led by the end user (A/R), with the supplier only Informed. This means you must first know what you need and then ask the supplier to provide it
  • Extraction procedure execution: led by the supplier (A/R), with all end users Informed. The supplier's laboratory capability is the key
  • Toxicological assessment: end-user Quality/Regulatory is R and the SME is A. Responsibility for the toxicological conclusion sits with the user

Key Notes: The Role of the SME (Subject Matter Expert)

Cross-disciplinary expertise needed for E&L studies:

  • Analytical chemistry: development and validation of GC-MS, LC-MS and ICP-MS methods
  • Toxicology: TTC (Threshold of Toxicological Concern) assessment and ICH Q3D elemental impurity risk scoring
  • Polymer science: knowledge of the known degradation products of specific polymers (for example the cyclic siloxanes D4 and D5 from silicone)
  • Regulatory affairs: cross-referencing the requirements of ICH Q3D, USP <661>, EP 3.1 and FDA/EMA E&L guidance

A CDMO without full SME capability will usually need to outsource the assessment to a specialist toxicology CRO.

CDMO Application Scenario: E&L Questionnaire in Supplier Audits

In supplier qualification, require the supplier to provide:

  • A summary of the E&L study report (does it cover the extraction conditions of the intended application?)
  • The analytical methods used and their detection limits (LOD/LOQ)
  • Whether an AET assessment has been done, with the toxicological assessment conclusion
  • Lot-to-lot variability data (at least 3 lots)
  • Whether E&L data after gamma irradiation are included in the report

Practice Questions

  1. Does your plant's current SUT supplier audit process require an E&L data package? If not, at which step would it be most efficient to add it?
  2. Who is responsible for the toxicological assessment in your organization: internal QA/RA or an outsourced CRO? What are the limitations of each?
2.2.2 Pre-requirements

Original Text

Components which are planned for an extractables study should first meet the following regulatory and pharmacopeial expectations:

  • Requirements for polymeric materials:
    • Plastic polymers used in manufacturing single-use components should not contain natural rubber, latex, or special case compounds (PAH, N-nitrosamines, and MBT)
    • USP <381> Elastomeric Closure for Injection
    • USP <661> Plastic Packaging Systems and Their Materials of Construction
    • European Pharmacopoeia (EP) General Chapter 3.1 Materials Used for the Manufacture of Containers
  • Requirements for major compendia:
    • USP <87> Biological Reactivity, In Vitro — in vitro testing is a minimum requirement
    • USP <88> Biological Reactivity, In Vivo — in vivo testing is preferred
    • EP 3.2.9, JP Section 7.03, and corresponding ISO 10993-1 tests for in vitro and in vivo testing
  • Animal derived Stearates: EU Commission Decision 97/534/EC

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Prerequisites for an E&L Study

Before a formal E&L study, the material must first pass these "basic qualification checks", like a job applicant who needs to meet the minimum education requirement before reaching the interview stage.

The most critical prohibited ingredients:

  • Natural rubber/latex: an allergen, absolutely prohibited for injectable drug products
  • Polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAH): carcinogens, usually originating from carbon black or mineral oil lubricants
  • N-Nitrosamines: genotoxic impurities under ICH M7, with a very low TTC (1.5 μg/day)
  • MBT (mercaptobenzothiazole): a rubber vulcanization accelerator and potential carcinogen

CDMO Application Scenario: Key Points of Supplier CoA Review

When a new lot of single-use assemblies arrives, confirm the following in the CoA review:

  • Material declaration: does it state that the material is free of latex/PAH/N-nitrosamines/MBT?
  • USP <87>/<88> test results (passes)
  • EP 3.1 or an equivalent declaration
  • Declaration for animal-derived raw materials (for example, if stearates are present, compliance with 97/534/EC is required)
2.2.3 Risk Assessment in Extractables Study

Original Text

In defining requirements for the design of extractables studies, the risk management tools described in ICH Q9 should be employed. For example, application of quality risk management would consider:

  • If an automated component manufacturing process is used
  • If the process has been satisfactorily audited
  • If a smaller sample may be needed, as opposed to a manually intensive manufacturing and/or inspection process for the component which may dictate a larger sample because of higher probability of variability

SUS extractables testing approach and methodology should be determined based on scientific knowledge and regulatory expectations. Decision trees and other risk assessment tools should be implemented at an early stage of the extractables study definition.

The purpose of these studies is to obtain extractables profiles of SUS components considered as PCM in direct contact with the process or product fluid. The risk assessment should consider both patient safety (with regard to the impact of extractables compounds on product quality and safety) and the overall process performance. Extractables studies should integrate regulatory expectations as inputs of the risk assessment. Patient safety should be considered by suppliers in the development of single-use components, and by end-users in the implementation of these components in the drug manufacturing process.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Applying ICH Q9 Risk Management to E&L Studies

Specific applications of the core ICH Q9 tools (FMEA, HACCP, risk matrix) in E&L studies:

  • Automated vs manual manufacturing: automated processes have low lot-to-lot variation and may allow fewer lots; manual processes vary more and need more lots to cover the variability
  • Chemical fingerprint: the ultimate goal of an E&L study is to build an "identity DNA" for each single-use component, containing qualitative and quantitative data on all potential migrants

Key Notes: The Dual Responsibility View

The original text stresses that the supplier and the end user each have responsibilities:

  • Supplier responsibility: consider patient safety from the component development stage, choose low-migration polymers and provide a complete E&L data package
  • End-user (CDMO) responsibility: when introducing a component into the process, perform a risk assessment and confirm that the supplier's E&L data apply to your own process conditions (temperature, pH and contact time may differ from the supplier's standard test conditions)

This is the most common blind spot: the supplier's standard extraction conditions do not necessarily cover the specific process conditions of the CDMO's clients.

CDMO Application Scenario: Building an E&L Risk Assessment Matrix

We recommend building the E&L risk matrix along these dimensions:

  • Contact time: <1 hr (low) / 1–24 hr (medium) / >24 hr (high)
  • Contact area: SA/V ratio <1 cm⁻¹ (low) / 1–10 cm⁻¹ (medium) / >10 cm⁻¹ (high)
  • Process position: upstream (low) / downstream (medium) / pre-filling (high)
  • Temperature: room temperature (low) / 37°C culture (medium) / >50°C (high)

The matrix score determines the depth of E&L study needed (supplier data are sufficient / supplementary study needed / full in-house study needed).

2.2.4 Design Space

Original Text

2.2.4.1 Basis for Comparative Evaluation

The starting point for an extractables study should be a review of the supplier's data. Ideally, these studies should be performed on materials at the component level, under standardized conditions of temperature, time, surface area, etc. (so the data are comparable) which are representative of the intended use, including steam sterilization or gamma irradiation. Using this data, the end-user can then calculate the maximum amount of extractables based on the surface area and other conditions in the process.

2.2.4.2 Extractables Study Design Space

The extractables study should be developed based on Quality by Design (QbD) principles described in ICH Q8 to gather all attributes and parameters that enter into the determination of a design space. Scientific variables should be identified to set up Design of Experiment (DOE) for the extraction procedure.

2.2.4.3 Critical Applications

Different requirements of the extractables study could be applied depending on the criticality of application, context, and utilization of SUS components, as illustrated in Figure 2.2 (Criticality Scale of Industrial Application).

Figure 2.3🔍 provides a high-level overview of the development stages in a typical biological manufacturing process, in relation to increased risk associated with materials safety requirements; that is, the closer the single-use component is to the fill/finish step, the less opportunity that risk mitigation (such as dilution or filtration) can take place.

2.2.4.4 Primary Packaging Component, Process Contact Material, and Container Closure Systems

Extractables studies are usually performed on individual PPCs (Primary Packaging Components) and PCMs (Process Contact Materials), as a precursor step to leachables studies which are performed on entire systems considered as CCSs (Container Closure Systems). E&L studies should demonstrate the safety and chemical compatibility of the CCS, PCM, and proximal materials used in the processing and intermediate storage of a drug substance.

The duration of exposure to these materials varies greatly depending upon the specific process. Compatible packaging and contact materials should not cause changes to the efficacy, potency, and safety of the drug product.

2.2.4.5 Decision Tree for Approval of Materials Based on Extractables Study

Figure 2.5🔍 illustrates typical key aspects that should be considered in the approval of materials based on an extractables study. The decision tree guides whether extractables data is acceptable, or whether further toxicological assessment or targeted studies are required.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Applying QbD + DOE to E&L Studies

Applying the QbD approach of ICH Q8 to E&L studies:

  • Critical Quality Attributes (CQA): which extractable compounds affect patient safety? (determined by toxicological screening)
  • Critical Process Parameters (CPP): how do temperature, pH, contact time and SA/V ratio affect the amount extracted?
  • Design Space: within which parameter ranges does the extractable concentration always stay below the AET?
  • DOE design: systematically vary the extraction conditions (for example a 2×2 factorial design: low/high temperature × low/high pH) to find the worst-case combination

Key Concept: The CCS / PPC / PCM Hierarchy

These three abbreviations appear frequently in regulatory documents and must be clearly distinguished:

  • CCS (container closure system): the broadest term, meaning the whole container + closure system, the subject of ICH Q1B and ICH Q3B studies
  • PPC (primary packaging component): packaging material in direct contact with the final drug product (such as vials, rubber stoppers and aluminum caps)
  • PCM (process contact material): material that contacts the intermediate during processing (the main subject of SUT studies)

In a biologics CDMO:
PCM = single-use bags and tubing (DS manufacturing stage)
PPC = vials and stoppers (after DP filling)
CCS = the vial + stopper + aluminum cap as a whole system

Logic of Tiering by Critical Application

Effect of process position on E&L risk:

Upstream (cell culture/fermentation)
  ↓ multiple purification steps can remove migrants
  ↓ ion exchange chromatography, virus filtration, ultrafiltration
  ↓
Downstream (purified drug substance)
  ↓ only one sterilizing filtration left
  ↓
Filling/finishing (Fill/Finish) ← highest risk
  ↓ no later removal mechanism
  ↓ goes directly into the final product

Principle: the closer to filling, the stricter the E&L requirements

Key Notes: From Supplier Data to End-User Calculation

Supplier E&L data are usually expressed as "amount extracted per unit area (μg/cm²)". The end user (CDMO) must convert them into the actual process exposure:

Actual exposure (μg/L) = supplier data (μg/cm²) × actual SA (cm²) ÷ batch volume (L)

Then compare with the AET to decide whether an additional targeted study is needed.

CDMO Application Scenario: Practical Execution of the Decision Tree

Standard handling paths when the E&L assessment reaches the following conclusions:

  • Calculated exposure < AET: document the assessment conclusion; no additional testing needed; review periodically
  • Calculated exposure > AET but supported by toxicological data: perform a full toxicological assessment and document the rationale for risk acceptance
  • Calculated exposure > AET with insufficient toxicological data: consider changing supplier, changing the component material, or adding a targeted E&L study
  • Known genotoxic compound present: even if below the general AET, ICH M7 must be met (TTC 1.5 μg/day)
2.2.5 Key Recommendations for Extractables Study

Original Text

Extractables should be readily identified, quantified, and evaluated for their impact on drug quality (and consequently patient safety) arising from the interaction between process fluids (simulated by model solvents) and polymeric PCM. In the extractables study, the single-use component is considered as PCM. The objective is to obtain a chemical fingerprint of the tested single-use components. The information from the extractables study should be used to support the selection of raw materials in the single-use component manufacturing process.

The overall approach to conducting an extractables study is:

  • Benchmarking of previous extractables testing protocols and results for comparable single-use components
  • Determination of acceptance criteria based on user requirements, the intended design and use of the single-use component, and scientific literature
  • Performing the extraction procedure on the single-use component to be tested
  • Comparison of actual results to acceptance criteria and performing toxicological assessment with conclusion

2.2.5.1 Requirements for Processing and Storage of PPC/PCM

The PPC and PCM should be processed in the same manner in which they will be processed for use in manufacturing. This includes steps such as washing, lubrication/coating, packaging, and sterilization. They should be held at their specified storage conditions, or as otherwise justified.

2.2.5.2 Requirements for Including Proximal Packaging Components

Packaging components and materials which do not directly contact the drug product should be evaluated on a case-by-case basis for their potential to leach chemicals into the dosage form. Polymeric containers with a glass transition temperature below room temperature have a high potential for migration of chemical entities through them. Packaging components (such as label stock, carton, adhesives, inks, varnishes, and lacquers) should be included in the overall extractables evaluation due to the potential for migration through the polymeric material.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Chemical Fingerprint

The final deliverable of an E&L study is a "chemical fingerprint" library containing:

  • A list of identified compounds (qualitative: identified by GC-MS, LC-MS)
  • The concentration of each compound (quantitative: compared with the AET)
  • The toxicological assessment conclusion (whether further attention is needed)

This fingerprint library is material-specific: if the supplier changes, the resin lot changes or the manufacturing process changes, you must reassess whether the fingerprint library still applies.

Core Concept: Effect of Sterilization Conditions on E&L

2.2.5.1 stresses that components should already have gone through the same treatments as in actual use before extraction testing, and sterilization is the most critical:

  • Gamma irradiation: radiation energy causes polymer chain scission and free-radical reactions, which may generate new degradation products (such as the low-molecular-weight cyclic siloxanes D4/D5 in silicone)
  • Autoclave: high temperature and humidity may accelerate hydrolysis and release additives from the polymer
  • Ethylene oxide (EtO): EtO residues and their reaction products (ethylene glycol, ethylene chlorohydrin) need to be included in the E&L assessment

Key Notes: Risk from Proximal Packaging

A frequently overlooked risk: chemicals in the outer packaging (cartons, labels, adhesives, inks) may permeate polymer containers whose glass transition temperature is below room temperature and eventually migrate into the contents. Common high-risk compounds:

  • Photoinitiators in printing inks: such as ITX (isopropylthioxanthone), as in the well-known "Isociate" contamination incident
  • Adhesive solvents: residual toluene and ethyl acetate
  • Mineral oil in paperboard: MOH (Mineral Oil Hydrocarbons) contamination

Analogy: Sterilization Changes E&L Like Cooking Changes Food

Raw chicken (an unsterilized component) and roast chicken (a component after gamma irradiation) have quite different chemical compositions. You cannot use a "food safety analysis" of raw chicken to represent the safety of roast chicken. Likewise, supplier E&L data must state clearly whether they were generated before or after irradiation, and the two cannot be interchanged.

Practice Questions

  1. Were the E&L data from your current SUT suppliers generated before or after gamma irradiation? Is the irradiation dose consistent with your site's receiving specification?
  2. Does the outer packaging of your single-use storage bags use inks containing organic solvents? What is the glass transition temperature? Has a migration assessment of proximal packaging been done?
2.2.6 Extractables Testing

Original Text

2.2.6.1 Selection of PPC/PCM Lots

Where practical, extractables studies of PPC, PCM, and proximal materials should be designed to capture lot-to-lot variability (preferably different resin lots, if possible). Multiple lots of PPC/PCM are recommended, when possible, for extractables evaluation. Non-consecutive lots, if available, should be used in the studies.

Consideration should be made for the large supplier lots that may be received at the end-user facility on different dates or purchase orders. These may be identified as different lots, but in fact are not reflective of supplier process variability. To determine whether or not multiple lots are needed, an assessment of the point of variability in the supply chain (e.g., manufacturing, batch processing, washing, sterilizing, etc.) should be conducted.

The mass to volume ratio and sample size of the PPC/PCM are important inputs to extractables characterization. Sample preparation should be consistent in geometry for the different PPC/PCM lots.
