Consideration can also be made to use a small-scale model for execution of the extractables study to support qualification of the large-scale model. However, the mass to volume ratio should be maintained as close as possible to the ratio of the original intended size.

2.2.6.2 Extraction Conditions and Procedures

Extraction conditions are critical in determining the chemical profile and levels of individual extractables. Prior to the execution of the extraction procedure, critical factors should be determined. An extractables assessment should provide quantitative and qualitative assessment of the safety threshold for individual extractables. The assessment should be based on different key factors, including:

  • Fluid path characterization
  • Nature of single-use components
  • Manufacturing process conditions

These factors are illustrated in Figure 2.6🔍 (Ishikawa principle/Fishbone Diagram), which enables study design and extraction strategies.

The manufacturing process should be evaluated with regard to process conditions, proximity to the final product, and pretreatment procedures (e.g., autoclaving or gamma irradiation).

These critical factors help to determine extraction conditions, which are based on parameters including:

  • Chemical nature of the extracting media (model solvent)
  • Duration of the extraction process and the temperature
  • Stoichiometry of the extraction process: surface area to volume ratio; mass of individual extractable compounds extracted per surface ratio
  • Physical characteristics of the extraction method

2.2.6.3 Extraction Parameters

The extraction conditions (including temperature, pH, and time) should be appropriately selected, well described, and justified. Extraction media may include a drug product vehicle and organic solvents of varying polarities, extreme pH, and extreme ionic strength.

Considerations for extraction parameters include:

  • Selection of appropriately aggressive solvents, rather than simulating the action of the fluid path
  • Performing extractable analysis with multiple substitute solutions — each solution should approximate the effect of one or more of the physical and chemical properties of the fluid path
  • Selection of model solvents based on variability of polarities — the power of model solvent extraction and their variabilities ensure the largest range of extractable compounds are encompassed
  • Volatility of the solvent as a key concern regarding extraction factor and the compatibility of the solvent with the analytical method
  • Determining the volume of extraction media using the surface area of the component in contact with the process fluid path (only the inner surface area in contact with the extraction media is considered)

2.2.6.4 Testing Methodology

The levels of the identified extractable compounds should be analyzed and compared to standard guidelines such as the ICH Q3D, EP General Chapter 3.1, and USP <661>. Common analytical techniques used in extractables testing include:

  • GC-MS (Gas Chromatography–Mass Spectrometry): for volatile and semi-volatile organic compounds
  • LC-MS (Liquid Chromatography–Mass Spectrometry): for non-volatile and polar compounds
  • ICP-MS (Inductively Coupled Plasma–Mass Spectrometry): for elemental impurities (metals)

In addition, refer to a proposed standardized extractables protocol (Ding et al., 2014).

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Scientific Basis for Lot Selection

The design logic behind "non-consecutive lots": the aim is to capture the supplier's process variation (resin lot, manufacturing time, sterilization parameters), not subdivisions of the same large lot.

A common pitfall: you buy 3 lots of single-use bags, but they are all made from the same resin lot within the same week, so these 3 lots do not represent true lot-to-lot variation. The correct approach is to ask the supplier for products made from different resin lot numbers.

AET (Analytical Evaluation Threshold) Calculation

AET definition: extractables below this concentration need no further toxicological attention

Calculation formula (simplified):
AET = SCT ÷ (daily dose volume × extraction efficiency)

Where:
SCT = Safety Concern Threshold
  - General organic compounds TTC = 1.5 μg/day (Kroes et al.)
  - ICH Q3D elemental impurities: by elemental toxicity class (PDE values)
  - Genotoxic compounds (ICH M7) = 1.5 μg/day

Example:
Daily dose volume = 100 mL
SCT = 150 μg/day (after the adjustment factor for general organic compounds)
Extraction efficiency = 50%
AET = 150 ÷ (0.1 L × 0.5) = 3,000 μg/L = 3 ppm

Analogy: The Ishikawa (Fishbone) Diagram in E&L Study Design

Use a fishbone diagram to analyze the factors that affect extraction results, like investigating the root cause of an incident: the main spine is "extractable concentration", and the six bones are "temperature", "pH", "contact time", "SA/V ratio", "solvent type" and "component characteristics". Only by assessing each bone systematically can you design a DOE plan that covers all worst-case conditions.

CDMO Application Scenario: Checklist for Accepting Supplier E&L Data

When a supplier submits an E&L report, CDMO QA should confirm:

  • Were non-consecutive lot numbers used for the test lots (≥3 lots)?
  • Do the extraction conditions cover your process worst case (highest temperature, longest contact time, largest SA/V)?
  • Is the AET calculation in the report based on your product's daily dose? (Different products = different AETs!)
  • Have all three analyses, GC-MS, LC-MS and ICP-MS, been performed? Is the LOQ below 1/5 of the AET?
  • Does every identified compound have a toxicological conclusion (pass / needs attention / needs further testing)?
  • For any compound exceeding the AET, has a full toxicological assessment (HHE) been provided?

Practice Questions

  1. Does the supplier of the single-use storage bags your plant currently uses provide a complete E&L report that includes all three analyses (GC-MS + LC-MS + ICP-MS)? Do the extraction conditions in the report cover your highest storage temperature?
  2. If you need to calculate the AET for a newly introduced mAb drug substance storage bag, what input data do you need? What is your toxicological assessment process? Who is responsible for the final conclusion?

Key Concept: The Logic for Selecting Model Solvents

Model solvents are selected along a polarity gradient so as to cover different types of chemical migrants:

ISPE recommendation: use surface area (not mass) to determine the extraction solvent volume, to keep the SA/V ratio consistent.

Chapter Summary

  • Three-level structure: single-use technology is divided into three levels: component → assembly → system (SUS). Wetted components are the main subject of E&L studies, and non-wetted components also need an assessment of indirect effects.
  • Component diversity: bioprocess bags (2-D/3-D), tubing (silicone/fluoropolymer/thermoplastic), aseptic connectors (gendered/genderless), filters (sterilizing/depth/tangential flow), sensors (single-use/multi-use) and other components each have their own design features and limitations; selection must consider process compatibility and the completeness of E&L data.
  • Fundamental difference between E and L: Extractables use "exaggerated conditions" for forced extraction and represent the upper bound of chemical risk; Leachables use actual process conditions and represent real patient exposure. The former comes first, and the latter is added as needed.
  • Risk tiering principle: the closer the process position is to the filling step, the stricter the E&L requirements, because there are fewer risk mitigation mechanisms such as dilution and filtration. The filling assembly is in the highest risk class and needs the most complete E&L assessment.
  • The analytical trio: GC-MS (volatile organics), LC-MS (non-volatile polar organics) and ICP-MS (elemental impurities) must be used together to cover the full chemical fingerprint. The AET is the key threshold for judging whether further toxicological attention is needed.
  • The CDMO's dual responsibility: as the end user, the CDMO must set up a clear process (RACI) for reviewing supplier E&L data, confirm that the supplier's extraction conditions are relevant to the worst case of its own process, and bear final responsibility for the toxicological assessment conclusion.
1b

Section 1b: 2.3–2.4: Equipment Design & Quality

p29-p50

ISPE Good Practice Guide: Single-Use Technology | p29 - p50

Learning Objectives for This Chapter

  • Understand the design considerations for SUT process equipment, including sensors, valves, pumps and bioreactors, and the selection logic and limitations of each type
  • Understand the environmental classification of single-use systems (open, closed and functionally closed processes), and how it affects cleanroom grade requirements
  • Understand the three levels of SUT quality requirements (single-use components, assemblies and systems), and the key focus and differences at each level
  • Understand the classification of wetted / non-wetted components and the corresponding differences in quality requirements (bioburden, endotoxin, particulates, integrity testing, etc.)
  • Become familiar with applying the supplier assessment matrix: a multi-level management structure that runs from component suppliers to assembly suppliers to system suppliers
  • Understand the quality management system approach: QbD (ICH Q8), QRM (ICH Q9), CoC / CoQ documentation requirements, and incoming inspection practice for end users
2.3 Process Equipment Design - Overview

Original Text

This section focuses on the design aspects of process equipment design and the operational characteristics of single-use components for implementation of SUT. The topics that are addressed in this section include: sensors, single-use valves, pumps, single-use assemblies, bioreactors, environmental requirements, and considerations for control systems, utility and process support, maintenance and calibration, safety, and cleaning.

Choosing the appropriate components can be one of the most challenging aspects of implementing SUT, especially for transitions from traditional clean-and-reuse systems. Creating detailed user requirements at the beginning of the project is essential in determining which single-use components are suitable for the application. In selecting equipment, primary consideration should be given to suitability with the process. Considerations should also be given to equipment flexibility, customization options, future scale-up requirements, and supplier support.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Challenge of Process Equipment Design

In moving from conventional stainless steel equipment to SUT, the hardest part is not the technology but choosing suitable components. Conventional systems have had standard specifications for decades, but the SUT market is still evolving quickly and there is no "one size fits all" standard.

The logic of this section is: start with the User Requirements, then evaluate which SU components "fit" the process. Process Suitability comes first, ahead of brand or cost.

Analogy

It is like fitting out a new kitchen. You do not simply go to the supplier and buy the most expensive equipment; you first work out how many people you cook for each day, which cooking methods you use and how much space you have. SUT selection works the same way: write the URS (User Requirements Specification) first, then look for suppliers who can meet it.

CDMO Application Scenario

In a CDMO, the same facility may serve the processes of different clients. When selecting SUT components, pay special attention to flexibility: can the component be used with different process fluids? Can it support different batch scales? This directly affects how quickly the equipment investment pays back.

2.3.1 Sensors

Original Text

The determination of the level of accuracy needed (as compared to what the traditional system can measure) is important, as sensors may vary in accuracy and cost. For all single-use sensors, it is highly advised to test the accuracy and precision of the device in-house before implementation in the manufacturing facility.

Considerations for the selection of single-use sensors include:

  • Fundamental technology behind the sensor (e.g., a rotary flow meter measuring rotations by reflection of infrared light from rotor blades will not be reliable when used with colored solutions such as cell culture media)
  • Materials of construction (sensors exposed to process solutions and solvents should be assessed for chemical compatibility)
  • Compatibility with the desired sterilization method (e.g., chemical, radiation, thermal) to ensure that calibration is not affected
  • Calibration strategy (availability of pre-calibrated probes, if required)
  • Robustness of the measurement and drift level under actual conditions of use
  • Standardized connections (selection of components with specialized connections may result in being locked in with one supplier)
  • Allowance for the end-user to perform pre-use steps without breaking sterility

2.3.1.1 Level Sensors

Options for level measurements in SUSs include:

  • For hanging bags (with understanding of solution density): load cell strain gauge
  • For mixers and larger tanks: guided-wave radar, floor scale, and load cells
  • For bubble traps: gas/liquid detector

2.3.1.2 Flow Sensors

Options for flow measurements vary greatly in fixed cost, variable cost, and accuracy:

  • Rotary meters: typically less accurate (± 5%) and more affordable
  • Coriolis meters: more accurate (± 1%) and more expensive
  • Ultrasonic flow meters: easily moved but tend to be more expensive
  • Magnetic flow meters: good accuracy for mid-range expense but not usable with deionized liquids

2.3.1.3 pH Sensors

Options for pH measurements include:

  • Traditional pH sensors: Cleaned while connected to an aseptic connector half, then autoclaved. Accurate across the range used in biological operations.
  • Single-use pH sensors: For physiological range (pH 5.5–8.5), can be inserted into the flow path and gamma irradiated. Provides similar accuracy to traditional probes in this range.
  • Sensor spots: Chemical optical spots placed inside a clear bioprocess container before sterilization, read by a fiber optical cable. Limited pH range; certain cell culture media components can affect accuracy. The end-user should verify accuracy on their specific media, feeds, and buffers before implementation.

2.3.1.4 Dissolved Oxygen

The most straightforward way to bridge DO sensing from stainless steel to SUSs is to use traditional DO sensors (cleaned, then autoclaved). Single-use sensors using plastic sheath and optical sensing technology are available, with narrower range and higher detectability limit but similar accuracy in typical cell culture ranges.

2.3.1.5 Pressure

Several types of single-use pressure sensors are available. Main differences are accuracy, connection type (in-line vs. T), inner diameter, maximum pressure rating, and cost. Multiple-use gauges added onto single-use instrument Ts in a tubing set are also common. Calibration concerns may drive the need for a pre-use calibration depending on the criticality of the measurement.

2.3.1.6 Temperature / Conductivity

Most available sensors have accuracy of approximately 3%. The major differences between suppliers are operating conductivity ranges and available hose barb connection sizes. An alternative is a single-use conductivity probe pre-connected to a bioprocess bag, pre-calibrated, irradiated, providing 2% accuracy (100 mS/cm) via an integrated four-electrode system and temperature sensor.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Golden Rules for Sensor Selection

Single-use sensors are not simply conventional sensors swapped for disposable ones. The underlying principle of the sensing technology determines whether it is reliable in a given application.

The trap most easily overlooked is supplier lock-in. Choosing sensors with proprietary connectors ties your future purchasing options to a single supplier. ISPE explicitly recommends giving preference to standardized connector designs.

Analogy

An infrared rotary flow meter will read inaccurately with a colored culture medium, much like trying to measure an object behind dark glass with visible light, where the color itself interferes with the reading. The lesson is that the physical principle of a sensor determines its application limits. Do not rely on the datasheet alone; understand the measurement mechanism behind it.

Framework for Comparing Flow Meter Accuracy and Cost

Accuracy: Coriolis (±1%) > Ultrasonic > Magnetic > Rotary (±5%)
Cost: Coriolis > Ultrasonic > Magnetic > Rotary
Portability: Ultrasonic is best (clamp-on type, no need to cut into the line)
Limitation: Magnetic cannot be used with deionized water (no conductivity)

Key Point: Practical Pitfalls of pH Sensors

Sensor Spots (optical sensor patches) are convenient to use, but certain cell culture medium components can affect reading accuracy. ISPE specifically cautions that before formal adoption, end users must verify performance with their own media, feed solutions and buffers, and cannot rely on supplier specifications alone.

In a GMP production environment, the calibration strategy for sensors must be clearly documented in the validation plan, including pre-use calibration, post-use verification and the acceptable drift range.

CDMO Application Scenario: Bioprocess Sensing

In a CDMO aseptic filling process, pH and DO are key parameters of the upstream bioreactor. Pairing a single-use pH probe (gamma-irradiable) with a single-use DO probe allows pre-use installation without breaching the sterile barrier, which is critical for shortening changeover time between batches.

Practice Questions

  1. What type of pH measurement solution does your facility currently use? If you were to switch to a single-use probe, what validation work would be needed?
  2. When the accuracy of Sensor Spots does not meet process requirements, what aspects should your risk assessment cover?
2.3.2 Single-Use Valves

Original Text

Opening and closing of flow paths on SUSs is typically performed using manual or automatic pinch valves. The two main types of automated pinch valves are:

  • Pneumatic pinch valves: Typically used for larger inner diameter (ID) tubing and can hold back greater pressures
  • Solenoid valves: Smaller and do not require the system to be supplied with instrument air (usually 90–100 psi)

Pressure control valves are simply pinch valves controlled by a stepper motor, such that they have thousands of positions rather than just open and closed. Due to the stepper motor function, solenoid pinch valves have greater versatility than pneumatic valves.

Single-use diaphragm-style valves are also available.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Design Logic of Flow Path Control

SUT valve design centers on one core principle: the tubing is the valve. The flow path is opened and closed by pinching flexible tubing, with no metal parts in contact with the process fluid.

  • Pneumatic pinch valve: suited to large tubing diameters and high differential pressure; requires the facility to supply compressed air (90–100 psi)
  • Solenoid valve: compact and needs no compressed air, but has lower pressure capability
  • Stepper motor pressure control valve: position control in thousands of steps allows fine flow regulation and is more flexible than a pneumatic valve

Key Point

SUT systems generally have no true pressure rating (unlike stainless steel piping), which means the pressure tolerance of the tubing and valves must be carefully evaluated at the design stage. If pressure exceeds the tubing limit, bags may rupture or tubing may burst, which is an important safety consideration in SUT implementation.

CDMO Application Scenario

In pre-aseptic fill-finish flow paths (such as compounding and filtration), solenoid pinch valves need no compressed air lines, so the whole SU compounding system can be more easily "moved as a module" and, with wheeled carts, flexibly set up in different production suites.

2.3.3 Pumps

Original Text

2.3.3.1 Pump Types

Positive displacement pumps are typically used in situations where accurate flow control is required while handling fluids sensitive to shear. The main pump types commonly used in single-use applications are peristaltic pumps, diaphragm pumps, and centrifugal pumps.

Peristaltic pumps operate with flexible tubing. The wear of the tubing will cause a slight drift in accuracy; if better flow control over time is needed, a flowmeter can be installed to control pump speed. The peristaltic pump is more flexible and requires less labor.

Diaphragm pumps can provide higher operating pressures and higher flow rates than a typical peristaltic pump. They can also operate for longer continuous periods.

In certain perfusion systems, single-use centrifugal pumps may be used. These will not build up pressure to the point of mechanical failure of the SUS, but will vary flow with pressure changes (e.g., filter loading).

2.3.3.2 Typical Materials of Construction

There is a large variety of tubing materials for peristaltic pumps; selection should be made with the fluid product as a primary consideration. For diaphragm pumps, construction materials are similar to soft goods in traditional stainless steel systems (e.g., housing PP, O-rings EPDM, diaphragms TPE).

2.3.3.3 Selection of Tubing/Diaphragms

Due to operating principles of positive displacement pumps, there are pulsations in pressure and flow that can cause stress on shear sensitive fluids.

For peristaltic pumps, tubing selection is a delicate balance:

  • Larger diameter tubing at a given flow rate operates at lower RPM — yields less frequent pulsations and lower shear; however, lower velocity can cause air to be trapped
  • Selecting pumps with multiple channels also mitigates pulsation

Additional considerations for tubing/diaphragm selection:

  • Dimensional consistency: Pump tubing must maintain its shape under repeated squeezing to ensure consistent flow rate vs. RPM correlation
  • E&L: Leachables testing should be done under operating conditions since tubing is squeezed, stretched, and stressed
  • Spallation: Fragmentation of material during use must be confirmed under process conditions. Recycling fluid to the pump concentrates any spalled material, making it more detectable
  • Compatibility with sterilization conditions: Autoclaving or gamma irradiation can stiffen material and modify the flow rate–pump speed correlation. Characterization after sterilization is strongly recommended

2.3.3.4 Overall Selection Criteria for Pumps

Recommendations for single-use pump package selection include:

  • Material compatibility with fluid
  • Accuracy of flow rate: A balance must be struck for each process
  • Minimum pulsation and stable flow rate: Pulsations challenge stability and strain components
  • Ease of replacement: After every run, quick and error-free reassembly is key
  • Synergy with other SUS components: E.g., different size tubing provides flexibility with pinch valves at higher flow rates

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Logic of Pump Type Selection

Biotechnology fluids (especially those containing living cells or proteins) are highly sensitive to shear stress. Positive displacement pumps move the fluid by volumetric displacement and cause far less shear damage to the fluid than centrifugal pumps.

  • Peristaltic: the most commonly used and most flexible; the tubing is the only part in contact with the fluid, and replacement is simple
  • Diaphragm: high flow rate and long continuous operation, suited to large-volume transfers
  • Centrifugal: suited to perfusion systems and does not build up pressure to the point of bag rupture

Analogy: Spallation (Material Fragmentation)

It is like a rubber band that, after long use, starts to shed rubber crumbs from its surface. Peristaltic pump tubing is repeatedly squeezed by the rollers over a long period, and once the material fatigues it may release tiny fragments into the process fluid. In biopharmaceutical aseptic manufacturing this is a serious contamination risk, and spallation characteristics must be evaluated under process conditions (not with pure water).

Key Point: Effect of Gamma Irradiation on Pump Tubing

Gamma irradiation sterilization causes many plastics (especially elastomers such as PVC and EPDM) to stiffen. Stiffened tubing delivers a different flow rate at the same RPM, so a flow-versus-speed calibration curve established before shipment may no longer be valid. ISPE recommends re-evaluating flow characteristics after irradiation and including this consideration in process qualification.

Framework for Pump Pulsation Control

Peristaltic pump pulsation control strategy:
  ↑ Tubing diameter → ↓ RPM → ↓ Pulse frequency → ↓ Shear stress
              but → ↓ Flow velocity → ↑ Risk of air entrapment
  Multi-channel pump head → Pulses cancel each other → Smoother flow

Diaphragm pump pulsation control strategy:
  Multiple small diaphragms act alternately → Pulse phases dispersed → More stable flow

CDMO Application Scenario: Batch Changeover

The biggest advantage of SUT peristaltic pumps is that changing the tubing requires no CIP/SIP: swapping in new tubing completes the batch changeover. However, after each tubing change the pump speed-flow relationship must be re-verified (tubing dimensions vary from lot to lot). In a GMP environment, this verification step should be recorded in the BPR.

2.3.4 Bioreactors

Original Text

2.3.4.1 Design

The primary application of single-use bioreactors is mammalian cell culture. Single-use bioreactors have had limited applicability with microbial cell lines due to limitations in vessel pressure, mass transfer, and venting. Microbial cell lines also present a challenge when the culture becomes heat generating, requiring a jacketed vessel with cooling capabilities.

Bioreactors are equipped with vent filters and filter heaters to promote evaporation of condensate. Even with heaters, filters may be blocked with condensate from exhaust gases, potentially resulting in overpressure, process stops, and potential breach of the bioreactor bag.

For inoculation or harvesting, all transfers should be performed by pumping since overpressure is not possible. This is a critical point for cells sensitive to shear stress.

Ease of use is an important consideration. As the bags are sensitive and complex setup/operations are required, end-users should be sufficiently trained. Factors to consider include: setup and removal time, number of personnel required, and the weight of the new and emptied bag.

Bioreactor designs range from mirroring traditional stainless steel bioreactors to novel agitation mechanisms. Options are still relatively limited with regard to impeller design, impeller size, and sparger type.

2.3.4.2 Process Transfer/Scale-Up

Single-use bioreactors should be characterized prior to use; a robust scaling factor should be selected for the scale-up strategy. Parameters may need to be adjusted since configuration options are more limited compared to stainless steel bioreactors. The recommended practice is to use an equivalent characterized small-scale model.

Considerations for applying conventional scale-up approaches include matching:

  • Geometric ratio (volume/surface) to retain equivalence between scales
  • Oxygen Transfer Rate (OTR) coefficient (kLa) — kept constant if possible
  • Power input per unit volume (P/V, in kW/m³)
  • Volumetric gas flow rate per unit volume (VVM)
  • Mixing time — an important consideration for pH adjustment

Other approaches: constant volumetric scale-up to maintain shear stress; constant tip speed; constant VVM correlated to theoretical kLa.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Why SU Bioreactors Are Mainly Used for Mammalian Cells

Mammalian cell culture (for example, CHO cells producing monoclonal antibodies) has low pressure tolerance and is shear-sensitive, requiring low-speed agitation and gentle gas-liquid mass transfer. This is exactly the design "sweet spot" of SU bioreactors.

Microbial fermentation (E. coli, yeast) is the opposite: it needs high oxygen transfer rate (OTR), high agitation speed and large heat removal, all of which call for robust metal vessels that SU bags can hardly meet.

Analogy: Vent Filter Blocked by Condensate

A vent filter blocked by condensate is like a drain pipe clogged with grease: the pressure inside the system has nowhere to go and may eventually rupture the bag. A filter heater works like heating and insulating the drain pipe, keeping water vapor in the gas phase so it does not condense into liquid. This is an easily overlooked but very important detail in SU bioreactor design.

Scale-Up Parameters

kLa (volumetric oxygen transfer coefficient) = the core scale-up criterion
  → Keeping kLa constant ensures the same dissolved oxygen supply capability

P/V (power input per unit volume, kW/m³)
  → Represents agitation intensity and affects mixing homogeneity and shear stress

VVM (gas volume per liquid volume per minute)
  → Controls aeration rate and affects kLa and CO₂ removal

Mixing Time
  → Efficiency of base mixing during pH adjustment,
     to avoid locally high pH that damages cells

Key Point: Scale-Up Limitations of SU Bioreactors

Compared with conventional stainless steel reactors, SU bioreactors offer very limited options for agitator design, impeller size and sparger type. This means scale-up may require accepting compromises in process parameters (for example, not being able to fully maintain the same kLa), rather than transplanting the parameters of a conventional system directly.

CDMO Application Scenario

When a CDMO takes on a new client process that originally used a stainless steel bioreactor, moving to an SU bioreactor requires a small-scale equivalence study. The approach ISPE recommends is to first verify the scale-up rules with a characterized small-scale SU model, and then execute at commercial scale. The results of this study must be documented and incorporated into the process validation plan.

2.3.5–2.3.6 Chromatography & Ultrafiltration

Original Text

2.3.5 Chromatography

Chromatography skids with single-use flow paths generally use peristaltic pumps and thus are not suitable for gradient elution, limiting use to binding/elution or flow-through chromatography operations.

This area of SUT is rapidly changing with advancements in continuous processing, in-line dilution, and in-line conditioning. These technologies often lower flow rates to within SUT limits. However, single-use components may not be compatible with concentrated solutions; end-users should consult with suppliers prior to using single-use assemblies with highly concentrated solutions.

2.3.6 Ultrafiltration

Single-use ultrafiltration membranes/systems are available; however, the vast majority of medium to large scale ultrafiltration systems remain multiple-use. This is mainly due to the cost of the membranes, flow limitations through single-use tubing, and the need to spread cost over multiple batches. As a result, single-use ultrafiltration tends to be restricted to smaller scale and niche applications.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Limitations of Single-Use Chromatography

Chromatography is currently one of the downstream purification unit operations with the lowest degree of SUT adoption. The reasons are:

  • Gradient elution requires precise multi-channel buffer mixing, which the pulsation characteristics of peristaltic pumps struggle to deliver
  • Large-scale operation (column diameter > 80 cm) requires flow rates far beyond the pressure and flow limits of SU tubing
  • Reuse vs. disposal: chromatography resin is very expensive, and the economics of single-use do not add up

Key Point: Compatibility of High-Concentration Solutions

Continuous processing and in-line dilution technologies reduce flow requirements and give more steps the opportunity to adopt SUT. However, ISPE specifically cautions that high-concentration solutions (high-concentration salts, organic solvents) may be incompatible with SU materials, and end users must confirm compatibility with the supplier before adoption.

The Predicament of Single-Use Ultrafiltration

Ultrafiltration membranes are expensive, and throwing them away after each use is like buying a new pasta machine every time you cook spaghetti: it makes no economic sense. Large-scale UF systems are still mainly multi-use. SU ultrafiltration is economically justified only at small scale or in special scenarios (such as preventing cross-contamination with highly potent substances).

2.3.7 Environmental Requirements

Original Text

The ISPE Baseline® Guide: Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities defines three categories of processing:

  • Open process: A process exposed to the environment, requiring environmental conditions to mitigate contamination risk.
  • Closed process: A process system designed and operated such that the product is never exposed to the surrounding environment. Additions to and draws from closed systems need to be performed in a completely closed fashion.
  • Functionally closed process: Process systems that may be opened but are rendered closed by a cleaning, sanitization, and/or sterilization process appropriate to the process requirements (sterile, aseptic, or low bioburden). These systems remain closed during production.

SUSs that use ready-to-use flow paths with aseptic connections are compatible with the reduced environmental requirements of closed or functionally closed operations. A process closure risk assessment should be performed to identify the required classification and segregation needed for different process steps.

Hybrid systems, such as a stainless steel vessel connected to a single-use flow path, are a typical application. A system that has any clean-and-reuse portion should be cleaned, closed, and returned to its sterile state. If the system can be returned to its closed sterile state, it could be reconnected to single-use components and operated in an area with lower area classification as a functionally closed system. The stainless steel vessel could be connected via a steam-through connector, cleaned, and steamed in place (SIP). Once through a SIP cycle, the connection is engaged and the hybrid system is both sterile (or bioburden controlled) and closed.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Process Closure Determines Cleanroom Grade Requirements

This is one of the most important cost-saving mechanisms of SUT: closed processes can be operated in lower-grade cleanrooms.

  • Open process: product is exposed to the environment → requires a high-grade cleanroom (ISO 5 / Grade A)
  • Closed process: no exposure at all → can be lowered to ISO 8 or even CNC (Controlled Not Classified)
  • Functionally closed: an intermediate state → depending on the nature of the process, usually ISO 7 or ISO 8

Key tool: a Process Closure Risk Assessment must be performed separately for each process step; a blanket approach is not acceptable.

Analogy

Picture water moving through a pipe (closed process) versus being passed around in an open container (open process). Water in a pipe does not care how dirty the outside air is, but once it is poured into an open container, a clean working environment is needed. The "closure" of SUT lets you downgrade the working environment for many steps from an "operating theater" to an "ordinary hospital corridor", saving a great deal of facility cost.

Key Point: Management Principles for Hybrid Systems

Hybrid systems combining stainless steel vessels with single-use flow paths are common, but note that the stainless steel portion must be managed as conventional multi-use equipment (CIP/SIP), and only after SIP is complete can it be connected to the SU flow path so that the whole system constitutes a "functionally closed" system. The management rules of the two worlds must not be mixed and must be clearly distinguished in SOPs.

CDMO Application Scenario: Facility Design Savings

Closed processes allow a considerable number of upstream steps (media preparation, buffer preparation) to be performed in lower-grade areas, greatly reducing cleanroom construction and maintenance costs. This is why almost all new biopharmaceutical CDMOs are built around SUT: not because SU consumables are cheap, but because SUT lowers the cost of the whole facility design.

2.3.8–2.3.10 Control Systems, Utilities, Maintenance, Safety & Cleaning

Original Text

2.3.8 Control Systems

Skid manufacturers should follow Good Automated Manufacturing Practice (GAMP®), use modern Open Platform Communications (OPC), and allow open standards to end-users — since closed systems prohibit inclusion into a GMP area.

Additional recommendations:

  • Automation should enable end-users to handle multiple scales
  • The system should display process and system operational parameters with a feedback loop for every control parameter (e.g., agitation should verify motor speed to set point)
  • The control system should include a network interface card and a batch manager
  • Connectivity to the network should be considered since the lighter weight of SUSs allows for more mobility (e.g., a single-use mixer wheeled between locations requires data ports at both locations or a wireless radio)

2.3.9 Utility and Process Support

Electrical: Main electrical load contributions are motors and temperature control. Unlike stainless steel skidded systems, electrical connections on SUSs are typically cord and plug, enabling reconfiguration within a suite.

Process Gases: The lack of a true pressure rating on SUSs may require different gassing/venting control schemes from equivalent stainless steel systems.

Water: For smaller scale operations, bagged water prepared off-site may eliminate purified water lines. For larger scale, water systems typically have fewer use points. Due to temperature sensitivity of SUSs, ambient vs. hot distribution loops with coolers should be evaluated.

Drains: Leveraging the closed system abilities of SUSs could allow systems in a classification that allows an open drain. Decrease in CIP activities may reduce pH neutralization demands.

2.3.10 Maintenance and Calibration

Maintenance requirements are generally reduced for SUSs. Skid manufacturers should: include automated pinch valve cycle counts in automation; allow easy access to single-use components; arrange tubing/connectors/filters for accessibility with sufficient support.

Supplier calibration can be used for single-use components, possibly coupled with on-site post-use calibration. Suppliers should be compliant with ISO/IEC 17025 or equivalent and provide transmitter calibration procedures as part of the turnover package.

2.3.10.1 Safety

Additional safety aspects for SUSs beyond standard E-stops include:

  • Secondary containment to capture leaks during system disassembly
  • Biosafety considerations and containment/inactivation procedures
  • Pressure sensors providing feedback to pump motors to prevent leakage due to bag/plastic rupture
  • Guards surrounding tubing for processes with toxic buffers/drugs
  • Guards/shields around exposed drive shafts
  • Electrical wiring designed per governing codes (e.g., UL listed)

2.3.10.2 Cleaning

While process line cleaning is eliminated for fully SUSs, non-product surfaces should still be wiped/washed per facility procedures for the designated area classification.

Single-use components are packaged in sealed containers. Some assemblies are packaged in multiple bags that should be cleaned/wiped as they move into stricter area classifications. Care should be taken in choosing sanitization methods since certain solvents may adversely impact plastics. The intended use of sanitizing agents (including chlorine dioxide and vaporized hydrogen peroxide) should be communicated between end-user and supplier to assess impact on hardware and plastic selection.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Open Principle for Control Systems

ISPE explicitly requires that the control systems of SUT equipment follow Open Standards: using the OPC communication protocol to allow integration into the plant's DCS/SCADA systems. Closed proprietary systems hinder GMP compliance (they cannot be integrated into the plant's batch record systems).

This is especially important in a CDMO: different clients may require batch data to be integrated into different plant information systems, and a closed control system becomes a compliance and commercial obstacle.

Key Point: Data Management for Mobile Equipment

SUT equipment is lightweight and can be moved around the site, but batch data must be completely recorded (21 CFR Part 11 requirement). Every operating location along the equipment's movement path needs a data interface (wired or wireless), and there must be no data gaps simply because the equipment has been moved. The data network coverage must be planned at the facility design stage.

Key Point: The Shift in Cleaning Validation

Conventional systems: CIP/SIP validation is core compliance work (extensive documentation, sampling and analysis).

SUT: cleaning validation of process tubing almost disappears, but this does not mean there is no cleaning work. Non-product-contact surfaces still need to be cleaned and disinfected. In addition, the unpacking procedure for SU components entering the cleanroom (removing the outer packaging layer by layer) is itself a contamination control measure and must have a written procedure.

Analogy: The Effect of VHP on Plastics

VHP (vaporized hydrogen peroxide) is a commonly used environmental decontamination method in aseptic filling facilities, but it has an oxidative effect on certain plastics (especially silicone and polycarbonate), much like washing clothes with strong bleach: cotton is fine, but colored synthetic fabrics may fade. Before using VHP for environmental decontamination, the material compatibility of all SU components must be confirmed and the supplier notified.

CDMO Application Scenario: Utilities Design Optimization

The biggest change SUT brings to utilities is a substantial reduction in points of use for purified water systems (WFI/PW). In a conventional stainless steel plant every CIP station needs a purified water connection, whereas an SUT plant may retain connections at only a few fixed locations and use prefilled bagged aqueous solutions elsewhere. This makes facility design simpler and also lowers the maintenance cost of the purified water system, making it an important ROI calculation item in planning a new CDMO facility.

2.4 Quality Requirements for Single-Use Products

Original Text

This section addresses fundamental criteria for single-use quality requirements associated with SUT implementation. This section should be read in conjunction with Section 2.6 (User Requirement Specification Development) and Section 3.2 (Regulatory Compliance).

In addition to the information provided in this section, the science and risk-based approach described in ASTM E2500, ASTM E3051-16, and ICH Q8, Q9, and Q10 should also be followed. A risk assessment should be completed at each key decision point.

2.4.1 Defining Quality Requirements

As identified in Section 1.4, SUT incorporates single-use components, assemblies, and systems. The requirements for each category are addressed in the following sections.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Regulatory Framework for Quality Requirements

SUT quality requirements are built on existing regulatory and scientific frameworks:

  • ASTM E2500: science- and risk-based approach to equipment verification
  • ICH Q8: pharmaceutical development (Quality by Design, QbD)
  • ICH Q9: quality risk management (QRM)
  • ICH Q10: pharmaceutical quality system (PQS)

ISPE's logic is that SUT does not need new regulations; it needs the existing frameworks applied correctly to the particular context of single use.

Key Point: Risk Assessment at Critical Decision Points

"A risk assessment should be completed at each critical decision point" means, in practice, that an SUT implementation plan needs clear Decision Gates, with each gate corresponding to a documented risk assessment record. This is an important document that auditors will look at during a PAI (Pre-Approval Inspection).

2.4.1.1 Quality Requirements for Single-Use Components

Original Text

Considerations for single-use quality requirements for components include:

  • Extractables profile of wetted surface components
  • Biological and chemical compatibility of wetted surface components with the product or process fluid
  • Integrity (structural and mechanical integrity, closure integrity for containers/bags)
  • Resistance and compatibility to temperature, pH, and pressure conditions during use and treatment (e.g., gamma irradiation or other sterilization techniques applied)
  • Qualification of components
  • Limits for bioburden and endotoxin
  • Limits for particulate matter
  • Manufacturing environment for components
  • incoming inspection of components on receipt
  • Inventory control of components

A primary approach to component assessment is based on two categories: wetted and non-wetted components.

Wetted component groupings:

  • Film, tubing, and connectors: basic to any assembly
  • Bottles, filters, instruments/sensors, mixers: common components
  • Liners for valves, pumps and centrifuges: more specialized

While non-wetted components typically require a less extensive list, considerations should still be made for contact between wetted and non-wetted components and potential impact on the environment of the wetted components. Both types are exposed to treatment conditions (e.g., gamma irradiation, autoclaving, shipping). Examples:

  • Clamps, fasteners, and affixed labels: have contact with the wetted components
  • Bubble wrap, protective foam, pack-out bags, boxes, packaging tape, and labels: can impact the environment of the assembly

Table 2.5: Suggested Quality Requirements Based on Classification of Components

Note: A = Required; B = Useful; — = Not needed

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Classified Management of Wetted vs. Non-Wetted Components

Wetted components: any part in direct contact with the process fluid or product. This is the focus of quality control: extractables, biocompatibility, bioburden, endotoxin and particulates are all required.

Non-wetted components: parts that do not directly contact the fluid but may have an indirect effect (for example, outer packaging affecting radiation dose distribution, or clamps touching the outer wall of the bag and affecting bag integrity). The requirements are fewer, but they are not ignored altogether.

This classified approach makes the quality system more efficient by concentrating resources on the most critical risk points.

Analogy

It is like cleaning standards in a hotel room: items that directly touch the guest (sheets, bath towels, tableware) need the highest cleaning standard (analogous to wetted components), while items that do not directly touch the guest (floor, walls, furniture) use a lower but still adequate standard (analogous to non-wetted components). You would not demand that the floor be cleaned to tableware disinfection standards, but you cannot ignore it either.

Key Point: Manufacturing Environment Requirements for Packaging Materials

A point in Table 2.5 that is easily overlooked: for non-wetted packaging materials such as foam and outer cartons, the manufacturing environment is still rated A (Required). The reason is that packaging materials manufactured in a poor environment may contaminate the outer surface of the component and in turn contaminate the clean environment during installation. This is an item that must be checked in SU supplier audits.

CDMO Application Scenario: Incoming Quality Management

When purchasing SU components, a common question is: "The supplier's CoC says bioburden < 1 CFU/100 cm². Is that sufficient?" The answer depends on the nature of your process (bioburden control vs. sterile) and whether sterilization follows (after gamma irradiation the bioburden requirement drops to zero). The sampling plan and acceptance criteria for incoming inspection must be based on a risk assessment of the whole process, not copied from the supplier's specification.

Practice Questions

  1. In your process, which SU components are "wetted"? Does your incoming inspection procedure cover all the "A (Required)" items in Table 2.5?
  2. If a supplier cannot provide a certain quality requirement (such as endotoxin test results), what is your alternative control strategy?
2.4.1.2–2.4.1.3 Quality Requirements for Assemblies & Systems

Original Text

2.4.1.2 Quality Requirements for Single-Use Assemblies

In addition to requirements for single-use components, considerations for single-use assembly quality requirements include:

  • Qualification and control of clean room environment (particle control)
  • Handling of components from inventory storage to clean room environment
  • Inspection (in-process and post-manufacturing)
  • Manufacturing methods and controls
  • Integrity testing of manufactured assembly (structural and mechanical integrity)
  • Training of assemblers and other manufacturing personnel
  • Sterile barrier bagging and packaging integrity
  • Sterilization
  • Manufacturing certificates (certificate of compliance / certificate of quality)
  • shelf life compliance with shipping requirements (e.g., ASTM D4169-16 or ISTA standards)

2.4.1.3 Quality Requirements for Single-Use Systems

SUSs consist of single-use components/assemblies and multiple-use parts. Examples of multiple-use parts include: bag carriers, system frames, tubing racks, bag lifts; pumps, motors, load cells, instruments, control valves; electrical/wiring/control enclosures; software.

Mechanical parts — quality requirements include:

  • Material of construction and compatibility with the operating environment (corrosion resistance)
  • Surface finish
  • Compliance with mechanical strength (stiffness, bending, pressure), including after sterilization
  • particulate presence and generation potential

Electrical/controls parts — quality requirements include:

  • Compliance with NEC or local electrical codes
  • Ergonomics of control Human Machine Interface (HMI)
  • Compliance with area classification (class, division, and zone)
  • Software compliance with 21 CFR Part 11
  • particulate generation potential of moving parts (motors, fans, pumps)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Three-Level Quality Requirement Structure

ISPE divides SUT quality requirements into three levels, each with its own additional requirements:

  • Component level: basic quality (material compatibility, biological indicators, particulates)
  • Assembly level: manufacturing control (cleanroom environment, assembly integrity, sterile barrier, sterilization qualification, transportation)
  • System level: integration verification (mechanical strength, software compliance (21 CFR Part 11), particulate generation potential)

Each level inherits the requirements of the level below and adds further considerations.

Key Point: The Importance of Packaging Integrity

The integrity of the sterile barrier is one of the most critical indicators of SU assembly quality. Assemblies are usually shipped in vacuum-sealed bags, so checking on receipt that the vacuum is intact is the end user's first quality gate. An assembly with a broken vacuum or damaged packaging, however good its other indicators, should be quarantined immediately and the supplier notified.

Key Point: Particulate Generation from Moving Parts

Motors, fans and pumps in SUT systems are all particulate generation sources. When used in high-grade cleanrooms (ISO 5 / Grade A), the particulate emission characteristics of this equipment must be confirmed, suppliers should provide test data, and validation should include both at-rest and in-operation particulate monitoring.

Analogy: The Importance of Transportation Qualification

ASTM D4169 transportation testing is like simulating the "worst-case journey" of an SU assembly from the factory to your hands: bumpy trucks, vibration in airport cargo holds, stacking pressure in warehouses. Only if the assembly still has good integrity after passing this test can the packaging design be considered sufficiently reliable.

CDMO Application Scenario: 21 CFR Part 11 Compliance

The software of SUT equipment (batch management, process history records) must comply with 21 CFR Part 11 requirements for electronic records and electronic signatures. When a CDMO takes on client projects under US FDA oversight, the supplier assessment stage should confirm whether the supplier's software has undergone Part 11 review, and obtain the related validation documents.

2.4.1.4 Supplier Assessment Matrix

Original Text

The component supplier matrix is useful in identifying supplier candidates that can provide components meeting specified requirements. The matrix can be used to:

  • Assess the availability of components: Components available from multiple sources are more established and have reduced supply risks
  • Identify specific component quality requirements readily available from suppliers and plan work needed to meet requirements that cannot currently be met

Three levels of supplier assessment matrices are defined:

  • Component Supplier Matrix (Table 2.6): Evaluates component suppliers across items including bags, fittings, tubing, connectors, filters, valves, ports, filling needles, pump liners, centrifuge liners, sensors, bottles, gaskets/O-rings, chromatography columns, clamps
  • Assembly Supplier Matrix (Table 2.7): Adds assembly-specific criteria — sterile barrier bagging, sterilization, integrity testing of manufactured assembly, compliance with shipping requirements, manufacturing methods and controls
  • System Supplier Matrix (Table 2.8): Adds system-specific criteria — Factory Acceptance Test, start-up support, Site Acceptance Test support; system components include equipment frame, bag carrier, instruments, pumps, centrifuges, bag lifts, electrical/control enclosures, software

Supplier Operations criteria common to all matrices: manufacturing and quality certificates, qualification and control of manufacturing environment, handling, inspection, training, past experience and organization history, local office and technical support availability.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Management Logic of the Three-Level Supplier Matrix

The supplier assessment matrix is a tiered, systematic assessment tool:

  • Component matrix: assesses "parts suppliers", who provide raw materials such as film, tubing and connectors
  • Assembly matrix: assesses "system integrators", who assemble components into customized flow path assemblies
  • System matrix: assesses "equipment suppliers", who provide complete SU bioprocess systems (hardware, software and services)

The same supplier may span several levels (such as Sartorius or Cytiva), and the matrix lets you assess its capabilities from different angles.

Analogy

It is like buying a custom sports car: the component matrix = assessing the suppliers of tires and engine parts; the assembly matrix = assessing the car maker (who assembles the parts into a working car); the system matrix = assessing the delivery service (factory acceptance, handover training, after-sales support). The assessment focus differs at each level, and one standard cannot be applied across the board.

Key Point: Supplier Diversification vs. Standardization

The matrix specifically notes that components available from multiple sources carry lower supply risk. This is a direct response to the risk of SUT supplier lock-in. Give preference to components that already have several competing suppliers (standard tubing, standard connectors); for unavoidable proprietary designs (a particular supplier's bioreactor bag), stronger supplier relationship management and a backup plan are needed.

Practice Questions

  1. Which of the SU components you currently use carry a "sole supplier" risk? What is your backup supplier plan?
  2. If a critical supplier cannot meet your quality requirements (such as a particulate limit), what is your response process? How does the matrix help you systematically identify alternatives?
2.4.2 Checking Single-Use Product Quality

Original Text

The performance of a single-use product is often dependent on many factors during the manufacturing process and subsequent handling throughout the product's life. It is important that the complete histories of single-use components and assemblies are tracked and documented.

Quality Aspects to be Checked by Suppliers:

At receipt (of components): Package conditions; visual check of components (by trained inspectors); compliance with specifications; presence of certificates; inspection of components (quantity and labeling)

During manufacture of assembly: Structural and mechanical integrity of components; compliance with manufacturing drawings; integrity of assembly; visual inspection for proper assembly

At release to ship: Compliance with manufacturing requirements; clearance with endotoxin / particulate criteria; compliance with packaging integrity requirements; confirmed irradiation level

Records should include acceptance/rejection criteria as well as item number, part number, revision letter, lot size, and inspector's name.

Quality Aspects to be Checked by End-Users:

At receipt (of assembly): Package conditions; outer and inner package label inspection; visual check for integrity of sterile barrier (presence of vacuum); assembly layout within package; presence of certificate of compliance / certificate of quality; visual check of assembly; sterility/irradiation marker check

At use: Compliance with assembly specifications; compliance with assembly drawings (end connection types, filter size, etc.); confirming inlet/outlet connection points

Personnel handling assemblies should be trained and familiar with the specific assembly and critical steps for moving it from package to manufacturing operations.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Division of Quality Responsibility Along the Supply Chain

SUT quality assurance is a shared responsibility of every link in the supply chain, not something that relies only on the end user's incoming inspection. ISPE explicitly defines three checking points:

  • Component supplier: incoming → in-process → pre-shipment (three checkpoints)
  • Assembly supplier: checks on the components + assembly process control
  • End user (you): on receipt → before use (two checkpoints)

This tiered checking structure means that the earlier a problem is found in the supply chain, the smaller the loss.

Key Point: The Difference Between CoC and CoQ

On receipt, do not just check whether a certificate exists; check what the certificate says:

  • Certificate of Compliance (CoC): the supplier's declaration that the product meets a specific specification; verify that the specification version matches the purchase order
  • Certificate of Quality (CoQ): accompanied by actual test data (such as bioburden CFU values and endotoxin EU/mL values); check whether the values are within specification, and do not rely solely on the supplier's own claim of "conforming"

In a GMP environment, copies of the CoC/CoQ should be kept in the batch record.

Analogy: Vacuum Check = The First Gate of the Sterile Barrier

Checking that the vacuum is intact when you receive an SU assembly is like pressing on vacuum-packed food before opening it to check that it still feels firm. You would not eat food whose pack has leaked air; an SU assembly with a broken vacuum should likewise not be used in aseptic manufacturing, because the sterile barrier has been breached and the assembly may have been contaminated by the environment.

Practice Questions

  1. Does your incoming inspection SOP for SU assemblies cover all the "At Use" verification items (assembly drawings, connection types, inlet/outlet confirmation)? Who is responsible for carrying them out?
  2. If you find that the vacuum packaging of a batch of SU assemblies is damaged, what is your non-conformance handling process? Which departments need to be notified?
2.4.3 Methods Applied to Meet Single-Use Product Quality Requirements

Original Text

2.4.3.1 Quality Management System

Quality by Design (QbD): A QbD approach, as described in ICH Q8, should be applied as part of SUT implementation.

Communication Flow: Successful SUT implementation depends on streamlined communication flow between the supplier and end-user, ensuring the supplier follows the User Requirement Specification (URS) written by the end-user. Assigning a key project contact from both parties is recommended.

Typical interactive activities:

  • The end-user: Writes the URS; reviews and approves drawings
  • The supplier: Confirms understanding of the URS; provides material data, advice on typical designs, component lists, and operation manuals; submits drawings; meets agreed manufacturing/delivery schedules

Quality Risk Management (QRM): QRM methods, as described in ICH Q9, should be applied for defining quality requirements for single-use products. Understanding and assessing product and process risks — with communication to upstream suppliers and downstream end-users — are key factors.

Acceptance/Rejection Inspections: Documented methods for acceptance/rejection of materials should be used throughout the supply chain. Any non-conforming material should be identified and placed in quarantine or returned to the supplier.

2.4.3.2 Development of Design Specifications

Functional and technical design specifications should be defined by end-users, with support from suppliers. Critical aspects (in terms of ensuring product quality and patient safety, per ASTM E2500) should be considered.

2.4.3.3 Qualification of Single-Use Components and Assemblies

Qualification practices should include defined and acceptable methods to qualify single-use components for their use in the manufacture of single-use assemblies and systems. Qualification criteria are often geared towards producing material for specific application requirements. For details of testing performed to meet these criteria, refer to Chapter 8 (Appendix 4) of the ISPE SUT Guide.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The URS Is the Starting Point for Everything

In SUT implementation the User Requirement Specification (URS) is like the blueprint of a building: all subsequent design, procurement and qualification activities are based on it. An unclear URS leads to:

  • The supplier misunderstanding the requirements → delivery of a product that does not meet them
  • No clear acceptance criteria at validation → inability to explain the position at audit
  • Frequent design changes → schedule delays and higher costs

ISPE stresses that the communication flow must be two-way: the end user writes the URS, the supplier confirms understanding and provides design feedback, and the end user reviews and approves the drawings.

Key Concept: Applying QbD in SUT

Quality by Design (QbD) is not used only in drug development; ISPE extends it to SUT selection and implementation. The core idea is: "build" the quality requirements into the system at the design stage, rather than "test" quality in afterwards.

The practical meaning for SUT: when writing the URS, clearly define the Critical Quality Attributes (CQAs) and Critical Process Parameters (CPPs), and ensure that these requirements are met in the selection of SU components.

Analogy: Practical Application of QRM

A typical application of the ICH Q9 risk management tools (FMEA, HACCP, risk matrix) in SUT is to assess the risk of "what if this SU bag fails": how likely is it? How severe is the impact on product quality? Can the current controls (pressure sensors, secondary containment) reduce the risk to an acceptable level? This way of thinking helps you decide where additional controls are needed.

Key Point: Validation Test Methods Refer to Chapter 8

This section provides only a framework for the validation approach. The specific test methods (detailed procedures for integrity testing, extractables testing and bioburden testing) are set out in Chapter 8 (Appendix 4) of the ISPE SUT guide. When establishing a validation plan for SU components, refer to that appendix as well, to ensure that the test methods conform to the best practices recommended by ISPE.

CDMO Application Scenario: Preparing for Client Audits

When a client or FDA auditor audits your SUT system, the questions most often asked are: "Where is your URS?" "How do you confirm that the supplier's quality system meets GMP?" "How were your incoming inspection standards established?" If all three questions have clear written answers that are consistent with the approach described in this section, the audit will go smoothly. SUT quality management is not about "producing a pile of extra documents"; it is about applying the existing GMP framework correctly to the situations specific to SU.

Practice Questions

  1. Does your SUT system currently have a complete URS document? Does the URS clearly define the Critical Quality Attributes and acceptance criteria?
  2. In your most recent SU supplier selection, which quality risk management tools did you use? How did the results influence your final choice?
  3. Has your SU component validation plan been updated to reference the test method requirements of ISPE SUT Chapter 8 (Appendix 4)?

Section Summary

  • Sensor selection principles: understand the physical mechanism of the measurement technology and give preference to standardized connectors (to avoid supplier lock-in). All SU sensors must undergo in-house accuracy and precision verification before formal use. For pH 5.5–8.5, a single-use probe or Sensor spots can be used, but must be verified with the actual culture medium.
  • Pump selection logic: for biotechnology fluids, give preference to positive displacement pumps (peristaltic is the most flexible, diaphragm gives high flow). Gamma irradiation stiffens tubing and affects the flow-speed relationship, so it must be re-evaluated after irradiation. Spallation (material fragmentation) must be evaluated under process conditions, not with pure water alone.
  • Bioreactor limitations: SU bioreactors are mainly suited to mammalian cell culture; microbial fermentation is limited by high OTR and high heat removal demands. Scale-up should use kLa, P/V, VVM and mixing time as key parameters. Vent filter condensate management is an easily overlooked design detail.
  • Process closure determines environmental grade: closed processes can be operated at a lower cleanroom grade, which is the core cost advantage of SUT. A closure risk assessment must be performed for each process step. Hybrid systems (stainless steel + SU flow path) need the two sets of management rules to be clearly distinguished.
  • Wetted vs. non-wetted quality requirements: wetted components need the full set of quality requirements (extractables, biocompatibility, bioburden, endotoxin, particulates); non-wetted components have fewer requirements, but the manufacturing environment is still A (Required).
  • Three-level quality requirement structure: component level (basic material quality) → assembly level (manufacturing control + sterile barrier + sterilization + transportation qualification) → system level (mechanical strength + software 21 CFR Part 11 + particulate generation control). Each level inherits and adds to the requirements of the one below.
  • Division of quality responsibility along the supply chain: component suppliers (incoming → in-process → pre-shipment), assembly suppliers (manufacturing control + CoC/CoQ), end users (checks on receipt: vacuum integrity, labels, certificates; checks before use: drawing conformance, connection point verification). None can be omitted.
  • Quality management system approach: the URS is the starting point for everything (end-to-end communication); QbD (ICH Q8) builds quality into the design; QRM (ICH Q9) identifies and controls risk; accept/reject criteria for incoming inspection must be documented, and non-conforming material must be quarantined.
1c

Section 1c: 2.5–2.8: Supplier, URS & Facility

p51-p78

ISPE Good Practice Guide: Single-Use Technology | p51 - p78

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the core elements of a Quality Agreement and how an audit program keeps quality consistent across the whole supply chain
  • Master the planning principles of a Supplier Audit: how audit frequency is set based on risk and the supplier relationship, and the key items on an audit checklist
  • Understand the structure and drafting of a User Requirements Specification (URS), including quality requirements, process requirements and how to communicate with suppliers
  • Become familiar with the three SUT facility design concepts (Dance Floor, Factory-in-a-Box, Box-within-a-Box), and when each applies and what challenges it brings
  • Understand the two main routes for SUT waste management (Autoclave Decontamination and Incineration) and when each is needed
  • Build the complete chain of thinking for SUT implementation: supplier selection → URS development → facility design → waste handling
2.5 Supplier Quality and Audits

Original Text

This section addresses supplier quality and audit requirements. This section is intended to be used in conjunction with Section 2.4 for selecting single-use suppliers and to establish consistent quality in SUT implementation.

The roles of the supplier and end-user are strongly linked in the SUT space. While this section is primarily targeted for use by the single-use product end-user, intermediate suppliers may also find parts of this section useful in defining the quality of components and the auditing of their respective suppliers. The process of establishing consistent quality along the entire supply chain allows for more robust final quality to the end-user and, ultimately, to patients.

This section is intended to be compatible with PDA Technical Report 66 and ASTM E3051-16. These publications emphasize the concept of technical diligence as a way of fostering transparency and open communication within the supplier and end-user relationship.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: What Makes SUT Supplier Relationships Special

The SUT supply chain is unlike conventional chemical raw material purchasing. A supplier is not just "selling material"; the quality of their manufacturing environment directly affects the quality of your drug product.

There is an important chain of logic here:

  • The supplier's cleanroom environment → affects particulate contamination of single-use components
  • The supplier's sterilization validation → determines the sterility assurance level of your product
  • The supplier's raw material control → affects your E&L (extractables/leachables) results

Quality management of SUT suppliers therefore requires deeper technical involvement than for conventional packaging suppliers. This is what technical diligence means.

Analogy

Choosing a SUT supplier is like choosing a surgical instrument manufacturer: you are not just buying a product, you are building a relationship that affects patient safety. Once you use one brand of single-use bioreactor bag, it is hard to switch suppliers midway (because E&L, validation and so on must be repeated). So evaluate the choice as seriously as you would a long-term partner.

CDMO Application

At a sterile fill-finish CDMO you serve several clients at once, and each may specify a different SUT supplier. You need to build an Approved Supplier List (ASL) and maintain for each supplier its audit records, quality agreement and history of technical change notifications. The complexity of this work far exceeds that of the stainless steel era.

2.5.1 Establishing Supplier Quality Requirements

Original Text

A formal quality agreement between the end-user and supplier is an important tool to outline critical roles for each party. End-users should use the quality agreements to define, establish, and document their responsibilities and the responsibilities of the suppliers. The quality agreement should define:

  • Scope of agreement and products covered
  • Key quality roles and responsibilities
  • Change notification and change approval
  • Obligations and responsibilities for the quality units of the parties involved
  • Communication expectations and points of contact
  • Auditing rights (periodic and for cause audits)
  • Escalation mechanism and dispute resolution process
  • Access to manufacturing, testing, and other data

Ideally, such quality agreements should be independent of supply agreements or technical agreements.

2.5.1.1 Supplier Manufacturing Operations

Manufacturers of single-use products should be aligned with operations that exist at the typical end-user facility. It is advantageous to have all suppliers in the entire supply chain aligned with these operations.

Single-use product suppliers should follow clearly defined methods for controlling and tracking any component, equipment, and procedures used in the manufacturing operations. Additional considerations for single-use manufacturing plant operations include:

  • Procedures to receive and route products to the inventory location
  • Establishing and tracking usability of components once in inventory
  • Procedures to approve the components for compliance with specifications, certificate of conformity, sterility claim
  • Quarantine procedures to handle/discard components with rejected status
  • Defining conditions for appropriate storage and handling of components
  • Defining controlled environment criteria for component preparation areas and assembly areas
  • Establishing conditions and methods for assembly, in-process control, final packaging, and release control
  • Tracking all components from their entry to their exit
  • Training of qualified operators, including on cleanroom procedures

Quality and supply agreements should be set up with key sub-suppliers to ensure consistent conformity to defined specifications, long term supply, and robust change control.

2.5.1.2 Supplier Quality Assurance

Establishing raw material specifications and process design spaces can significantly reduce raw material variability, strengthen change management, and provide consistent quality attributes. Suppliers should follow a QbD (Quality by Design) approach to define their design space and manufacturing operations attributes. Proper in-process and release controls should be in place.

Single-Use Product Specification Document: Specifications, developed in collaboration by suppliers and end-users, should be formatted so that the information is presented clearly. Periodic review of specifications should be performed to ensure the success of delivery and integration into the end-user facility.

Single-Use Product Codes Management: Suppliers should follow a standardized coding procedure for their components. An incremental system of coding is recommended based on: type of single-use components, volume of containers, diameter of tubing, size of connectors, size and nominal pore size of filters, type of material of construction.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Quality Agreement vs. Supply Agreement

A Quality Agreement focuses on "who is responsible for what": allocation of GMP responsibilities, audit rights and change notification obligations.

A Supply Agreement focuses on "commercial terms": price, delivery time and payment terms.

A Technical Agreement focuses on "product specifications": design parameters and test methods.

ISPE recommends keeping the three separate, because the quality agreement should be led by QA and should not be subject to the commercial pressure of purchasing negotiations. In practice, many companies mix all three types of clauses into one document, which leaves QA without control at critical moments.

Key Point: The Importance of the "Audit Rights" Clause

One of the most overlooked yet most useful clauses in a quality agreement is: "the end user has the right to conduct routine and for-cause audits of the supplier."

If this clause is not written in explicitly before signing, the supplier can later refuse your audit request. When a SUT supplier has a quality event (for example, doubts about the sterilization records of a batch of single-use bags), this clause is your legal basis for entering the supplier's site to investigate.

Analogy: The Supplier's "Factory Language"

ISPE expects the supplier's manufacturing operations to be "aligned" with the end user's, just as you would want an outsourced contractor to speak the same language. A good SUT supplier does not just "make the product"; they should understand why cleanroom classification is needed, why traceability is needed and why change notification matters, because they understand the GMP compliance pressure their downstream customers face.

Core Concept: Applying QbD at the SUT Supplier

Quality by Design (QbD): this drug development concept applies equally to SUT suppliers. A good supplier does not "produce first and test afterwards" but instead:

  • Defines the raw material design space (which polymer properties are critical)
  • Sets the process design space (the condition ranges for molding and welding)
  • Implements in-process control rather than relying on final testing

Only this truly reduces batch-to-batch quality variation, which is especially important for E&L consistency.

CDMO Application

In CDMO purchasing, it is very practical to require the supplier to provide a standardized product coding system. It lets your ERP system track exactly "which batch of product used which model of component from which supplier", an indispensable basis for traceability when investigating complaints or recalls.

2.5.1.3 Supplier Capability Matrix

Original Text

The supplier capability matrix is useful in identifying supplier candidates with the capabilities to meet specified requirements. The matrix can be used as a tool for the selection of the most appropriate suppliers. Two types of templates are presented:

  • Supplier Capability Matrix: Based on identification of "must have" criteria. Communication with suppliers during this assessment is recommended since suppliers may be able to offer alternatives. (Table 2.9)
  • Weighted Supplier Capability Matrix: Used to identify the suppliers that meet the most important requirements. Capability criteria are scored with importance weighting (Low 1 – High 10). (Table 2.10)

Key "must have" capability categories include: environmental classification, drawing revision control, change notification and control procedures, cleanroom monitoring, release criteria, lot control, training, and irradiation methods.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Different Purposes of the Two Matrices

Basic matrix (Must-Have): first filters out unqualified suppliers. This is a pass/fail threshold test. For example, if a supplier has no cleanroom monitoring program, eliminate them directly without further evaluation.

Weighted matrix: among suppliers that pass the basic threshold, compare who is "better". Each capability is given an importance score (1-10) according to your business needs, and the weighted total is calculated for ranking.

This two-tier screening method ensures that you neither accept an under-qualified supplier nor miss the best choice among qualified ones.
