Practice Questions

  1. At your site, if you were selecting a supplier of single-use bioreactor bags, which three capabilities would be your absolute must-haves? Why?
  2. In the weighted matrix, which is more important to your business: "Irradiation methods" or "Change control"? How would this priority affect your supplier selection?

CDMO Application

In a CDMO serving multiple clients, the supplier capability matrix can become part of your client presentation material: "the criteria we use to evaluate and select SUT suppliers". It is not only an internal purchasing tool but also evidence of the rigor of your supply chain management.

2.5.2 Practices Applied to Meet Supplier Quality Requirements

Original Text

2.5.2.1 Standard Operating Procedures

Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) are written procedures which specify how routine activities are to be performed by trained personnel. SOPs provide detailed instruction to facilitate consistent conformance to technical and quality system requirements.

Single-use supplier manufacturing operations that should have a clearly defined SOP include:

  • Manufacturing environment classification and associated cleanroom design
  • Gowning and de-gowning in manufacturing areas
  • Operation and maintenance of manufacturing equipment
  • Validation and qualification procedures
  • Cleaning procedures and frequency
  • Shipping, post-shipping and transportation activities
  • Investigation and sharing of reports related to recalls, non-conforming products, or unexpected conditions

2.5.2.2 Particulates Monitoring and Control

Since single-use components and assemblies may come into contact with drug product or process fluids, their environment during manufacture can have direct influence on the quality of the drug products. Aspects critical to the handling of parts and the SUT manufacturing process include:

  • Manufacturing environment classification and associated cleanroom design
  • Operators gowning and handling
  • Closed system for fluid path surfaces
  • Process steps that emit particulates (e.g., spallation)
  • Process steps that remove particulates (e.g., filtration)
  • Frequency and methods for particulates monitoring
  • Existence of continuous improvement plan

Environmental classification recommendations for the manufacturing of single-use products:

  • For the manufacture of single-use components and assemblies: ISO 8 / Grade C (in operation)
  • For final assembly operations: ISO 7 / Grade B (in operation)

2.5.2.3 Customer Change Notification

Customer change notification procedures should be established to inform customers of changes to single-use products. Suppliers should communicate all changes, however small, that directly impact the single-use product. A change notification document should be issued to the customer to provide guidance on the changes, including the date the change will occur and opportunities for the end-user to manage the change period.

The severity of the change should be assessed using a risk-based approach. For example, a change in polymer type would need to be assessed to determine the potential impact on polymer properties and E&L profiles.

2.5.2.4 Manufacturing Certificates

Manufacturing certificates should be provided by all suppliers along the complete supply chain. At delivery of single-use products, the supplier should provide all appropriate certificates including:

  • Product description, reference, batch number, and expiration date
  • Related irradiation batch number (if applicable)
  • Compliance statements
  • Batch release testing information (product conformity, visual inspection, bioburden, endotoxin, particulates, leak testing, integrity testing, gamma irradiation, etc.)

2.5.2.5 Shelf Life

Suppliers should provide expiration dates for single-use products in the post-sterilization stage. For a complete single-use assembly, the expiration date should be determined by the worst-case component (the one with the shortest shelf life after sterilization). To determine shelf life, suppliers should consider sterility testing, gas barrier properties, structural and mechanical integrity, biocompatibility and physicochemical tests, material resistance to gamma irradiation dose, and packaging integrity.

2.5.2.6 Database of Approved Materials

Through a partnership between suppliers and end-users, a database may be generated to list product contact components that have been tested and qualified per the requested technical specifications and quality level. This database of approved materials should be maintained by the quality department.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Cleanroom Grade Requirements for SUT Suppliers

ISPE SUT sets the following requirements for the supplier's manufacturing environment:

  • General component and assembly manufacture: ISO 8 / EU Grade C (in operation)
  • Final assembly operations: ISO 7 / EU Grade B (in operation)

The logic behind this is that SUT components directly contact the drug or process fluid, so the cleanliness of the manufacturing environment needs to match the cleanliness grades of drug manufacturing facilities. Otherwise particulate contamination is introduced at the supplier and is difficult to eradicate by any control applied later.

Key Point: Change Notification Is One of the Most Important Quality Controls for SUT

The Customer Change Notification (CCN) from SUT suppliers is often underestimated in practice. A typical example:

  • The supplier changes the film polymer formulation of a component (for them just a "raw material optimization")
  • The end user's E&L (extractables/leachables) study was based on the old formulation
  • The new formulation may introduce leachables that were never evaluated

Without a change notification mechanism, you would never know the change happened. That is why the quality agreement must explicitly require that "all changes affecting the product, large or small, must be notified in advance".

Analogy: The SOP Is the Supplier's "Recipe"

Think of a restaurant chain: the same dish should taste the same in every city. The SUT supplier's SOP is that "recipe". If the same single-use film bag varies in quality across production shifts and operators, the SOP is not detailed enough or is not being followed. During an audit, asking to watch operators' actual practice against the SOP is the most direct way to verify quality.

Core Concept: The "Weakest Link Principle" for Shelf Life

The shelf life of a complete single-use assembly is determined by the component with the shortest shelf life (the weakest link principle).

This means that even if 99% of the components in an assembly can be kept for two years, if one connector has a shelf life of only one year, the whole assembly can only be used for one year. This has a major impact on warehouse management: purchase batch sizes must be calculated from actual usage rate and the component with the shortest shelf life, to avoid large quantities expiring and being scrapped.

CDMO Application

Maintaining an approved materials database is critical in a CDMO:

  • Different clients' products may have different compatibility requirements
  • Record the approval status of each component, the basis for approval (which tests) and the shelf life
  • When a supplier issues a change notification, assess which client products are affected
  • Access to the database should be controlled by QA to prevent unapproved changes

Practice Questions

  1. Has your site built an approved materials database for SUT suppliers? If not, what is the biggest challenge in building one?
  2. If a supplier notifies you of a minor change to a polymer formulation, what is your internal assessment process? Who has the authority to decide "retesting is needed" or "it can be accepted directly"?
2.5.3 Defining Supplier Audit Requirements

Original Text

Single-use suppliers, manufacturers, and assemblers should be audited on design, manufacturing process, and supply chain management until delivery to the end-user. The frequency and duration of such audits should be included in the quality agreement.

The supplier audit should cover both manufacturing and technical aspects, with special focus on:

  • Compliance to ISO 9001 certification (and other applicable standards)
  • Management and control of raw materials and components
  • Manufacturing process overview
  • Sourcing and supply chain strategy
  • Manufacturing environment monitoring (microbiological and particulates trending)
  • Sterilization process (frequency, procedure, and records of dose audits for irradiation)
  • Quality management systems (including quality policy and change management processes)
  • SOPs, document controls
  • Complaint management (with tracking, recording, and CAPA processes)
  • Change management process (major versus minor status)
  • Operator training plan and records
  • Batch record tracking

The frequency of audits should be determined based on risk of the supplier and type or criticality of the item/service:

  • New suppliers or highly critical items: once a year
  • Well-known suppliers with documented long-term reliability: every two to three years

Additional audit documentation to review includes: environmental monitoring SOPs and reports, training SOPs, inventory control SOPs, change control SOPs, inspection SOPs, packaging SOPs, quality control SOPs, health and safety SOPs and records, job tracking file review, quality agreement, and checklists for audits.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Point: Two "Killer Questions" in an Audit

In a supplier audit, the answers to two questions best reveal the supplier's quality maturity:

  1. "Please show me the closure records of your last three CAPAs." A good supplier will have clear nonconformances, root cause analyses and effective preventive actions. A poor-quality supplier may have no CAPA records (meaning problems were never seriously handled) or CAPAs that were never closed.
  2. "Where is your sub-supplier list? Have they all been audited?" The SUT supply chain is deep, and raw material suppliers (such as polymer resin manufacturers) matter just as much for quality.

Analogy: Audits Are Like Driver's License Renewal

An audit is like the periodic renewal of a driver's license: not because you have had an accident before, but to make sure that "your current status still meets the standard". A new driver (new supplier) renews every year, while an experienced driver (a long-term supplier with a good reputation) may renew every three years. But if you have a recent violation (a quality event), you need to be reassessed immediately, no matter how long ago the last renewal was.

CDMO Application

In a CDMO, clients often ask to see your SUT supplier audit records as part of their due diligence. Build a structured Supplier Audit Program that includes:

  • An annual audit plan (prioritized by risk)
  • A standard audit questionnaire (covering all areas required by ISPE)
  • An audit report template (with a CAPA tracking table)
  • A supplier performance scorecard (linked to later purchasing decisions)

This program itself can also be a highlight of your quality capability when bidding for new clients.

Core Concept: Risk Tiering of Audit Frequency

ISPE provides a clear framework for audit frequency:

Note: SUT suppliers are usually classified as "high criticality", so an annual audit is the common industry practice.

2.6 User Requirement Specification Development

Original Text

The URS (User Requirement Specification) is used to specify aspects of an equipment (i.e., single-use product) to ensure it is appropriate for the intended application. The URS is a document that the end-user of the product develops and writes; the document is used to convey the requirements to the supplier.

It is beneficial to discuss requirements with suppliers during URS generation since ongoing technology advances may open up new options for commercially available systems. The URS document should define the needs and acceptance criteria based on the intended use, containing specific criteria that need to be met while providing general guidance for non-critical aspects.

2.6.1 Developing the URS Document

The foundation of the URS is based on knowledge of the drug product and process along with knowledge of the capabilities of the single-use product. Specific requirements should describe:

  • Quality requirements for single-use products (as discussed in Section 2.4)
  • Supplier quality and audit requirements (as discussed in Section 2.5)
  • End-user product and process requirements

Quality Requirements for Single-Use Products (examples):

  • Assembly shall be designed based on the sketch provided with this URS
  • Wetted components in the assembly shall have a complete extractables profile
  • Wetted components shall be biocompatible and compatible with temperature, pH, and pressure conditions during use
  • All components shall comply with the particulates requirements
  • Product certificates shall be provided with the product confirming URS requirements are met

Supplier Audit and Quality Requirements (examples):

  • Manufacturing area shall be classified and controlled to specified classification area
  • Product inventory shall be tracked by the manufacturer and its suppliers
  • Periodic auditing of suppliers shall be performed with frequency based on risk
  • A documented change management process shall be followed

End-User Product and Process Requirements: Sharing process information and operational constraints are key requirements. Since process specific details may contain sensitive information, the information can be a partial disclosure; in other cases, full disclosure with a signed non-disclosure agreement may be needed.

Example requirements for common single-use assembly types include:

  • Bioprocess Container Assembly: Size, fill rate, drain rate, vent(s), dimensional constraints, connection type, mixing, sparging, sensors, storage time/conditions
  • Filter Assembly: Separation/pore size, capacity, pressure profile, connection type, frame/support criteria, pump characteristics
  • Transfer Assembly: Flow rate with fluid viscosity, pressure profile, connection type, pump characteristics

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Three-Layer Structure of a URS

ISPE recommends that a URS include three types of requirements:

  1. Product quality requirements: E&L (extractables/leachables), biocompatibility, particulates, integrity (from Section 2.4)
  2. Supplier quality requirements: manufacturing environment, audits, change control (from Section 2.5)
  3. Process technical requirements: flow rate, pressure, temperature, dimensions, connector types (defined by the end user)

The URS is a document written by the end user and passed to the supplier. It is not the supplier's specification; it is the document in which you tell the supplier "what I need".

Key Terms: URS vs. FRS vs. DS

In the C&Q (commissioning and qualification) system, the document hierarchy is usually:

  • URS (User Requirements Specification): what functions I need (the owner/user perspective)
  • FRS (Functional Requirements Specification): how the system delivers those functions (the engineer's perspective)
  • DS (Design Specification): the detailed technical design (the manufacturer/supplier perspective)

The SUT URS sits at the very top of the C&Q system. It is the basis for all later qualification activities, so it must be complete and accurate.

Key Point: Balancing Confidentiality and Technical Disclosure

ISPE states clearly that when developing the URS, it is sometimes necessary to disclose sensitive process information to the supplier so that the single-use component design meets your needs. For example, you need to tell the supplier your buffer pH range so that the supplier can select a suitable polymer material.

Solution: sign a Non-Disclosure Agreement (NDA) before the URS is issued. This protects your intellectual property while allowing the supplier to propose the most suitable technical solution.

Analogy: The URS Is Your "Renovation Brief"

Imagine hiring a contractor to renovate your kitchen. A good renovation brief (URS) should say: "I need an island that seats 6 people (functional requirement), all joints must be waterproof (quality requirement), and all materials must pass food safety certification (regulatory requirement)." It should not say: "Please build the island using brand X quartz". That is already a design specification and limits too early the supplier's room to propose innovative solutions.

CDMO Application

In a CDMO, developing a SUT URS has a particular challenge: the URS is written by you (the CDMO), but the final user is your client. The best practice is to:

  • Build a standard URS template that covers all elements required by ISPE
  • Review the URS jointly with the client's technical and QA teams to make sure the client's special requirements (such as specific E&L specifications) are included
  • Obtain the client's signed approval on the URS to establish accountability
2.6.2 Additional Considerations for SU Components, Assemblies, and Systems

Original Text

Single-Use Components Versus Single-Use Assemblies: The URS document for single-use components usually contains a subset of the requirements listed for a single-use assembly. The differences are related to the methods of connection of components and for containment of fluids. The same steps would be followed for developing the URS for both.

Single-Use Assemblies Versus SUSs: Since SUSs (Single-Use Systems) integrate single-use assemblies with multiple-use equipment, the URS documents for SUSs are typically more complex than for single-use assemblies. The SUS URS builds on the URS requirements for assemblies and includes additional requirements for mechanical, electrical, and software/controls that add significantly to the complexity of the URS.

For additional information on the development of the URS for the multiple-use portion of SUSs, refer to:

  • ISPE Good Practice Guide: Good Engineering Practice
  • ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification
  • ISPE GAMP® 5: A Risk-Based Approach to Compliant GxP Computerized Systems
  • ISPE Guide: Science and Risk-Based Approach for the Delivery of Facilities, Systems, and Equipment

It is strongly recommended that the end-user includes a sketch of the expected assembly as part of the URS document.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Point: Why Attach an Assembly Sketch?

ISPE specifically stresses attaching an Assembly Sketch to the URS. The reasons:

  • Text descriptions are prone to ambiguity (for example, for a "tee connector", which direction is which?)
  • A sketch can clearly confirm connector positions, flow direction and sensor positions
  • The supplier can use the sketch as the basis for design, reducing rework
  • The sketch becomes the "design baseline document" for later qualification

Practice Questions

  1. When your site buys single-use components, do you have a written URS? If not, what problems occur most often (for example, the product does not meet conditions of use, or is incompatible with existing equipment)?
  2. If you needed to write a URS for a single-use bioreactor (bioreactor bag + stainless steel support + control system), how would you organize the document? Which departments would need to be involved?

Core Concept: Three Levels of URS Complexity

Understanding the levels of URS complexity for SUT helps allocate the effort of document development sensibly:

The URS for an SUS (single-use system) must integrate multiple guides such as ISPE C&Q and GAMP 5, because it contains both single-use consumables and equipment that must be qualified.

2.7 Facility Design

Original Text

Single-use products have been incorporated into facility design since the 1980s; however, only recently have companies been altering the adjacency of rooms specifically to adapt to SUSs. This section intends to explain the affected parts of the facility and current trends so that the facility designer can successfully design an efficient and cost-effective next generation facility.

2.7.1 Therapeutic Manufacturing Applications

SUT has become more widely used in pharmaceutical and biopharmaceutical applications. Key therapeutic applications where single-use products are currently being used:

  • Research and development (R&D)
  • Cell therapy and gene therapy
  • Large-scale protein manufacture (including vaccines and antibody drug conjugates)

General trends across these applications:

  • Wash/autoclave areas and mechanical space for utilities have been scaled back in size
  • Warehousing space, corridors, lay down areas and waste processing areas have increased in size

For cell therapy and gene therapy: these facilities consist of small, flexible, and modular units. Due to heavy reliance on manual operations, automated processing will likely impact facility designs and modular facilities may get smaller.

Large-scale protein manufacturing has been impacted the most. Adjacency of areas is important if excessive movement and long tubing lengths are to be avoided. There is often a need to make more space available for the movement and storage of bioprocess bag containers (totes).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Point: The Special Case of Cell Therapy Facilities

Cell and gene therapy facilities rely almost entirely on SUT because cleaning validation of conventional stainless steel equipment is simply impractical for highly customized, small-batch personalized medicines. Each batch may correspond to a different patient, so full cleaning validation between batches is not possible. Single-use consumables in cell therapy are therefore a regulatory necessity, not just a convenience.

CDMO Application

For a sterile fill-finish CDMO, the main SUT applications are the holding tanks and transfer lines on the filling line. When evaluating whether to introduce SUT, the key considerations are:

  • Is there enough warehouse space to store large quantities of single-use consumables?
  • Have waste staging and disposal routes been planned (considering GMP flow)?
  • Is there enough staging space next to the filling line for assemblies waiting to be used?

Core Concept: The Two-Way Impact of SUT on Facility Design

After SUT is introduced, facility space requirements show an interesting trade-off:

This "space reallocation" effect must be planned in advance when designing a new plant or retrofitting an old one.

2.7.1.1–2.7.1.3 Facility Design Concepts

Original Text

Ballroom Concept vs. Dance Floor Concept

The ballroom concept is "a large manufacturing area that has no fixed equipment and minimal segregation due to the use of functionally closed systems." These facilities consist of large open spaces where skids could be wheeled in and set up to perform a process. However, users of operational facilities have experienced drawbacks:

  • Process equipment is not truly mobile since connections to utilities and drains are often required
  • Tubing management is not addressed, causing trip hazards and mix-ups
  • Volumes for production continue to increase, creating a need to make systems static in location
  • Large lay down spaces are needed for storage
  • There is minimal standardization in SUT, leading to minimal opportunities for interchangeability

To address the above concerns, the dance floor concept was created where the equipment is clustered together to create a more compact facility without trip hazards. Considerations include:

  • Concept Design: Orientation, clustering, and spacing of equipment to provide compact facility footprint
  • Basic Design: Ergonomic aspects (height of platforms, high level tubing routes). Can be subcontracted to equipment integrators for planning utilities/electrical/data routing
  • Detailed Design: Tubing and pipework routing can be 3-D modeled in detail, providing assurance that GMP and operational excellence aspects have been considered

Factory-in-a-Box Concept

The factory-in-a-box concept is where a facility is built with standardized modules and then assembled at the end-user site. The development starts from the actual production process and then a facility is created around it. It is a complete turnkey approach where the manufacturing process is optimized including the validation of the facility and the training of the manufacturing staff.

Complete manufacturing facilities have been placed into operation within eighteen months using this method. This timeline compares favorably when considering the three to five years needed to design and bring a facility to production in the traditional way.

The factory-in-a-box option allows modules and associated processing equipment to be built off-site while the foundation of the new facility is completed concurrently (parallel activities).

Box-Within-a-Box Concept

The box-within-a-box concept is where an empty facility is constructed well in advance of its intended use or where a previously constructed building is used as a starting point. A factory-in-a-box or custom-built facility can then be constructed inside this outer envelope, thus significantly reducing the project timelines.

Tutorial Commentary

Analogy: Ballroom vs. Dance Floor

The ballroom concept is like an "open-plan office": in theory it is flexible and anyone can move anywhere. In reality, everyone's network and power cables are plugged into fixed positions, so "can be moved at any time" becomes an illusion.

The dance floor concept is like a carefully designed "island of workstations": the position of each workstation is fixed, but the cabling runs through the ceiling, the aisles stay clear and nobody trips over cables. It takes more up-front design but is safer and more efficient in use.

Key Point: What 18 Months vs. 5 Years Means

A Factory-in-a-Box can shorten the time to bring a facility into operation from the traditional 3-5 years to 18 months. For the pharmaceutical industry this means:

  • Faster response to the market: especially in emergency pandemic response (such as COVID-19 vaccine production)
  • Lower capital risk: capital returns can be seen after 18 months rather than waiting 5 years
  • Easier expansion: standard modules can be replicated at different locations on an existing site

This is also why companies such as GE (now Cytiva) strongly promote modular SUT facility solutions.

Practice Questions

  1. Which concept is your site's current layout closest to (ballroom, dance floor or something else)? What operational problems have you met because of poor layout design (such as tripping over lines or confused material flow)?
  2. If your company were building a new sterile fill-finish CDMO plant, which facility design concept would you choose? Why?

Core Concept: Comparing the Three Facility Design Philosophies

The three facility design concepts introduced by ISPE reflect the evolution of thinking in SUT facility design:

2.7.2 Bioprocess Area Applications

Original Text

2.7.2.1 Upstream Operations

Inoculum Preparation:

  • Current State: The inoculum preparation area was an early adopter of SUT. Vials, shake flasks, and roller bottles have been in use for many years.
  • Future State: Trend to move single-use bioreactor operations to the main bioreactor area; this reduces the footprint of the higher classification area.

Bioreactors:

  • Current State: Single-use bioreactors up to the 3,000 L scale are currently available. Even in stainless steel facilities, there are significant single-use applications (feed/media hold, sampling, antifoam addition, transfer lines).
  • Future State: Continuous/semi-continuous equipment reduces vessel/bag size, enabling greater use of single-use equipment while significantly increasing facility throughput. Connected equipment should be positioned next to each other, resulting in more compact facilities.

2.7.2.2 Downstream Operations

Purification:

  • Current State: As a rule of thumb, if column sizes are above 80 cm in diameter, stainless steel buffer tanks will likely be required.
  • Future State: With the advent of continuous chromatography and in-line dilution, buffer volumes may be reduced to a level where SUT becomes more applicable.

Bulk Formulation and Filling:

  • Current State: The bulk formulation and fill areas have not changed greatly in room size or classification due to SUT. Elimination of CIP and SIP requirements for vessels has a clear benefit in removing CIP skids and reducing pure steam generation capacity requirements.
  • Future State: With single-use freeze thaw/filling systems, future considerations may include filling bulk drug within the purification areas.

Final Fill and Finish:

  • Current State: Single-use components (needles and associated tubing) have been in the product pathway for many years.
  • Future State: Improvements in single-use components and peristaltic pump systems have accelerated SUT adoption. In newer facilities, it is common to interchange stainless steel intermediate tanks with single-use bags on filling machines through load cell hanger frames.

2.7.2.3 Process Support Operations

The size of wash facilities and utilities are impacted in all therapeutic applications. Some facilities have been able to remove the wash area and autoclave from their process with the use of CIP and single-use assemblies; this may become a future trend.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: SUT Penetration Across Bioprocess Stages

The adoption of SUT is uneven across the stages of bioprocessing:

  • Upstream: the highest SUT adoption, because batch sizes are usually small (<3000 L) and SUT technology is mature
  • Downstream: moderate adoption. Chromatography columns are still mostly stainless steel, but buffer hold vessels and transfer lines widely use SUT
  • Fill/Finish: adoption is increasing rapidly, especially as intermediate holding tanks have commonly changed to single-use bags

Key Point: The "80 cm Column Diameter" Rule of Thumb

ISPE gives a practical decision rule: if the chromatography column diameter exceeds 80 cm, stainless steel buffer tanks are usually needed. The logic is that large-diameter columns need large volumes of buffer, and large single-use containers (>2000 L) currently have no advantage in either technology or cost.

But this rule will change as Continuous Chromatography becomes widespread. Continuous chromatography greatly reduces buffer demand and makes larger-scale SUT applications possible.

Analogy: The SUT Revolution on the Filling Line

The intermediate holding tank on a traditional filling line is like the big soup pot in a restaurant kitchen: every change of menu requires a thorough wash, with a lot of labor and validation work. A SUT single-use bag is like using a disposable pot: after use you simply replace it and save the trouble of cleaning validation. A "Load Cell Hanger Frame" is the fixture that lets you hang a single-use bag where a stainless steel tank used to stand, so you can introduce SUT quickly on an existing filling line.

CDMO Application

For a sterile fill-finish CDMO, the most direct SUT opportunities are:

  • Change the intermediate holding vessel on the filling line to a single-use bag (with a load cell hanger frame)
  • Change the transfer line set that moves bulk product from production to the filling line to single-use tubing
  • These two changes can significantly reduce the risk of cross-contamination (especially important for a CDMO, because the same equipment serves multiple clients)
  • They also reduce the CIP validation workload and raise equipment utilization
2.7.3 Key Considerations, Challenges, and Potential Advantages

Original Text

Volumetric Process Scale: In bioprocessing areas where required vessel size is greater than 2,000 L, physical assembly/handling of the bag and tubing size restrictions on fluid flow should be considered. There is a threshold where stainless steel systems become more cost-effective than SUSs, in terms of price per gram.

Continuous Processing: Continuous chromatography allows for a compact layout of equipment to facilitate liquid transfer between columns, minimizing holdup volumes. Single-use mixers can be used for collecting, combining, and conditioning fluids, providing a smaller space advantage over pool vessels.

Software Integration and Clustering: Continuous processes increase the need for integration/software integrators. Clustering equipment into small groups allows for:

  • Controlling and monitoring of isolated pressure sensors, pumps, and balances
  • Enabling of defined tubing routing between systems
  • Reduction of the number of utility drops

Using Multiple Floors: In an SUT-based facility, using multiple floors should be approached with caution. The full liquid height should be taken into account with regard to pressure. For example, a length of 10 m would result in a head pressure of 0.981 bar; if tubing sets need to be uprated to handle higher pressures, the cost may increase.

GMP Flows: Two concepts of GMP flow in hybrid BSL-1 facilities:

  • Two Corridor Concept: Separate supply and return corridors. Operators enter through one set of doors (clean corridor) and leave through another (dirty corridor)
  • One Corridor Concept: Operators enter and leave through the same set of doors, or enter through one airlock and leave through a different airlock but to the same corridor

Selection will depend on a thorough risk assessment considering product type (sterile, low bioburden), level of closure, product cross-contamination risks, and environmental contamination risks.

Process Connectivity, Closure, and Containment:

  • The biopharmaceutical industry continues to challenge historical guidance on environmental particulate requirements by leveraging single-use containment and aseptic connection advantages
  • BSL-2 and BSL-3 overlaid on GMP creates complex conflicts in pressurization and airlock sequences when hazardous materials are stored in single-use tubing and bioprocess containers
  • Non-aseptic connections and potential for leaks should be considered; preparations made for comprehensive SOPs, personnel safety, secondary liquid containment, and hazardous waste decontamination

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The SUT Scale Threshold: When to Stop Using SUT?

SUT is not more economical than stainless steel at every scale. There is a "cost crossover point":

  • When container volume exceeds 2000 L, the physical handling of single-use bags and fluid line limitations start to become a problem
  • On a "cost per gram of drug" basis, stainless steel systems have the advantage at large scale
  • This crossover point will gradually move up as continuous processing matures (in future, stainless steel may only be needed above 5000 L)

For a CDMO, knowing this threshold helps give clients of different scales the most suitable technical advice.

Key Point: The Hydraulic Trap of Multi-Floor Design

This is an easily overlooked but important engineering detail. When a single-use bag is on the first floor and liquid must be piped to equipment in the basement or on the second floor:

  • A 10 m height difference = about 0.981 bar of static head pressure
  • This may exceed the rated pressure of standard single-use tubing
  • High pressure rated tubing is needed, which increases cost
  • Or the layout must be re-planned so that material flows "with gravity"

This problem does not exist in stainless steel systems (the piping is designed to withstand high pressure), but in SUT it must be a factor in facility design.

Analogy: Double-Corridor vs. Single-Corridor GMP Flow

A double corridor is like an airport where arrivals and departures are completely separate: people coming in (clean side) and leaving (used side) take different routes and never cross. It is the strictest option but needs a lot of space.

A single corridor is like most offices: entry and exit use the same corridor, and SOPs manage the timing to avoid conflicts. It is space-efficient but needs strict operating discipline. For BSL facilities, a double corridor is usually required.

CDMO Application

In a sterile fill-finish CDMO, GMP flow design is especially important, because used single-use consumables (contaminated with product after use) must be kept completely separate from clean new consumables. Common best practices:

  • Waste leaves the production area through a dedicated waste corridor or elevator
  • Used single-use bags go straight into sealed waste containers and are not opened in the cleanroom
  • Keep the waste exit as far as possible from the raw material entrance
2.7.4–2.7.5 Storage, Supply, and Safety Considerations

Original Text

2.7.4 Storage and Supply

Sufficient storage space should be provided in the warehouse and in the staging areas of the production floor, to account for incoming single-use components, packaging/cardboard waste, and used single-use components. Considerations should be made for a layout that facilitates the flow of waste products. Special entry/exit points may be needed for a smooth transition out of the facility. Pickup frequency by the disposal company is an important factor that defines the storage space and layout.

Standardization can result in a reduction in the space requirements for the on-site warehouse. Currently, significant contingency stocks are required due to the long lead times of single-use assemblies. If these assemblies are readily available off the shelf, the inventory can be significantly reduced.

2.7.5 Safety Considerations

SUSs impose a special set of safety concerns due to inherent features of SUT:

  • The need to install and dismantle systems brings the operator closer to the system, increasing the need for risk mitigations associated with spills and ruptures
  • The possibility to move systems may introduce transportation activity, increasing the need for risk mitigations

Facility design for SUSs should include a health and safety risk assessment as part of the design work. Safety considerations include:

  • Pressure rating of connectors used between single-use components and stainless steel parts
  • Bioprocess bags and single-use tubing at eye level (splashing risk)
  • Flammables, caustic or other hazardous materials, spills
  • Potential risk of leakage or rupture
  • Tubing on floor creating tripping hazards
  • Cuts to frozen bioprocess bags, resultant spills
  • Equipment malfunctions, clamps, fingers
  • Stability of mobile stairs, loose items
  • Protection rails for glass surfaces (transportation protection)

Health considerations include:

  • Ergonomics for installation, handling and removing containers
  • Ergonomics for handling solid waste
  • Mobile bag containers at reasonable size and weight
  • Aerosol formation when disconnecting systems
  • Odor waste handling
  • Provisions for handling spills

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Warehousing Challenges of SUT

After SUT is introduced, warehouse management is more complex than expected:

  • Long lead time: the lead time for single-use assemblies is usually 8-16 weeks, requiring a larger safety stock
  • Bulky: even folded, the outer packaging of single-use bags takes up a lot of space
  • Waste volume is 3-5 times that of new product: expanded used waste takes up more space than compressed new product
  • Waste pickup frequency determines staging space: if the waste contractor comes every two weeks, you need two weeks of waste staging space

Key Point: Unique Safety Risks of SUT

Compared with stainless steel systems, SUT brings some new safety risks that call for specific facility design and operating SOPs:

  • Trip hazard from floor lines: single-use tubing stretched across the floor is more likely to cause tripping than stainless steel piping
  • Eye splash risk: transparent bioreactor bags are at eye level, so operators need eye protection
  • Cut risk from frozen bags: single-use bags in frozen storage are hard and brittle, and improper handling during thawing may cause leaks
  • Aerosol risk on disconnection: aerosols may be generated when connections are disconnected, which needs special attention in high biosafety level facilities

Practice Questions

  1. Has your site assessed the waste volume of single-use consumables? Roughly how much SUT waste does one filling batch generate? Is your current waste staging space sufficient?
  2. When installing a single-use bioreactor bag, what safety problems do operators meet most often? Are the existing SOPs and safety provisions adequate to deal with these risks?
2.8 Waste Management

Original Text

This section addresses methods for the handling of single-use products after use. It includes general considerations for waste management methods, which are dependent upon the components used and the nature of the usage.

Generally, biopharmaceutical manufacturing facilities handle soiled components (i.e., contaminated with product solutions) as either biohazardous waste or waste for landfill. In addition, components which contain highly potent compounds require additional control and handling procedures.

2.8.1 Decontamination

Decontamination of single-use products refers to the process of rendering it safe for disposal by removing agents that should not be present (e.g., bacterial, virological, chemical).

If required, decontamination can be achieved through high temperature or chemical treatment. These treatments typically include:

  • Autoclave Sterilization: Moist heat sterilization uses steam heated to 121°C and injected into a chamber. This method is used to decontaminate single-use items before disposal when biological risk is present.
  • Incineration: Waste material is burned, and the resultant energy released is typically used to generate usable heat or power. High temperatures during incineration can destroy many pathogens and toxic materials; hence incinerators are often used in the disposal of medical waste.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Terms: Decontamination vs. Sterilization

Decontamination: the process of making waste "safe enough to discard". The aim is to reduce biohazard risk, not necessarily to reach a sterilization level.

Sterilization: the process of destroying all microorganisms (including spores), a more stringent treatment.

In waste management, "decontamination" rather than "sterilization" is usually required, but the two often use the same method in practice (autoclaving). The main difference is the rigor of validation and record requirements.

Analogy: Autoclave vs. Incineration

An autoclave is like a high-temperature, high-pressure rice cooker used to "kill germs": bacteria and viruses are destroyed, making the waste safe enough to enter the general waste stream (or the incinerator).

Incineration is like using fire to "completely eliminate" waste: it not only kills germs but also removes the physical form of the waste, leaving only ash. Incineration can recover energy (power generation) and is especially effective for highly potent compounds (such as anticancer drug residues), because high temperature destroys the molecular structure.

Key Point: SUT Waste Is the Biggest Environmental Criticism of Single-Use Technology

The main environmental challenge facing SUT is the large volume of plastic waste. A mid-sized biopharma plant can generate tens of tonnes of single-use plastic waste every year. Current industry response strategies include:

  • Waste-minimizing design: choose assembly designs with fewer components
  • Energy-recovery incineration: convert incineration heat into site electricity or heating, reducing net carbon emissions
  • Recycling after decontamination: some waste with no biological contamination can enter plastic recycling streams after appropriate decontamination
  • Biodegradable material development: the industry is researching biodegradable polymers as alternative materials (still at an early stage)

CDMO Application

Special situations that SUT waste management must consider in a sterile fill-finish CDMO:

  • Cross-contamination risk across products: SUT waste from different clients' products must be labeled and handled separately to avoid cross-identification errors
  • Highly potent compounds: if a client's product contains highly potent compounds (oncology), all SUT waste that has contacted the product needs a special handling route, with corresponding waste disposal records
  • Waste cost included in the service contract: the autoclave and incineration costs of SUT waste are not small, and should be explicitly included when negotiating service contracts with clients
  • Environmental regulatory compliance: Taiwan's waste regulations require all biohazardous waste to be entrusted to a licensed waste disposal contractor, with waste transfer manifests retained

Practice Questions

  1. How does your site currently handle SUT waste? Is it autoclaved first and then incinerated, or sent directly for outsourced incineration? What is the basis for choosing this process (regulatory requirements, cost, efficiency)?
  2. If a client asked you to submit an environmental impact report on SUT waste, what data could you provide today? Which tracking records are missing?
  3. When calculating the Total Cost of Ownership of SUT, what percentage does waste disposal cost usually account for? How does this ratio look at your site?

Core Concept: Waste Type Determines Treatment Method

The treatment method for SUT waste depends on the nature of the waste:

Section Summary

  • The Quality Agreement is the foundation document of SUT supply chain management and must be kept separate from the supply agreement and the technical agreement, so that QA keeps the lead. The agreement must explicitly include audit rights, change notification obligations and the division of responsibilities.
  • SUT supplier manufacturing environment grades: general component manufacture requires ISO 8/Grade C and final assembly operations require ISO 7/Grade B. This is because SUT directly contacts the drug, so its manufacturing cleanliness grade must align with the drug manufacturing environment.
  • Risk tiering of supplier audit frequency: new suppliers or high-criticality materials are audited annually, and long-term partners with a good record every 2-3 years. SUT suppliers are usually classified as high criticality, so an annual audit is the norm.
  • The three-layer structure of the URS: product quality requirements + supplier quality requirements + process technical requirements. The URS is written by the end user (you) and passed to the supplier. Attaching an assembly sketch is recommended. For an SUS (an integrated system that includes equipment), URS complexity rises significantly and the C&Q and GAMP 5 frameworks need to be integrated.
  • Evolution of facility design: ballroom style (flexible but chaotic) → dance floor style (compact and safe, needs up-front design effort). Factory-in-a-Box (modular, 18-month delivery) and Box-within-a-Box (using the shell of an existing building) offer options for accelerating new plant construction. After SUT is introduced, warehouse and waste space increases significantly while cleaning/utility space shrinks.
  • The dual-track approach to waste management: general waste → landfill; biohazardous waste → autoclave decontamination + incineration. Highly potent compound waste requires an authorized waste contractor. The cost and space requirements of waste management must be built into planning at the very start of SUT facility design.
2

Section 2: 3. Implementation & Use

p79-p102

ISPE Good Practice Guide: Single-Use Technology | p79 - p102

Learning Objectives

  • Master the technology transfer process for single-use technology (SUT), and understand the core considerations of gap assessment, planning and scheduling, facility constraints and regulatory compliance
  • Understand the role of regulatory compliance in SUT implementation, and become familiar with the data requirements that the main regulators (EMA, FDA, WHO) and standards (USP, ISO) place on single-use materials
  • Gain an in-depth understanding of the definition, sources, assessment methods and safety thresholds of leachables, and their potential impact on patient safety and product quality
  • Systematically understand the validation framework for SUT, including supplier documentation review, installation qualification (IQ), operational qualification (OQ), performance qualification (PQ) and the various in-house testing requirements
  • Build a robust supply chain management capability, covering quality agreements, lead time management, complaint management and security of supply
  • Plan an effective single-use technology (SUT) training program, understand the risk introduced by the increase in manual operating steps, and learn how systematic training reduces the risk of failure
3.1 Technology Transfer

Original Text

Single-use products may be integrated into an existing facility, creating a hybrid system of stainless steel and single-use components, or implemented as an end-to-end solution. While it is possible to create a complete single-use manufacturing train, the majority of SUT implementations are hybrid systems which leverage existing infrastructure. Implementation may be as simple as integrating some fluid processing technology, such as bioprocess bags and encapsulated filters, or may extend to major unit operations including bioreactors and chromatography systems. The availability of single-use solutions, that can address a wide variety of process needs, continues to increase as more suppliers enter the market and technology matures. The technology transfer process for SUSs has commonalities with traditional stainless steel systems as well as unique requirements. Implementation of a bioprocess bag, unit operation, or end-to-end solution requires the same considerations:

  • Assessment, selection and design of single-use products
  • Supplier risk assessment and sourcing
  • Planning and implementation
  • Scale-up considerations
  • Facility constraints

Assessments should be performed in several areas including organizational drivers and goals, the process, available single-use products, supplier capabilities/availabilities/sourcing, the facility, future scale-up requirements, and regulatory compliance.

Assessment of the process and single-use product should be made with consideration for the overall goal to be achieved by implementation. Goals include, but are not limited to:

  • Expanding capacity, scale-out or scale-up strategies
  • Eliminating bottlenecks or process fit
  • Replacement of a unit operation or obsolete equipment
  • Reduction or elimination of cleaning and sterilization
  • Reducing unit operation, lot-to-lot, or campaign turnaround time
  • Reducing cost
  • Improving process assurance, e.g., use of completely closed sampling and fluid handling solutions

Organizational goals and process requirements should be used to develop robust user requirements to evaluate current single-use products to implement in a new or existing facility. Assessment should include consideration of the following factors:

  • Material compatibility
  • Equipment design and configuration
  • Facility configuration and capabilities
  • Process requirements (particularly instrumentation and controls)
  • Regulatory requirements
  • Scale-up
  • Risk management
  • Supplier capabilities and regional support

For further information, refer to ISPE Good Practice Guide: Technology Transfer (Second Edition).

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Dual Nature of SUT Implementation

Technology transfer to SUT takes two forms:

  • Hybrid systems: SUT is introduced into only part of an existing stainless steel facility, for example by replacing only the buffer bags or filters. This is the most common scenario and accounts for the majority of SUT implementations.
  • End-to-end single-use (End-to-End SUS): the entire process from upstream to downstream uses single-use consumables. It offers the greatest flexibility, but also the greatest supply chain management complexity.

The choice depends on a combined assessment of process requirements, current facility status, regulatory strategy and long-term business goals. There is no single right answer.

Analogy

Think of SUT technology transfer as a restaurant kitchen renovation: a hybrid system is like replacing only the stove and keeping the rest of the equipment, which is low cost and low risk but of limited benefit. An end-to-end approach is like redesigning the whole kitchen workflow, which needs a high one-time investment but gives the best long-term efficiency. Most restaurants first try out one new oven, and expand step by step once it proves useful. The logic of SUT implementation is exactly the same.

Key Notes

The original text lists seven objectives for SUT implementation, two of which deserve particular attention:

  • Eliminating cleaning and sterilization: this is one of the core advantages of SUT. Without CIP/SIP, batch turnaround time is greatly shortened.
  • Improving process assurance: using fully closed sampling and fluid transfer solutions reduces contamination risk and meets GMP expectations for sterile manufacturing.

When evaluating, do not look only at the "implementation cost". Include the long-term savings in cleaning validation, labor savings and turnaround benefits.

CDMO Application Scenario

In a contract development and manufacturing (CDMO) environment, technology transfer assessment is especially complex, because every client's process is different and the regulatory strategy (the applicant is the client, not the CDMO) must be aligned between both parties. When a CDMO implements SUT, it must confirm:

  • Whether the client's original IND/BLA/NDA filing already includes a statement on the use of SUT
  • Whether a supplement must be submitted if the process is switched from stainless steel to SUT
  • Whether the supplier can support the regulatory documentation required in Taiwan and the Asia-Pacific region (such as TW-FDA inspections)

Practice Questions

  1. Is your facility currently a pure stainless steel system or a hybrid system? If you were to introduce SUT, which process step would be the best pilot?
  2. The original text lists "Regional Support" as one of the evaluation factors for SUT. Does your main SUT supplier have local technical support in Taiwan?
3.1.1 Gap Assessment

Original Text

When considering technology transfer, a gap assessment should be performed to address inherent changes to match the desired state and to account for potential impacts. Table 3.1 provides an example gap assessment to match process capabilities with available SUT capabilities.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Essence of Gap Assessment

Gap assessment is a structured tool for answering the question: "What are the gaps between my current process capability and what SUT can provide?" Each gap requires:

  • An assessment of its potential impact on product quality or process safety
  • A specific mitigation strategy (for example: replace a sensor, add test points, revalidate the buffer formulation)

This tool serves two purposes at once: engineering decisions (which SUT to choose) and regulatory strategy (which supplementary data must be submitted).

Analogy

A gap assessment is like a "renovation requirements checklist" before moving house. You have a furniture list for the old home (your current process capability) and a room specification for the new home (SUT capability). Comparing the two shows which pieces of furniture do not fit (gaps), and whether to buy new ones (redevelopment) or modify them (adjust process parameters). Moving without this checklist means implementing SUT blindly.

Key Notes

The appearance of "Extractable Assessment" among the gap assessment items in the table emphasizes an important concept: changing the material means changing the contact surface. Even if the process parameters are identical, moving from stainless steel to a polymer contact surface introduces entirely new E&L risks, which must be assessed at the start of technology transfer and not only realized at the validation stage.

Practice Questions

  1. If you replace a stainless steel bioreactor step with a single-use bioreactor (SUBR), which CPPs in the gap assessment are likely to be most affected (hint: agitation, DO control, sampling method)?
  2. When buffer storage moves from stainless steel tanks to bioprocess bags, which new stability risks need to be assessed?
3.1.2 Planning and Scheduling

Original Text

Timelines for technology transfer vary depending on the complexity of the process, facility capabilities, staff familiarity with SUT, previous use of SUT, and regulatory compliance requirements. Many technical and quality related activities may be carried out in parallel. As with any new technology, operator training, and generation of SOPs should be incorporated into the transfer schedule.

Once a single-use product has been selected, the lead time for capital equipment is typically less than that of the stainless steel counterpart. If the single-use component is an off the shelf product, it may be readily available at the time of production. Typical delivery time (including plant integration and commissioning and qualification) can be up to six to eight months. However, most single-use components require customization to meet process needs. Sufficient time should be designated to develop user requirements and to generate and approve customized supplier drawings. Typical lead time for customized products is about twelve weeks for already ordered product and up to twenty weeks for a new design.

Supplier audits should take place after the selection of a single-use product to support accepting the supplier's manufacturing certificates and to leverage the E&L reports.

Since SUT are intended to be modular and generally do not require utilities for cleaning or sterilization, installation of the equipment can be accomplished quickly. Additional time may be required for very large equipment, such as support structures for vessels that do not fit through doors in the production areas.

Validation packages are typically available from the suppliers for the SUS hardware and software. Installation and qualification should be scheduled well in advance of equipment delivery.

Scheduling of production batches is process dependent. Considerations for the impact of SUT on scheduling include:

  • Single-use products generally do not require cleaning or sterilization; therefore, turnaround time for equipment is reduced significantly. For example, installation and removal of a single-use bioreactor is faster than cleaning and steaming a stainless steel bioreactor of comparable size.
  • The number of stainless steel tanks is often a limiting factor in media and buffer preparation. With SUT implementation, media and buffer may be prepared and held in totes provided there is sufficient storage space available.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Key Numbers in the SUT Timeline

The original text gives specific lead time references:

  • Off-the-shelf: available immediately, but most SUT requires customization and cannot be used as is
  • Customized, previously ordered products: about 12 weeks (3 months)
  • New design: up to 20 weeks (nearly 5 months)
  • Full implementation (including integration and validation): 6-8 months

These numbers mean that SUT procurement must start early in process development. Orders cannot wait until the validation plan has begun.

Analogy

An SUT implementation schedule is like building a house. Plumbing and electrical, masonry and carpentry can run in parallel (parallel technical activities), but the finishing cannot start before the foundation is laid (the supplier audit takes place after the equipment is selected). And if you only remember the custom door frames when you are building the walls, the lead time will certainly be too long.

Key Notes

The original text mentions that "large equipment may require wall removal to enter the production area". This is an often overlooked construction risk in SUT implementation. Confirm the following early in the plan:

  • Equipment crate dimensions vs. plant door widths and corridor widths
  • Cleanroom classification maintenance (contamination control during wall removal work)
  • Whether other production batches must be suspended

CDMO Application Scenario

When a CDMO implements SUT for a client, scheduling faces extra pressure: the client usually has timelines for IND-enabling studies. The recommended scheduling strategy is:

  • Start SUT selection once Phase I process development is complete, instead of waiting until the GMP manufacturing plan is confirmed
  • Establish a forecast order mechanism with the supplier to reserve capacity and shorten the lead time after the formal order
  • Include review of the supplier validation package as a prerequisite activity for IQ/OQ

Practice Questions

  1. The last time you introduced a new SUT consumable, how long did it actually take from selection to the first production batch? How does it compare with the reference timeline in this section?
  2. SUT eliminates CIP/SIP, but how much of the time saved is consumed by the new "assembly verification" steps? How can this trade-off be quantified?
3.2 Regulatory Compliance

Original Text

This section highlights key regulatory aspects for consideration in the implementation of SUT. Currently, there are no specific guidelines available to provide specific direction on the implementation and use of SUT. As such, early adopters of SUT have leveraged related regulations for supporting their product filings and for managing the quality of SUT being used and of the products manufactured using SUT. Such regulations generally define requirements for disposable medical devices, primary packaging of drug substances and drug products, and the control of materials used in drug manufacturing.

SUT can be incorporated in a wide range of applications within a manufacturing process. Due to this diversity of application, the risk to the product and process can range from low (e.g., use of single-use filter for filtration of buffer upstream of the process) to high (e.g., bags used in the filling of a parenteral drug). A large part of the responsibility falls on the drug manufacturer to ensure that single-use materials selected for the manufacturing process do not adversely impact the drug product. Regulators expect adequate data to ensure that the product contacting materials do not introduce contaminants into the product so as to alter the strength, identity, safety, quality, and purity.

3.2.1 Sources of Information

Table 3.2 provides sources of information for regulations, guidances, standards, and industry good practices, which can be used for setting quality expectations of single-use products and to support regulatory filings.

3.2.2 Data Requirements

While evaluating various regulatory data requirements, performing a science and risk-based assessment is a key activity. Considerations for evaluating the scope and extent of regulatory data needed include:

  • Characteristics of the product fluid in contact with the single-use product (physical state, organic solvents, surface active compounds, hydrophobic additives, etc.)
    • Solids in contact with single-use products have a lower risk potential compared to fluids
  • Stage where the single-use product is being used
    • Single-use products used in early process steps followed by purification have lower risk potential than those at final stages
  • Product contact time and condition
    • Tubing and connectors used for transfers have shorter contact time and lower risk potential compared to products used for holding or storing process fluids

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The "Borrowing" Strategy for SUT Regulation

The original text states candidly that, as of the publication of this guide, there is no regulatory guidance specific to SUT. The industry has therefore borrowed three existing regulatory frameworks:

  • Medical device regulations (ISO 10993-1): assess the biocompatibility of materials that contact the human body
  • Pharmaceutical packaging regulations (USP <661>, EMA guidelines): assess plastic materials that contact drug products
  • Pharmaceutical manufacturing control regulations (general GMP): ensure the integrity of material control in the manufacturing process

This "patchwork" approach requires drug manufacturers to exercise a high degree of scientific judgment. They must decide for themselves which regulations best apply to a specific SUT application.

Analogy

SUT regulatory compliance is like running a "health food restaurant" that combines a restaurant and a medical clinic. There are no dedicated regulations, so you must comply with both the health department's food safety rules (drug manufacturing GMP) and the requirements of the medical regulator (medical device standards), and then decide yourself which provisions apply to your particular service. Risk assessment capability is the key.

Key Notes: Risk Tiering Is the Key

The original text establishes a very important risk tiering logic:

  • Low risk: buffer filtration (upstream of the purification step)
  • Medium risk: product transfer in intermediate steps
  • High risk: final filling of a parenteral drug

The risk level directly determines the amount of regulatory data required. The closer to the final product, the longer the contact time and the larger the contact area, the more data is required. This is the core logic of a science and risk-based regulatory strategy.

CDMO Application Scenario

In an aseptic filling CDMO, the highest-risk application is direct filling of injectables. In that case the SUT (such as filling lines and bags) must meet the strictest requirements for biocompatibility, particulates, endotoxin and leachables. Practical recommendations:

  • Establish a risk classification matrix for SUT materials, managed by contact type, duration and dosage form
  • Require suppliers to provide complete evidence of compliance with USP <87>/<88> and USP <661>
  • For high-risk applications, retain independent in-house test data and do not rely entirely on supplier-provided data

Practice Questions

  1. In your facility, which SUT applications are high risk (direct contact with the final injectable)? What regulatory documentation currently supports these consumables?
  2. USP <665> and USP <661> both address the assessment of plastic materials. What is the main difference between them? Which is more applicable to single-use bags used in bioprocessing?
3.3 Leachables

Original Text

As discussed in Section 2.2, extractables and leachables (E&L) profiles are integral to qualifying and implementing single-use products. Leachables are the chemical species that migrate from or through a contact surface into a drug product or process stream during storage or normal use conditions. The supplier-provided extractables information can give preliminary indication of the potential chemical species that may impact the single-use product. The leachables evaluation should be done by the end-user, with a strong focus on the conditions of the therapeutic product or its precursor that is processed in the single-use product.

While the two terms E&L are often discussed together, they are executed at different times and by different entities. Therefore, this Guide correspondingly separates the two topics to represent the actual activities.

Some interactions between the dosage form and packaging/polymeric contact materials may give rise to E&L that can be readily identified, quantified, and evaluated for their impact on patient safety. However, leachables are often present at extremely low levels and the analytical methods should be capable of detecting and quantifying trace levels of these compounds. Other leachables may show up in stability studies, and may pose a risk to safety and efficacy via interactions with the formulation.

For example, a well-known industry case study is the issue arising from the common use of Irgafos 168® compounds (antioxidant additive) in single-use bioreactor films. Irgafos 168® had shown no cytotoxic effects in numerous studies. However, an oxidative derivative, Bis(2,3-di-tert-butylphenyl)phosphate (bDtBPP), was shown to have a cytotoxic effect on Chinese Hamster Ovary (CHO) cells once the bioprocess container was gamma irradiated. This case study shows the importance of risk analysis. A new film composition was later introduced with reduced concentration of Irgafos® 168 in the polymer formulation.

As an additional example, low levels of leachable iron may catalyze oxidation of a preservative and other excipients, leading to formation of protein-preservative adducts. Therefore, even if extractables by themselves do not pose a safety risk, safety and product quality should be assessed with appropriate risk assessment and risk mitigation strategies for leachables.

For a detailed approach, refer to the BioPhorum Operations Group (BPOG) Best Practices Guide for Evaluating Leachables Risk from Polymeric Single-Use Systems.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Terminology: The Essential Difference Between E&L

Extractables: chemicals deliberately "extracted" from a material under extreme or non-representative solvent conditions. They are generated by the supplier as worst-case reference data.

Leachables: chemicals that naturally "migrate" from a material into the process fluid or drug product under normal conditions of use (including storage and production). They are assessed by the end user and reflect the real risk.

Key logic: extractables are the "maximum list of what could leach", while leachables are "what actually leaches". The latter determines the patient safety risk.

Core Concept: Safety Concern Threshold

The core of leachables assessment is to determine whether the concentration of a chemical exceeds the Safety Concern Threshold (SCT). The SCT is the toxicological floor: below this value, no further safety testing is usually required. The main reference frameworks are:

  • BPOG guidance: provides best practice for leachables risk assessment of SUS used in bioprocessing
  • ICH Q3E (draft): safety thresholds for elemental impurities in drug products (partly applicable by analogy)
  • Threshold of Toxicological Concern (TTC): estimates safety based on daily intake

Analogy

Think of E&L as the "lead in tap water problem". Extractables are the pipe material analysis report (it tells you how much lead the pipe contains). Leachables are the analysis of a water sample taken from the tap (it tells you how much lead is actually in the water you drink). A material containing lead does not mean the lead in the water exceeds the limit, but it is a risk factor and the actual amount leached needs further assessment.

Key Notes: Lessons from the Irgafos 168® Case

This well-known case has three core lessons:

  • Gamma irradiation can change the properties of chemicals: the sterilization method (gamma irradiation) can convert a non-toxic substance into a toxic derivative (bDtBPP). E&L testing must be performed on the final state material, including all post-processing steps.
  • Cell lines differ in sensitivity: the same substance can have different toxicity to different CHO cell lines, so supplier data may not apply to your specific cell line.
  • Suppliers must actively improve their formulations: the case eventually led suppliers to reduce the amount of Irgafos® 168, showing that end-user feedback can drive material improvement.

CDMO Application Scenario

When taking over a client's bioprocess, the division of leachables assessment responsibility for a CDMO is typically as follows:

  • Supplier: provides extractables data and a preliminary toxicological assessment
  • CDMO: assesses the actual leachables risk based on the client's process conditions (pH, temperature, contact time, drug product characteristics)
  • Client: responsible for the final regulatory filing and for patient safety

Clearly defining the responsibility boundaries of these three parties in the contract is the key to avoiding regulatory disputes.

Practice Questions

  1. How does your facility currently manage the extractables data provided by SUT suppliers? Is there an internal procedure to assess its relevance to actual process conditions?
  2. If a new client's protein process is particularly sensitive to oxidation, which additional leachables indicators would you pay attention to when selecting SUT materials?
3.4 Validation

Original Text

Once the design phase is complete, validation of the single-use products can begin. An assessment should be performed to evaluate and quantify all potential risks associated with the single-use products and their introduction to the manufacturing process. A cross-functional team is recommended to conduct a thorough and robust assessment; the team members would be critical contributors to the Failure Mode and Effect Analysis (FMEA) protocols.

Knowledge of the process, single-use products, and contact solutions are essential for a thorough assessment. The following information should be gathered:

  • Product application and URS
  • Supplier product documentation (specifications and drawings)
  • Contact solutions
  • Proximity of the single-use product to the final process step
  • How the single-use product will be used
  • Ancillary equipment impact (tubing welders, pumps, etc.)
  • Sanitization/sterilization requirements

Supplier qualification, as well as analysis of leachables, would be nearly complete at this point in the SUT implementation project lifecycle.

3.4.1 Supplier Data Review

Suppliers typically provide a set of information including manufacturing, compendial, and additional data. End-users should evaluate the supplier provided information to ensure the data supports the intended use. An outline of supplier provided data:

  • Materials of construction, shelf life, and sterilization validation statement (per ANSI/AAMI/ISO 11137)
  • Biocompatibility test results per USP <87> and USP <88>
  • Mechanical properties test results (ASTM F2097-16, BPSA 2015 Guide)
  • Gas transmission properties
  • Physicochemical test results for plastic components (USP <661> or EP 3.1.3)
  • Statement of animal origin (TSE/BSE compliance)
  • Chemical compatibility charts
  • Endotoxin test results per USP <85> and EP 2.6.14
  • Particulate test results per USP <788>, EP 2.9.19, EP 2.9.20

3.4.2 In-House Testing

The extent of in-house testing depends on the application and availability of supplier and in-house data. It is acceptable to use supplier provided data and limit in-house testing based on risk assessment.

Solution Stability Testing: A solution stability study should be performed to ensure quality and safety of a solution in a single-use container. Key recommendations:

  • Compare all solutions to be held; identify representative solutions for testing. WFI should always be tested.
  • The ratio of surface area to volume is critical; bags should be filled to only half of full capacity for testing (worst case).
  • Bags should not be used at less than 50% of full capacity during production, as stability and E&L studies are typically carried out at 50% fill volume.
  • Sample at time zero and at 25%, 50%, 75%, 100% of expiry period. Evaluate pH, conductivity, microbial limits, color, and visible particulates.
  • For cell culture media: additionally perform small-scale cellular growth studies.

Container Integrity Testing: Media fill studies using Tryptic Soy Broth (TSB); test the largest bag size at full capacity (largest external surface area = worst case).

Filter Validation: If a filter is part of the single-use product, consider hydrophilicity, filtration area, pressure/flow limits, extractables, and water permeability. For sterilizing-grade filters: retention studies, integrity test determination, and compatibility studies must be conducted by the end-user.

Shipping Validation: Validate integrity through the entire shipment — identify shipping container, secure a carrier, and evaluate solution at start and end of shipment.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Three-Layer Framework of SUT Validation

SUT validation differs from conventional equipment validation: it combines materials science, process validation and packaging integrity testing. It can be understood in three layers:

  • Layer 1: Supplier documentation review (a prerequisite to IQ): confirm the adequacy and applicability of supplier documents
  • Layer 2: In-house testing: on-site verification for specific process conditions (solution stability, container integrity, filtration validation)
  • Layer 3: Process comparability: when converting from stainless steel to SUT, demonstrate that process results show no significant difference

Validation Framework

SUT validation activity flow:

1. Design Qualification (DQ)
   → Write URS → Select supplier → Freeze specifications

2. Installation Qualification (IQ)
   → Confirm correct installation of equipment
   → Supplier documentation review (documentation IQ)

3. Operational Qualification (OQ)
   → Functional testing (pressure, flow, sensors)
   → Filter integrity testing

4. Performance Qualification (PQ)
   → Solution stability studies
   → Container integrity testing (media fill)
   → Process comparability studies

5. Ongoing verification
   → Periodic monitoring of batch data
   → Periodic supplier audits

Key Notes: The Golden Rule of 50% Fill Volume

The original text stresses a practical detail that is often overlooked: stability and E&L studies are usually performed at 50% fill volume (worst case, with the highest surface area to volume ratio).

This means that if the fill volume of your production batch is below 50%, it falls outside the scope of the validation studies, and the risk assessment must be redone, or even additional testing performed. In commercial production, using a validated bag at below 50% fill volume is prohibited unless supported by additional data.

Analogy

Supplier documentation review for SUT is like the "vehicle history report" before buying a car: the test data provided by the supplier is equivalent to the safety test reports from the car manufacturer. But you still need to take a test drive yourself (in-house testing), especially if you will drive this car somewhere very unusual (a high-risk application such as direct filling of injectables). Supplier data is the starting point, not the end point.

CDMO Application Scenario

In a CDMO environment, SUT validation faces a special challenge: the same SUT may serve the processes of several different clients. The recommended approach is:

  • Establish a "platform validation" approach: complete the basic validation of a given SUT model once, covering all similar applications
  • Perform a differentiated risk assessment for the special conditions of each client process (special pH, protein concentration, organic solvents)
  • Filtration validation (especially for sterilizing-grade filters) must be performed by the end user and cannot be left entirely to the client or the supplier

Practice Questions

  1. In your SUT validation program, are the roles of IQ/OQ/PQ clearly defined? Is supplier documentation review done before IQ or as part of IQ?
  2. If the actual fill volume of a production batch is only 40% of the bag capacity, which risk management measures should you take according to the criteria in this chapter?
3.5 Supply Chain

Original Text
