A robust supply chain is composed of many aspects in the process of establishing a strong relationship with suppliers and sub-suppliers. This section aims to highlight recommendations for developing this relationship.

3.5.1 Quality Requirements

Confirm or Finalize Quality Agreement with Suppliers: The quality agreement is a key document to maintaining a robust supply chain for a process using SUT. This document should be drafted once a supplier is selected and the order for prototypes is being placed. The agreement typically covers quality, service, and financial topics. Negotiation should start during the prototype evaluation stage.

Confirm Audit Schedule: Auditing of suppliers is important for any organization dealing with SUT. Once the supplier is selected, a preliminary audit schedule should be defined. Periodic audits are recommended, with schedules being defined as soon as practical with all suppliers of single-use products.

3.5.2 Lead Times and Order Processing

Confirm or Agree Upon Lead Times for Individual Assemblies: During the design and selection of single-use assemblies, considerations should be made for using one design of the assembly in multiple locations. This can provide advantages in lead times and inventory levels. Shorter lead times often result if an established order schedule is worked out.

Confirm or Negotiate Pricing for Recurring Orders: At this point in SUT implementation, there should be defined prices for each assembly. Recurring order schedules can be defined along with associated pricing.

Develop and Agree on a Predefined Order Schedule: Once the production process is in operation, it is helpful to develop a long term (months or years) order schedule.

3.5.3 Complaint Management

Confirm Process for Handling Complaints and Investigations: Best practice is to define the process and expectations prior to a complaint occurrence. Confirm with the supplier the specific process and contact person for complaint entry.

Confirm Return and Crediting Process for Defective Products: Smooth return of defective products is key to timely investigations. Understand: how to initiate return, decontamination requirements prior to return, and when to expect credit.

Establish Expectations and Response Time: Investigations can take weeks or months. Understanding the expected communication during the investigation keeps all parties up to date.

CAPA Plan: Clarify typical CAPA implementation plans including timeline, in case an investigation results in a CAPA.

3.5.4 Security of Supply

Establish Inventory Requirements: The use rate and lead time combine to define the foundation for inventory levels. Having inventory at both the supplier and end-user warehouses helps retain commitment from both parties.

Agree on Change Notification Timelines: Change notifications throughout the supply chain impact the end-user. Critical factors to convey to the supplier include: what constitutes a notifiable change, whom to contact, and time needed to assess and implement a change.

Confirm Business Continuity Plans: A supplier business continuity plan should be reviewed, particularly for unique assemblies with long lead components, to ensure availability under supply chain disruption conditions.

3.5.5 Service, Maintenance, and Ongoing Training

Preventative Maintenance: A preventative maintenance service agreement for mechanical and electrical components (pumps, controls, etc.) of the SUSs should be established.

Training: Consider establishing periodic training programs for new staff or refreshers for existing staff. Suppliers can be an excellent resource to provide this training.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Special Nature of the SUT Supply Chain

SUT supply chain management is more complex than that for conventional equipment, for the following reasons:

  • Consumable nature: SUT is a continuously consumed item, not a one-time capital investment. A stock-out directly stops production.
  • Customization: most SUT components are customized for a specific application, so replacements cannot be bought urgently on the market at any time.
  • Sub-supplier complexity: SUT suppliers themselves depend on multiple tiers of raw material suppliers (polymers, connectors, sensors), and a disruption at any tier passes downstream.
  • Change notification: a change in the supplier's material formulation or process may require the end user to revalidate, which directly affects regulatory compliance.

Analogy

SUT supply chain management is like managing the purchase of disposable supplies for a hospital operating room. Scalpel blades, sutures and surgical gowns are all single-use, none can be missing, and many have custom specifications. The hospital cannot wait until the last box of blades is used before ordering. It must set safety stock, establish backup suppliers, sign service level agreements (SLAs) with suppliers and predefine emergency procedures for stock-outs. The supply chain logic for SUT is exactly the same.

Key Notes: Quality Agreement vs. Purchase Contract

The original text stresses that the quality agreement should start to be negotiated at the prototype evaluation stage, not after formal procurement. This is because a quality agreement involves several departments (QA, legal, finance) and the negotiation cycle is long. A common mistake is to wait until the production plan is confirmed before starting, which leads to production starting without a quality agreement.

The quality agreement should cover: batch release criteria, change control requirements, deviation notification timelines, CAPA timeline expectations and audit frequency.

Safety Stock Calculation

Basic safety stock formula:

Safety stock = (maximum usage/day - average usage/day) × longest lead time (days)

Example:
  Average usage: 10 bags/day
  Maximum usage (peak): 15 bags/day
  Supplier's longest lead time: 60 days (custom product)

  Safety stock = (15 - 10) × 60 = 300 bags

Reorder point =
  average usage × lead time + safety stock
  = 10 × 60 + 300 = 900 bags

CDMO Application Scenario

For a CDMO that takes on the processes of multiple clients, supply chain management has additional considerations:

  • Inventory allocation: different clients use the same SUT model, but lot numbers in batch records must be strictly segregated to avoid mixing lots across clients
  • Change notification management: once a supplier issues a material change notification, all affected clients must be informed and the revalidation plan coordinated
  • Business continuity plan: the COVID-19 pandemic revealed the real risk of SUT supply chain disruption. It is recommended to review the backup supplier list for critical SUT every year

Practice Questions

  1. Does your facility currently have a written quality agreement with your critical SUT suppliers? Does the agreement include change notification clauses and CAPA timeline requirements?
  2. How many days of inventory buffer do you currently hold for your critical SUT components? Is this number based on calculation or on "gut feeling"?
3.6 Training

Original Text

SUT has grown in both complexity of design and criticality of application in the past twenty years. The main advantages of SUT include improved flexibility and manufacturing throughput as well as reduced labor and risk for cross-contamination; these benefits can only be expressed if the end-users are properly trained. Therefore, operator single-use training is a key factor for successful implementation of SUT.

3.6.1 Impact of Single-Use Technology on Procedures

3.6.1.1 Comparison of Operations for Stainless Steel Systems and SUSs

For decades, operations have primarily been based on stainless steel equipment and piping. In general, the operators are well trained, with years of experience based on full integration of the equipment in the training programs. Even with the increasing level of automation available for SUT, stainless steel systems still allow for a higher degree of process automation and process control; these factors generally reduce the risk of human error.

While most stainless steel based facilities operate using process automation, SUSs involve many operations that are carried out manually by operators.

3.6.1.2 Increase in Manually Performed Steps

With SUT implementation, there may be changes associated with the facility and personnel and material flows. In general, there is an increase in manually performed steps. Considerations for increased or different steps include:

  • Receipt, inspection, storage, and transfer of SUSs to production areas
  • Inspection before use (checking irradiation label, damage during shipping, missing components, incorrect tubing engagement)
  • Connection and disconnection of single-use assemblies
  • Installation of single-use components in proper holders with accessories
  • Handling of flexible tubing and associated clamps/valves
  • Manual steps replacing CIP/SIP operations
  • Handling of connectors, disconnectors, welders, and sealing operations
  • Point-of-use leak testing
  • Handling of single-use sensors including calibration
  • Sampling steps from a variety of SUSs
  • Manipulation of aseptic transfer doors
  • Manual control of working pressure
  • Labeling of bioprocess bags after filling
  • Transfer of SUSs post-use for decontamination and disposal

Health and safety aspects of increased manual operations should be considered, especially regarding ergonomics and safety hazards due to tubing on or near the floor.

3.6.1.3 Failure Risk Related to SUT

Based on an industry survey, a primary risk related to SUT is breakage/leakage of the SUS leading to loss of production material and potential contamination. The distribution of leak locations from studies:

  • 61% — bag chamber
  • 18% — sampling manifold
  • 12% — connector
  • 9% — tubing

Leakages can be related to improper operations such as use of improper tools to open cardboard boxes, use of sharp objects in close proximity, rough handling during setting or filling, and use of improper transfer carts or containers. The leakage risk can be significantly reduced by ensuring proper usage through end-user awareness and operational training.

3.6.2 Training Considerations for SUT Implementation

A rigorous training qualification program should be established. Training is needed in three situations:

  • Initial training for new implementation of SUT
  • Recurring training refreshers all along the process lifecycle
  • Corrective action in case of malfunction or failure

SUT suppliers can provide specialized support. The main focus should be on hands-on sessions to allow operators to practice under conditions as close as possible to the actual process.

Once a core team has been fully trained, a mentorship program may be established. Mentors would also act as representatives for regular refreshers, design improvement requests, and process optimization proposals.

As with any equipment, insufficient operator training may result in:

  • Damage to SUSs during use → loss of product and time
  • Increased quality complaints and decreased confidence in SUT
  • Increased production costs due to higher consumption of SUSs
  • Slowdown of production and underutilization
  • Dissatisfaction due to operators being inadequately prepared

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Manual Operation Paradox of SUT

A common misconception about SUT is that "single-use consumables are simpler than stainless steel systems". The reality is the opposite: SUT greatly increases manual operating steps:

  • Stainless steel systems: automated CIP/SIP, fixed piping, highly automated
  • SUT: reassembly for every batch, connection verification, disassembly and disposal, with a large number of manual steps

The implication of this contradiction is that, although implementing SUT eliminates CIP/SIP validation work, it also introduces higher operator training needs and a higher risk of human error. This is a hidden cost that is often underestimated when evaluating SUT implementation.

Key Notes: 61% of Leaks Occur in the Bag Itself

The management implications of this statistic (61% of leaks in the bag chamber) are very specific:

  • Handling bioprocess bags by hand is the highest-risk behavior and requires the strictest training
  • Unpacking tools (no box cutters!), transport cart specifications and fill rate control are all controllable risks that can be managed through SOPs and training
  • Root cause analysis (RCA) of leaks should first examine compliance with bag handling procedures, rather than immediately pointing to supplier quality problems

Key Terminology: Special Considerations for Gowning in SUT Assembly

Gowning for SUT assembly has its own particularities:

  • SUT assembly is usually performed in a higher-grade cleanroom (especially the aseptic connection steps), and the gowning procedure must meet the requirements for the corresponding cleanroom grade
  • Thick gloves can affect handling precision (especially connector operation), so training must simulate operations in the actual gowned state
  • Fatigue effect while gowned: when operators perform complex SUT assembly procedures for long periods in full sterile gowning, the risk of human error rises significantly, so training must consider working time limits

Analogy

SUT training is like pilot training. A flight simulator (hands-on training with a mock-up) is worth far more than classroom lessons, because the "muscle memory" and "situational response" needed in real operation cannot be built by reading SOPs alone. And if you only practice in perfect weather in the simulator, you will be flustered in a real emergency (a bag leaking unexpectedly). Training must include abnormal situation simulation.

CDMO Application Scenario

In a CDMO environment, SUT training faces particular challenges:

  • Multiple clients and multiple SUT models: different clients use SUT from different suppliers, operators must master several assembly and operating procedures, and training is complex
  • Staff turnover: CDMO operator turnover is usually higher than in large pharmaceutical companies, and a mentorship program is an effective mechanism for preserving training knowledge
  • Training qualification: GMP requires that training be traceable and assessed. Evaluating the effectiveness of SUT operation training should include a practical demonstration, not only a written test

Practice Questions

  1. In your facility, does SUT operator training include "wrong demonstrations" (showing incorrect ways of operating)? Does this training method help raise operators' risk awareness?
  2. If an SUT leak deviation occurred in your facility, is "assessment of training adequacy" a standard investigation item in your RCA (root cause analysis) process?
3.6.3 Building an Effective Training Program for SUT

Original Text

3.6.3.1 General Considerations

A generic training model can be applied to SUT implementation with a clear focus on explaining the failure risks related to improper use and providing opportunities for operators to practice all manually performed steps. Training should be supported by supplier documentation (operating manual, maintenance recommendation, and spare part lists) and compliance with GMP requirements. Single-use training documents, including SOPs and work instructions, should follow Good Documentation Practices.

The trainer should consider:

  • Adapting the training session to the specific skill set of the staff in attendance
  • Understanding and accounting for the process flow and working environment
  • Adapting the handling procedures to constraints in the working environment

Useful tools for training include training videos and visual aids (pictures of correct vs. incorrect examples). Working instructions, as supplements to SOPs, contain step-by-step procedures with pictures and should be easily accessible at the point of use.

Key concepts applicable to all SUT training:

  • Provide hands-on training and ensure sufficient skills and comfort level
  • Inform operators of possible things that could go wrong
  • Forecast regular training refreshers and process review
  • Apply root cause analysis in case of failure
  • Monitor impact of corrective actions

A training program should start with a "watch it" step followed by a "do it" step, supported by an evaluation of training performance.

3.6.3.2 Timing for Training

Training qualification should be introduced as early as possible (during testing and validation phases) as an integral part of the SUT implementation project. Involving operators from the beginning helps enhance interest and reinforce confidence.

3.6.3.3 Specify Content According to Complexity and Audience

Training content and time required depend on:

  • Process scale and working environment (e.g., handling a 3,000 L storage bag is more complicated than handling a 50 L bag; working in higher classification areas requires additional time and precautions)
  • Complexity of the SUSs (bioreactors and automated systems require more in-depth training)
  • Stakeholder role:
    • Supervisors and operators — performing daily work
    • Supply chain personnel — procurement, warehouse, and disposal
    • Quality control personnel — performing inspections
    • Maintenance personnel
  • Purpose: refresher/corrective (focus on specific key points) vs. new SUT training (system features and instructions for use)

Formal training, in compliance with GMP requirements, should be implemented once the SUSs are delivered, installed, and qualified. Suggested timing for formal training qualification:

  • For hardware (sealers, welders, aseptic transfer doors): training can be planned just after the SAT; maintenance personnel should be included
  • For single-use assemblies (bags, connectors, tubing): formal training should follow qualification of the assembly

As highlighted, it is beneficial to implement hands-on training in the early phase before final implementation to ease SUT adoption. However, formal GMP training should be implemented once the SUSs are delivered, installed, and qualified. A proposed three-step training sequence: Observation → Practice → Evaluation (for new implementation) or Observation of current practice → Correction → Re-evaluation (for corrective training).

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The "Three Whens, Three Whos, Three Whys" Framework for SUT Training

Three timings (When):

  • Before technology transfer: build basic awareness and perception
  • During technology transfer and validation: operating skills training
  • Ongoing production after validation: troubleshooting and continuous improvement

Three audiences (Who): operators, QC personnel and maintenance personnel. Each group needs training of different depth and emphasis.

Three situations (Why): new implementation, periodic refresher and corrective training. The purposes differ, so the training design should differ too.

Key Terminology: Training Qualification

Training qualification is a formal GMP requirement used to demonstrate that an operator is competent to perform a specific operation. It usually includes:

  • Theoretical assessment: a written or oral test to confirm mastery at the knowledge level
  • Practical demonstration: a qualified assessor observes the operator performing the operation, to confirm competence at the skill level
  • Periodic requalification: for high-risk SUT operations, reassessment every 1-2 years is recommended

In FDA inspections or EU GMP inspections, "training records" are always examined. Incomplete training records can lead to a 483 observation or a warning letter.

Key Notes: The "Watch It → Do It → Evaluate" Three-Step Model

The training model recommended in the original text is consistent with psychological research:

  • Watch It (demonstration and observation): observe a correct demonstration, including a demonstration of the consequences of errors
  • Do It (hands-on practice): practice under conditions close to the real ones (including the gowned state), and ask the supplier to provide training consumables
  • Evaluate (skills assessment): an assessor observes and records, to confirm that the operator meets the minimum competence standard

The starting point of "corrective training" is to observe first the operator's existing behavior, because the problem may be a specific bad habit rather than a general lack of knowledge. Targeted correction is more effective than retraining from scratch.

Analogy

The tiered logic of SUT training (by audience and complexity) is like the course categories of a driving school. Parallel parking (simple SUT: transfer tubing sets) and highway driving (complex SUT: a 3000 L bioreactor) need completely different depths of training. Maintenance personnel learn "changing a tire", operators learn "driving", and QC personnel learn "traffic rules and vehicle inspection standards". It is the same car, but different training content.

CDMO Application Scenario

To build an efficient SUT training program in a CDMO, the following practices are recommended:

  • Establish an SUT training matrix: for each SUT model × each operator role, clearly define the required training modules and retraining intervals
  • Establish a training cooperation agreement with suppliers: add a clause for "periodic operator training support" to the supply contract, using the supplier's expertise as an external training resource
  • Place visual working instructions at the point of operation: especially for operators whose hand dexterity is limited after gowning, illustrated on-site operation cards are more effective than recalling SOPs from memory
  • Abnormal situation simulation drills: regularly rehearse the "bag leak emergency procedure" to ensure operators do not panic when a real event occurs

Practice Questions

  1. Does your facility's current SUT training program distinguish "initial training for new employees", "periodic retraining" and "corrective training"? Are the assessment methods different for the three?
  2. "Involve operators before technology transfer": at what stage did operators in your facility start to be involved in the last SUT implementation? What specific benefits or challenges did early involvement bring?

Chapter Summary

  • Technology transfer: SUT implementation is mostly hybrid; a gap assessment and a schedule must be completed (lead time for customized products 12-20 weeks, full implementation 6-8 months); the supplier audit should start immediately after selection. The assessment must cover material compatibility, process requirements, facility constraints, regulatory requirements and scale-up capability.
  • Regulatory compliance: there is currently no SUT-specific regulation, and the industry borrows medical device regulations (ISO 10993-1), pharmaceutical packaging regulations (USP <661>/<665>) and general GMP. Risk tiering is the core logic: low risk (upstream filtration) → high risk (final injectable filling), and the higher the risk, the more regulatory data is required. USP <87>/<88> is the baseline threshold for biocompatibility.
  • Leachables: leachables are chemicals that migrate from the material into the product under normal conditions of use, and are assessed by the end user. Testing must be done on final state material (including after gamma irradiation). The Irgafos 168® case warns that the sterilization process can convert a non-toxic substance into a toxic derivative, and that different cell lines differ in sensitivity. The Safety Concern Threshold (SCT) is the key criterion for deciding whether further safety assessment is needed.
  • Validation: three-layer framework: supplier documentation review (prerequisite to IQ) → in-house testing (solution stability, container integrity, filtration validation) → process comparability study. Golden rule: stability and E&L studies are performed at 50% fill volume (worst case), and production must not go below this ratio. Testing of sterilizing-grade filters in filtration validation must be performed by the end user.
  • Supply chain: the quality agreement should be negotiated from the prototype evaluation stage; safety stock calculation must consider the long lead time of customized products (up to 60 days or more); change notification management is key to GMP compliance, because a change in the supplier's material formulation may trigger revalidation. A business continuity plan is especially important for customized components with no alternative.
  • Training: SUT increases manual operating steps, and while it eliminates CIP/SIP it introduces a higher risk of human error. 61% of leaks occur in the bag chamber, and the root cause lies in operator behavior. An effective SUT training program has three timings (before/during/after transfer), three audiences (operators/QC/maintenance) and three purposes (new implementation/refresher/corrective), and uses the "Watch It → Do It → Evaluate" model. Training qualification must include a practical demonstration and is always examined in GMP inspections.
3

Section 3: 4. Program Management

p103-p120

ISPE Good Practice Guide: Single-Use Technology | p103 - p120

Learning Objectives

  • Master the full lifecycle of a SUT implementation plan, from supplier assessment and supply security confirmation through to putting the system into production
  • Understand the five-step SUT risk management framework and use the risk score formula (A × B × C × D) to perform a quantitative risk assessment of process contact materials (PCM)
  • Become familiar with the 15 key qualification attributes and the in-house validation tests required at low, medium and high risk levels
  • Understand the typical types of SUT change (maintenance/improvement vs. major disruptive) and how to respond to them, and establish a standard mechanism for communicating supply chain changes
  • Understand the structure and logic of a SUT implementation project schedule, including the use of Gantt charts, critical path identification, and how parallel tasks compress the overall implementation timeline
4.1 Implementation of Single-Use Technology

Original Text

This chapter is aimed at providing guidance on the implementation of SUT in a manufacturing operation. The topics highlighted cover the project phases from when a decision is made to implement SUT to when the SUT is operational in a manufacturing process.

While implementation methods applied to SUT are similar to those of stainless steel systems, there are characteristics of SUT that require emphasis on certain aspects of the implementation process. These include:

  • Strong collaboration with suppliers
  • Risk management and change management throughout the process
  • Flexible schedules to handle the development of single-use assemblies
  • Interface/connection of SUT with stainless steel technology

This chapter is primarily focused on the risk management, change management, and the implementation schedules. As SUT is a relatively new technology, there is a fair amount of development that often arises during SUT projects. This development can result in changes, and attention to risk management and schedules are important to a smooth implementation.

4.1.1 Assessing Supplier Capabilities

End-users should assess suppliers for their capability to supply the required single-use products. The dependence on suppliers, particularly with regard to sourcing, should be properly handled and managed. Primary SUT suppliers should define a sourcing strategy to ensure business continuity, e.g., from film suppliers or other unique components, to ensure the needs of their end-users can be fulfilled.

The general checklist below represents a subset of topics critical when assessing supplier capabilities:

  • Complete evaluation of SUT suppliers by the end-user (purchasing) – the choice of a supplier may be defined by criteria such as:
    • Financial aspects
    • Technical knowledge and support
    • Flexibility/adaptability
    • Quality
    • End-user's process criticality
    • Qualification and validation of their own supply chain (i.e., raw materials suppliers)
  • Assessment of the risks to supply delays
  • Implementation of quality audit plans for all suppliers, including auditing of their change management system
  • Evaluation of automation integration (automation portion of the SUS into the end-user automation system)
  • Evaluation of flexibility of the supplier to work with the end-user to make their SUS easy to maintain/calibrate

End-users should source at least two suppliers that meet the implementation team's needs. Having dual-sourced single-use products in the process can provide buffer against vendor supply issues that may impact the manufacturing process.

4.1.2 Confirming Supply Security

Supply security is another key factor during implementation of SUT. End-users should request details from single-use product suppliers about their supply security process. Suppliers should confirm the strength of such systems by demonstrating it is reliable, robust and secured.

The supply security process should be managed by all stakeholders. Responsibilities are shared during the supply process, which should be a collaborative effort.

During the implementation and management of supply security, key factors include:

  • For SUT suppliers:
    • Anticipation of sales and the management of storage area to be compliant with requirements from the end-user
    • Providing warranty on delivery times by targeting an elimination or reduction of delay
    • Anticipation of procurement and raw material (especially resin) supplying
    • Planning sufficient reserve manufacturing capacity
    • Implementing quality procedures to improve single-use components and equipment quality, supply chain flexibility, and manufacturing productivity
  • For SUT end-users:
    • Implementing quality procedures for supplier facility audits
    • Reducing the risk of failure in the supply security chain by using a dual sourcing strategy
    • Managing sourcing, sales orders, and stakeholders commissioning plans

Tutorial Commentary

Core concept: key differences between SUT and stainless steel system implementation

The framework for implementing SUT is similar to that for conventional stainless steel systems, but there are four special areas of focus:

  • Stronger supplier collaboration: SUT is highly customized, so the design requires close cooperation from the supplier
  • Risk and change management throughout: a change in any link of material, design or process can affect compliance
  • Flexible scheduling: development lead times for single-use components are hard to predict precisely, so the plan must include buffers
  • Interface integration challenges: the connection points between SUT and stainless steel equipment are areas of high leakage risk and high audit attention

Key elements of supplier assessment

When assessing a supplier, do not look only at "can they supply now"; also assess:

  • Financial stability: if the supplier runs into financial trouble, the whole supply chain is interrupted
  • Technical support capability: troubleshooting SUT problems often requires the supplier's engineers on site
  • Validation of its own supply chain: the supplier's raw material suppliers (such as the film manufacturer) must also be controlled
  • Change management system: any change the supplier makes to materials or processes must be notified to the end user in good time

Analogy: dual sourcing

Dual sourcing is like the twin-engine design of an aircraft: you may normally use only one engine, but the presence of the other ensures flight safety. At an aseptic filling CDMO, if a critical single-use bag has only one supplier, then once that supplier cannot deliver (typhoon, raw material shortage, quality problem), the whole production line stops. Dual sourcing is the core insurance mechanism for supply security.

Key point: shared responsibility for supply security

The source text stresses that supply security is "managed jointly by all stakeholders." This means:

  • Suppliers need to forecast demand, stock raw materials and reserve capacity
  • End users need to audit supplier facilities regularly and manage order planning
  • Both sides need to define communication channels and notification mechanisms in advance

Supply security is not about "dealing with problems when they occur" but about building resilience systematically.

CDMO application

At an aseptic filling CDMO, single-use bioreactor bags, filling tubing and filter assemblies are usually put out to bid among 2-3 suppliers. Besides price, the purchasing department should confirm when selecting a supplier:

  • Whether the supplier has local technical support in Taiwan/Asia-Pacific (to shorten problem response time)
  • Whether the key film raw material comes from a single source (geopolitical risk)
  • Whether the supplier's change management SOP meets the notification requirements of the client (the pharmaceutical company), which usually call for 6-12 months' advance notice

Practice Questions

  1. Which critical single-use components at your site are still single-sourced? If that supplier stopped delivering, how many weeks could you last at most?
  2. Does your supplier audit program include an assessment of the "supplier's change management system"? When was the last time a problem was found?
4.2 Risk Management

Original Text

New technology can carry risks that arise from many sources. While SUT is becoming established in the pharmaceutical manufacturing environment, it is still evolving with improvements routinely incorporated into the equipment and operations. These improvements often trigger changes and associated risks that need to be minimized. The types of risk that can arise during the implementation of SUT include:

  • Material compatibility
  • Availability of products
  • Changes in source materials
  • Leaks or performance issues
  • Inventory fluctuations

Risk management should be applied throughout the implementation of SUT. Risk assessments should be conducted at the beginning and at multiple points of the implementation program. At a minimum, an FMEA should be done at the beginning of the implementation that addresses at least the potential risks listed above.

4.2.1 Risk Assessment Approach

The risk assessment model presented in this Guide involves the calculation of a risk score representing the potential risk of the Process Contact Material (PCM) that the SUS consists of. For a given risk category, additional required in-house qualification should be performed, or leveraged from existing data, and documented.

The steps for the risk assessment model are:

  1. Step 1: Identify user requirements for the PCM
  2. Step 2: Obtain PCM validation data package from the supplier
  3. Step 3: Perform risk assessment
  4. Step 4: Execute in-house qualification studies required based on the risk score
  5. Step 5: Perform risk mitigation

There are fifteen critical qualification attributes to consider (full description in Chapter 8, Appendix 4):

  • Attribute 1: Biocompatibility Testing
  • Attribute 2: Mechanical Properties
  • Attribute 3: Gas Transmission Properties
  • Attribute 4: Compendial Physicochemical Testing
  • Attribute 5: Animal Origin Control
  • Attribute 6: Total Organic Carbon (TOC) Analysis
  • Attribute 7: pH and Conductivity
  • Attribute 8: Extractables and Leachables (E&L)
  • Attribute 9: Chemical Compatibility
  • Attribute 10: Protein Adsorption Studies
  • Attribute 11: Endotoxin Testing
  • Attribute 12: Sterilization (Irradiation)
  • Attribute 13: Container Closure Integrity
  • Attribute 14: Particulate Testing
  • Attribute 15: Calibration of Embedded Instrumentation

Additionally, other requirements should be taken into consideration, including: establishing expiration dates for PCM, determination of any pre-use testing, and determination of any post-use testing (e.g., vent filter integrity post-use where regulatory requirements exist).

Tutorial Commentary

Core concept: the five sources of SUT risk

SUT risks are more varied than those of conventional stainless steel equipment, and arise mainly from:

  • Material compatibility: polymer materials may react with the drug product, solvents or cleaning agents
  • Product availability: a supplier discontinuing a product or changing a model creates a risk of supply interruption
  • Raw material changes: a change in film formulation may alter the extractables profile
  • Leakage/performance problems: welds and connectors are where single-use components fail most easily
  • Inventory fluctuation: single-use components have expiry dates, so both overstocking and understocking are risks

Key term: PCM (process contact material)

Process Contact Material (PCM) refers to all single-use materials in direct contact with the drug product, including bag film, tubing, filter membranes and connectors. Qualification of PCM is the core work of SUT compliance: you must demonstrate that these materials will not affect product quality or patient safety under the intended conditions of use.

The five risk assessment steps explained

These five steps form a closed loop:

  • Step 1 (User requirements): confirm the type of drug product, contact conditions and sterilization method
  • Step 2 (Supplier data): collect the supplier's extractables, compatibility and biocompatibility test reports
  • Step 3 (Risk assessment): calculate the risk score and decide whether additional in-house validation is needed
  • Step 4 (In-house validation): perform the tests that correspond to the risk level
  • Step 5 (Risk mitigation): define mitigation measures such as incoming controls and usage restrictions

Key point: priority among the 15 qualification attributes

Not all 15 attributes have to be done; the risk score determines which are needed. Almost every high-risk application needs the three most important ones:

  • Attribute 8 (E&L): extractables and leachables, which receive the most regulatory attention
  • Attribute 1 (Biocompatibility): biocompatibility, the bottom line for patient safety
  • Attribute 13 (Container Closure Integrity): container closure integrity, the core of sterility assurance

CDMO application: how to plan PCM validation

Because a CDMO serves multiple clients at once, the PCM validation strategy needs to be designed with care:

  • Build a PCM Component Library so that validated components can be reused across projects
  • Complete full validation first for high-risk, high-usage components (such as BioProcess bags)
  • Use a QbD bracketing approach, with data from high-risk conditions covering lower-risk applications, to reduce repeated testing

Practice Questions

  1. Does your site have a PCM component library? How many validated single-use components does it currently hold? Which of the most commonly used components have not yet been validated?
  2. For E&L testing, does your site rely on supplier data or run its own in-house tests? What criteria do you base that decision on?
4.2.2 Risk Assessment – Calculation of Risk Score

Original Text

The purpose of the risk assessment is to determine the extent of in-house qualification required according to a calculated risk score for each PCM. The risk assessment model takes into account the potential risk to product quality and patient safety.

A risk assessment model may be formulated to calculate the risk score as follows:

Risk Score = A × B × C × D

Where:

  • A = Route of administration
  • B = Proximity to final product
  • C = Contact time
  • D = Surface area to volume ratio

For each risk factor, the score can be classified as high (risk score = 10), medium (risk score = 5), or low (risk score = 1).

Risk Factor A: Route of Administration

Risk Factor B: Proximity to Final Product

Risk Factor C: Contact Time

Risk Factor D: Surface Area to Volume Ratio

Determination of Final Risk Level

  • Low: calculated risk score ≤ 1,000
  • Medium: calculated risk score between 1,001 and 4,999
  • High: calculated risk score ≥ 5,000

Tutorial Commentary

The formula explained: Risk Score = A × B × C × D

Because this is a product formula, the four factors are multiplied (not added), so any single factor equal to 1 (low risk) lowers the total score significantly:

Maximum score: 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 = 10,000 (far above the high-risk threshold of 5,000)
Minimum score: 1 × 1 × 1 × 1 = 1 (very low risk)
Typical aseptic filling example: 10 × 10 × 1 × 5 = 500 (low risk)
    A = injectable (10), B = final filling (10),
    C = contact time <48 hr (1), D = medium SA/V (5)

Note: even if the drug product is an injectable (A = 10) and the step is final filling (B = 10), if the contact time is short (C = 1) and SA/V is small (D = 1), the final score = 100, which is low risk.

An intuitive understanding of the four risk factors

  • A (route of administration): an injectable goes straight into the bloodstream and any impurity cannot be metabolized away by the body, so the risk is highest
  • B (proximity to final product): the closer to the final filling step, the fewer opportunities downstream processing has to remove contamination
  • C (contact time): static contact over a long period (such as a storage bag held for several days) carries much higher risk than a brief flow through tubing
  • D (surface area/volume ratio): using a large bag for a small batch dilutes the leachable concentration, so the smaller the batch, the higher the relative risk

Analogy: the intuition behind risk calculation

Imagine assessing the risk of a plastic cup affecting a cup of coffee:

  • A (do you drink it or apply it to your skin?) → drink it = high risk
  • B (is it the final drink or a raw ingredient?) → ready-to-drink coffee = high risk
  • C (how long does the coffee sit in the cup?) → several days = high risk; throw the cup away right after drinking = low risk
  • D (cup size vs. amount of coffee?) → a large cup holding one sip of coffee = high risk

The risk assessment logic for SUT is exactly the same: multiply the four dimensions to obtain the overall risk level.

Key point: what the risk score thresholds mean

The three thresholds (low ≤1,000; medium 1,001-4,999; high ≥5,000) look arbitrary, but they have an engineering rationale:

  • Low risk (≤1,000): only basic validation (leak, tear, sterility, endotoxin, integrity tests) is needed
  • Medium risk (1,001-4,999): adsorption testing and particulate (shedding) testing are additionally needed
  • High risk (≥5,000): pH change, leachables testing and particulate assessment are additionally needed

The thresholds are designed to concentrate resources on the materials that are truly high risk, rather than testing all materials to the same degree.

CDMO application: typical risk assessments for aseptic filling

Risk calculations for common scenarios at an aseptic filling CDMO:

  • Pre-filling holding bag (24 hr): A = 10, B = 10, C = 1, D = 5 → score = 500 (low)
  • Buffer preparation bag (72 hr): A = 10, B = 1, C = 5, D = 5 → score = 250 (low)
  • Long-term storage bag for biologic intermediate (10 days): A = 10, B = 5, C = 10, D = 10 → score = 5,000 (high!)

A long-term storage bag is a high-risk scenario and requires full in-house validation, including E&L testing.

Practice Questions

  1. Take a single-use bag actually used at your site, apply the A × B × C × D formula to calculate its risk score, and decide which additional in-house validation tests are needed.
  2. For a medium-risk material (score 1,500), which factor would you work on first to lower the risk score, and why?
4.2.3–4.2.4 In-House Qualification & Risk Mitigation

Original Text

4.2.3 Execute In-House Qualification

Based on the final risk level of the PCM, the required in-house qualification activities can be determined.

Regardless of final risk level, in-house qualification requirements should always include:

  • Leak/pressure/crack verification
  • Tear evaluation (for bags)
  • Sterility evaluation (for sterile-supplied PCM)
  • Endotoxin evaluation (for sterile-supplied PCM)
  • Integrity testing (for 0.2 µm filters, whether sterilizing or for bioburden reduction)

Where final risk level is medium (1,001–4,999), additional requirements include:

  • Sorption testing
  • Spallation testing (for peristaltic pump tubing)

Where final risk level is high (≥5,000), additional requirements include:

  • pH change evaluation
  • Leachables testing
  • Particulate evaluation

Approaches to reducing the amount of testing include:

  • Utilizing existing data from suppliers and in-house data – existing data can be utilized without the need to perform additional work; careful evaluation is necessary to ensure it supports the intended use. Only formal and official documents issued by the supplier should be admissible.
  • QbD approach – using data for a higher risk PCM to qualify the same PCM in the same or lower risk categories, provided:
    • Same grade of resin and material of construction
    • Same supplier/manufacturer
    • Used for the same/similar drug substance, drug product, or excipient
  • Paper exercise – for certain low risk applications, in-house qualification studies can potentially be satisfied with a paper exercise.

4.2.4 Risk Mitigation

Following qualification activities, incoming requirements should minimally include:

  • Confirmation of materials (correct PCM and site of manufacture)
  • Confirmation of sterility status, if applicable
  • Review of Certificate of Analysis (CoA) for any prescribed supplier testing and certification
  • Confirmation of packaging integrity or packaging configuration

If the material does not meet the minimum requirements, remediation options include:

  • Selecting another supplier
  • Requesting the supplier to generate the test data
  • Performing in-house qualification
  • Implementing a risk mitigation step in the process, if possible

Tutorial Commentary

The three-tier structure of in-house validation

ISPE SUT establishes a clear hierarchy of validation:

  • Base tier (required at all risk levels): leak, tear, sterility, endotoxin and filter integrity. These are the absolute bottom line for sterile manufacturing.
  • Medium-risk additional tier: adsorption testing (drug components adsorbed by the material) plus particulate (shedding) testing (particles generated by wear of peristaltic pump tubing)
  • High-risk additional tier: pH change, leachables and particulates. These are the core tests directly tied to regulatory expectations.

Three strategies to reduce the amount of testing

Not every PCM needs "all tests from scratch"; three strategies can greatly reduce repeated work:

  • Reference existing supplier data: the most time-saving, but you must strictly confirm the data's applicability (same process, same conditions, formal official documents)
  • QbD bracketing approach: use data from the harshest conditions to cover milder application scenarios, similar to "if you have passed the hardest exam, you do not need to take the easy one"
  • Paper exercise: for low-risk materials, a documented rationale may be enough, with no actual testing

Analogy: incoming control is the last line of defense

Incoming control of PCM is like airport security. However strict the baggage check was at the departure airport, you still go through security again on arrival. Even when the supplier's validation data are complete, each delivery must still be checked for correct material (CoA check), intact packaging (no damage or contamination), and maintained sterility (lot number, expiry). This is the last gate of risk mitigation and cannot be skipped.

Key point: a CoA is not validation

The CoA (certificate of analysis) provided by the supplier only proves that "this lot meets the supplier's release specifications"; it does not mean the product is suitable for your particular process application. The CoA review in incoming control should confirm that:

  • Every specified test item has a result and meets the criteria
  • The part number, lot number and manufacturing site on the CoA match the purchase order
  • Any known lot-to-lot variation (such as film lots) is specifically tracked

CDMO application: when a material does not meet requirements

In CDMO practice, when PCM validation fails or an incoming lot is rejected, the four remedial options in their actual order of priority are:

  1. Ask the supplier to supply the missing tests (most common): the supplier provides the missing test data
  2. Perform validation in-house: high cost but fast, suitable for urgent batch needs
  3. Add a mitigation step to the process: for example, an additional filtration step to reduce the leachables risk
  4. Change supplier (last resort): takes the longest, but if the material has a fundamental flaw this is the only solution

Practice Questions

  1. What are the current incoming control standards for single-use components at your site? Which items does your CoA review checklist include?
  2. If a supplier claims to use the "QbD bracketing approach" to cover your low-risk application with high-risk data, how would you evaluate whether that claim is reasonable?
4.3 Change Management

Original Text

Change management is an important element of a pharmaceutical manufacturing quality system. With the rapid development of SUT, implementation of a structured change control process within a change management system allows for smooth operations and predictable compliance with regulatory requirements.

Changes may come from product improvements, availability of new types of products, or phasing out of products as they become obsolete due to improved products.

4.3.1 Typical Changes Associated with SUT Operations

Typical changes associated with SUT operations can be categorized as maintenance/improvement changes and major disruptive changes:

  • Maintenance/improvement changes:
    • Operational improvements
    • Change in drawing format or documentation
    • Storage location
    • Transit route
  • Major disruptive changes:
    • Material of construction or formulation
    • Obsolete component
    • Manufacturing methods (process parameter or equipment)
    • Manufacturing location
    • Dimensions of component
    • Supplier of single-use product
    • Sterilization supplier/location

Change management with SUT operations can be a complex process due to the number of stakeholders (e.g., compounders, manufacturers, assemblers) and raw materials involved in the supply chain. Primary suppliers should ensure total ownership of delivered single-use assemblies and provide full transparency. The following aspects should be addressed:

  • Supplier's definition of change
  • Supplier's qualification process for alternative raw materials sources (e.g., resins, additives, films)
  • Supplier's change management process, including:
    • Customer change notification and agreement on lead times for such notifications
    • Mitigation activities, such as inventory of raw materials

A robust supply chain communications process is a major requirement for effective change management. Enhanced by:

  • Quality agreement
  • Periodic audits
  • Predefined and direct communication channels

4.3.2 Typical Methods/Activities Applied in Change Management

The level of evaluation depends on change criticality and is related to the intended use and processing conditions (risk-based approach) of the SUS. The following methods/activities are typically performed to support the change evaluation step:

  • E&L tests
  • Functional tests
  • Robustness tests
  • Mechanical strength evaluation
  • Interaction with other components
  • Shelf life

Changes can also be managed by qualifying alternative sourcing for single-use products. PDA Technical Report 66 presents the concept of single-use component interchangeability, dependent on intended function, processing conditions, and fit-for-purpose evaluation.

Tutorial Commentary

Core concept: the complexity of SUT change management

SUT change management is more complex than for stainless steel equipment because the supply chain has many layers:

End user → single-use component supplier → film manufacturer → resin supplier → raw material source

A change at any layer may affect the properties of the final product-contact material. A "small change" by the supplier can mean the end user needs to revalidate, or even resubmit a regulatory filing.

Key term: Change Notification

Change Notification is the formal mechanism by which a supplier informs the end user of an upcoming change to materials or processes. A quality agreement usually specifies:

  • Major changes (such as film material or sterilization provider): at least 6-12 months' advance notice
  • Minor changes (such as document format or warehouse location): 30-90 days' advance notice is sufficient

Notice without enough lead time puts the end user under urgent revalidation pressure and can even affect commercial batch production.

Analogy: maintenance vs. disruptive changes

Imagine you are a restaurant owner (the end user):

  • Maintenance change: the supplier changes the color of the outer packaging or moves to a new warehouse address. You may need to update your receiving documents, but food quality is unaffected.
  • Major disruptive change: the supplier switches its vegetables from organic to conventional farming. This directly affects the "organic vegetables" claim on your menu, so you must reassess and may need to change supplier.

A SUT "material formulation change" is equivalent to the latter and must trigger the full change control process.

Key point: the quality agreement is the foundation of change management

Effective SUT change management depends on three mechanisms working together:

  • Quality Agreement: clearly defines which changes require notification, the notification timing, and the responsibilities of both parties
  • Periodic audits: verify that the supplier actually complies with its notification obligations, and detect unreported changes early
  • Predefined communication channels: when a change occurs, who notifies whom and in what format; this cannot be handled with "just a phone call"

A supplier relationship without a quality agreement is like driving without a seat belt: most of the time nothing happens, but when something goes wrong it is serious.

CDMO application: special challenges in change management

Change management is even more complex for a CDMO, because the same single-use component may be used in different drug projects for several clients at once. A single "material change notification" from a supplier may require you to:

  • Assess the impact on each client (different drug products, different regulatory filings)
  • Trigger multiple change control records
  • Possibly notify each client (depending on the client's regulatory filing status)
  • Coordinate the testing and release schedules of different clients

This is why a CDMO's PCM component library and standardized validation procedures are especially important: validate once, share across multiple projects.

Practice Questions

  1. Has your site signed a quality agreement with its main single-use component suppliers? Are the "change notification" requirements in the agreement detailed enough?
  2. In the past three years, has your site encountered a major supplier change without advance notice? If so, what were the consequences, and how can it be prevented?
4.4 Project Schedules

Original Text

This section provides example schedules that capture important tasks in the various phases of SUT implementation. The schedules are structured to allow for selected tasks to be accomplished in parallel while optimizing the use of time and resources. Running activities in parallel reduces the overall project time; however, this may cause spikes in the resources required and usually decreases the efficiency in the use of resources.

The reader should use these example schedules to develop customized schedules to fit the specific project requirements.

4.4.1 SUT Implementation into Operations with Qualified Components

The example schedule (Figure 4.1) targets the overall implementation tasks, based on the following conditions which allow for fast SUT implementation:

  • All components used in the assemblies are qualified to be used in the process
  • E&L information has been developed and is available for the specific process fluid

Note: Even if all components are qualified, the complete assembly may be new in terms of component engagement or dimensions (e.g., tubing length or diameter). It should be tested through technical checks with samples and prototype testing.

4.4.2 SUT Implementation into Operations with Components Needing Qualification

These schedules target the overall implementation tasks when one or more components need to be qualified. These schedules also incorporate time for conducting extractables tests. Two example schedules are provided:

  • Figure 4.2🔍a: Top level task groups
  • Figure 4.2🔍b: Details of key tasks

Time-consuming tasks are arranged early in the process and in parallel with other tasks to expedite implementation.

4.4.3 Qualification of SUT Components

Figure 4.3 shows the tasks to conduct a qualification of components, primarily focusing on extractables testing. Based on the condition that fundamental information on gamma irradiation compatibility, functional compliance, and shelf life information is available from the component supplier.

4.4.4 Introduction of SUT into Operations

These schedules are based on a broad scope to introduce SUT in bioprocessing. Includes tasks on container/component qualification within a fast implementation schedule:

  • Figure 4.4🔍a: Top level task groups
  • Figure 4.4🔍b: Details of key tasks

4.4.5 Technology Transfer

These schedules are intended for technology transfer of SUT via scale-up into another bioprocess operation:

  • Figure 4.5🔍a: Top level task groups (Technology Transfer)
  • Figure 4.5🔍b: Detailed key tasks (Technology Transfer)

Tutorial Commentary

Core concept: the five SUT scheduling scenarios

The ISPE SUT scheduling templates cover five different starting conditions, reflecting the diversity of real projects:

  • 4.4.1 (all components already validated): the fastest path, suitable for changeover or scale-up in a mature operation
  • 4.4.2 (some components need validation): the most common case, where new components need testing
  • 4.4.3 (component qualification focus): focuses on systematic validation of components from a new supplier
  • 4.4.4 (brand-new SUT implementation): the complete timeline for establishing SUT operations from scratch
  • 4.4.5 (technology transfer): special considerations for transferring from one site to another

Key terms: Gantt chart and critical path

Gantt chart: shows the duration of each task and their interrelationships as horizontal bars. The ISPE scheduling templates are in Gantt chart form.

Critical path: the longest chain of dependencies from project start to finish. Any delay on the critical path directly pushes back the completion date of the whole project. In SUT implementation, E&L extractables testing is usually on the critical path (the test cycle is long and cannot be compressed).

Phase-gate approach: divides a project into several phases, with a review gate at the end of each phase, and moves to the next phase only after the criteria are met. In SUT implementation, this avoids committing too many downstream resources before validation has succeeded.

Analogy: the cost of parallel tasks

Parallel tasks are like cooking several dishes at once: they shorten the time until dinner is served, but need several burners (resources) running at the same time. If you have only one cook (limited resources), too much parallel work causes things to fall through the cracks. In a SUT project, starting too many parallel tasks (such as running validation, automation integration and verification at the same time) can overload key personnel and actually slow overall progress.

The hidden time killer in scheduling: E&L testing

E&L (extractables and leachables) testing is the activity in a SUT schedule that most needs advance planning:

  • The test cycle usually takes 3-6 months (including extraction, analysis and reporting)
  • It must be completed before the other validation activities end, so that the final risk assessment can be done
  • If the results fail, the material must be changed and testing repeated, at a very high time cost

ISPE's recommendation: E&L testing must start as early as possible and run in parallel with other tasks; do not wait until other work is finished before starting it.

Key point: special scheduling considerations for technology transfer

The SUT schedule for a technology transfer (4.4.5) needs to take the following into account:

  • Scale changes: scaling up or down changes the SA/V ratio, which may require the risk assessment to be redone
  • Regulatory submissions: when transferring to a different country/region, design changes to the SUT may have to be notified to the new regulatory authority
  • Revalidation: even with identical components, installation and operating conditions at the new site may differ, so part of the validation must be repeated

CDMO application: from project plan to reality

Common scheduling pitfalls when a CDMO takes on SUT implementation for a new client's drug product:

  • Late supplier samples: supplier-promised sample delivery dates are often 2-4 weeks later than actual, so buffers must be built into the schedule
  • Client review delays: validation reports need client approval, and cross-time-zone communication can cause delays of several weeks
  • Prototype test failures: even when all components are validated, the assembled prototype can still fail on connection dimensions or pressure testing and need redesign

Recommendation: in the SUT implementation Master Timeline, reserve at least 20% buffer time each for E&L testing, prototype evaluation and client review.

Practice Questions

  1. In your most recent SUT implementation project, which tasks were on the critical path? Compared with the plan, what was the largest source of deviation in actual progress?
  2. If a client asks you to complete implementation of a brand-new SUT system (including component validation) within 6 months, which tasks in the 4.4.4 scheduling strategy would you run in parallel, and which must be run in sequence?

Chapter Summary

  • Four special considerations for SUT implementation: stronger supplier collaboration, risk and change management throughout, flexible scheduling, and integration of the interface between SUT and stainless steel systems. A dual sourcing strategy is the core safeguard of supply security and should be built into purchasing policy.
  • The A × B × C × D risk score formula: the four factors (route of administration, proximity to final product, contact time, surface area/volume ratio) are multiplied to give a quantitative risk score. A score ≤1,000 is low risk, 1,001-4,999 is medium risk and ≥5,000 is high risk, which directly determines the scope of in-house validation tests required.
  • The 15 PCM qualification attributes: E&L (attribute 8), biocompatibility (attribute 1) and container closure integrity (attribute 13) are the three most critical. Repeated testing can be reduced through strategies such as referencing existing supplier data, the QbD bracketing approach and paper exercises.
  • The two-track classification of change management: maintenance/improvement changes (low impact) vs. major disruptive changes (substantive changes in material, process, supplier or dimensions). The quality agreement must clearly specify the advance notice period for "major material changes" (usually 6-12 months), and implementation must be verified through periodic audits.
  • Critical path awareness in SUT scheduling: E&L extractables testing has a long cycle (3-6 months) and must be started early in the project and run in parallel. The Gantt chart templates (five scenarios) are a starting point and should be customized to actual supplier capability, client requirements and available resources, with buffer time reserved for E&L testing, prototype evaluation and client review.
4

Section 4: App 1–3: Regulations & Training

p121-p132

ISPE Good Practice Guide: Single-Use Technology | p121 - p132

Learning Objectives

  • Identify the main international standards, US regulations (21 CFR Part 211, USP <661>, USP <381>) and EU GMP requirements that apply to single-use systems (SUS), and build a regulatory map
  • Understand how the ISO 10993 biocompatibility series and the ISO 11137/11135 sterilization standards apply directly to the qualification of SUS materials
  • Master the correct receiving inspection procedure for SUS, from checking outer carton integrity and verifying the irradiation label to item-by-item visual inspection of each component type (bags, tubing, filters)
  • Understand the defective product complaint process: when to notify the supplier, which documents to submit, and the investigative logic of root cause analysis
  • Master the common failure modes of SUS in use (mainly leakage), match each risk to its mitigation measures, and apply them to CDMO aseptic filling operations
Appendix 1 — Regulations and Standards
5.1 International Standards — ICH & ISO

Original Text

Note: The tables in this appendix are not intended to be all inclusive.

International Council for Harmonisation (ICH)

International Organization for Standardization (ISO) — Quality Management

ISO — Biological Evaluation of Medical Devices (ISO 10993 Series)

ISO — Sterilization Standards

ISO — Cleanrooms & Other

Tutorial Commentary

Core concept: why does SUT need so many standards?

What makes single-use systems (SUS) special is that they span three regulatory domains at once: pharmaceutical GMP (ICH Q7/Q9/Q10), medical device safety (the ISO 10993 biocompatibility series), and sterilization technology (ISO 11135/11137). These three domains used to be independent, but SUS forces them together.

For example, a disposable bioreactor bag must meet all of the following:

  • ISO 10993-5 (in vitro cytotoxicity): the bag material must not harm the cultured cells
  • ISO 11137 (radiation sterilization): the sterilization method must be validated
  • ISO 14644-1 (cleanrooms): the process environment must be classified and controlled
  • ICH Q9 (quality risk management): the overall risk assessment framework

Analogy

Imagine building a submarine. It must meet "mechanical engineering standards" (ISO 9001 structural strength), "life support standards" (air quality), and "corrosion protection standards" (material compatibility) all at the same time. SUS is like that submarine: one product that has to satisfy several authorities and standards systems at once. The purpose of Appendix 1 is to lay out this "standards map" so you know which document to check for each aspect.

The role of the ICH series in SUT

ICH Q9 (Quality Risk Management) is the methodological basis for overall SUS risk management. All risks related to leachables, extractables and particulates must be assessed within the ICH Q9 framework.

ICH Q10 (Pharmaceutical Quality System) requires that supplier management, the CAPA system and change control be integrated into the company's quality system, which directly affects SUS supplier qualification.

ICH Q3D (Elemental Impurities) may also apply: if a SUS material contains specific metal catalysts, you must assess whether they could dissolve into the drug product and cause an elemental impurity problem.

Key point: the most important parts of ISO 10993

In SUS applications, the parts most often cited are:

  • ISO 10993-1: the overarching standard that sets the evaluation strategy. Chemical analysis comes first, and only then is the need for animal testing decided.
  • ISO 10993-5: in vitro cytotoxicity, the most basic biocompatibility threshold for SUS materials
  • ISO 10993-4: hemocompatibility, especially important for SUS that contact blood products
  • ISO 10993-11: systemic toxicity, usually satisfied through extractables/leachables studies

Key point: ISO 14644-1 cleanroom classification

The air cleanliness classes of ISO 14644-1 (ISO Class 5, 6, 7, 8) are the regulatory basis for designing the environments in which a pharmaceutical plant uses SUS. Aseptic filling must be performed in an ISO Class 5 (EU Grade A) environment, while SUS assembly is usually done in an ISO Class 7 or 8 (EU Grade C/D) environment. Understanding this classification system helps you define the environmental control requirements for SUS unpacking areas.

CDMO application: building a SUS standards matrix

For CDMO aseptic filling operations, we recommend building a "SUS applicable standards matrix" that lists, for each type of SUS (bioreactor bags, filling tubing, filter capsules, etc.), the relevant ISO biocompatibility requirements, the sterilization validation basis, and the cleanroom environment requirements. This matrix is requested during supplier quality assessment (SQA) and in regulatory submissions.

5.2 United States Regulations and Standards — FDA, AAMI, ASTM

Original Text

US Food and Drug Administration (FDA)

Association for the Advancement of Medical Instrumentation (AAMI)

American Society for Testing and Materials (ASTM) — Key Standards for SUS

Tutorial Commentary

Core concept: the direct impact of 21 CFR Part 211 on SUS

21 CFR Part 211 is the US FDA's GMP regulation for finished pharmaceuticals. Two of its provisions are most relevant to SUS:

  • §211.65 (Equipment construction): requires that product-contact surfaces of equipment must not be reactive, additive or absorptive. E&L studies on SUS plastics exist precisely to meet this requirement.
  • §211.94 (Drug product containers and closures): requires that drug product containers and closures must not be reactive, additive or absorptive to the extent that they alter the safety or potency of the drug. This directly governs the E&L requirements for SUS bag materials and connectors.

Analogy

21 CFR §211.65 is like a food regulation stating that "food containers must not contain substances harmful to humans." For a stainless steel vessel this is easy to meet. For a SUS plastic bag, however, the formulation may differ from lot to lot, so you must use scientific data (E&L studies) to "prove" it is safe, rather than relying on a material claim alone.

Key point: ASTM E3051, the SUS-specific standard

ASTM E3051-16 is currently the most direct industry guide for SUS design and validation. It covers the specification, design, validation and application of SUS, and is the SUS-specific extension of ASTM E2500 (validation of pharmaceutical systems in general). In a SUS validation program, this standard is usually listed as one of the main reference documents and used together with the BPOG guidance.

The impact of 21 CFR Part 11 on SUS digitalization

21 CFR Part 11 governs electronic records and electronic signatures and is increasingly important in modern SUS operations, because single-use sensors (disposable pressure sensors, pH sensors, etc.) output digital data directly to DCS/SCADA systems. These systems must comply with Part 11, including audit trails, access controls, and ensuring that electronic records have regulatory standing.

Key point: container closure integrity testing (CCIT)

The FDA's 2008 guidance allows container closure integrity testing (CCIT) to replace sterility testing as a stability test item. This is especially important for manufacturers who store intermediates in SUS bags. They need to select and validate an appropriate CCIT method (such as ASTM F2338 vacuum decay or ASTM F2391 helium tracer gas), and cannot rely on visual inspection alone.

CDMO application: common FDA inspection questions

Typical lines of questioning on SUS during an FDA inspection:

  • Has your SUS supplier been formally audited? Does the audit scope cover material qualification data relevant to §211.65 and §211.94?
  • How do you ensure that the E&L profile of each incoming SUS lot matches the one used in validation?
  • 21 CFR Part 820 (Quality System Regulation) sets requirements for medical devices. Some SUS suppliers are themselves subject to the QSR. Do you understand what this means for their quality system?

Practice Questions

  1. At your site, do the people responsible for SUS purchasing know what 21 CFR §211.94 requires of supplier E&L data? Has this information been built into the supplier qualification (SQA) checklist?
  2. Which components of the SUS you currently use contain single-use sensors? Has the digital output of these sensors been included in the scope of your 21 CFR Part 11 audits?
5.3 United States Pharmacopeia (USP)

Original Text

Tutorial Commentary

Core concept: the USP <661> series, the regulatory basis for plastic packaging materials

USP <661> is the most important pharmacopeial standard for SUS plastic materials and is divided into two subchapters:

  • USP <661.1> (plastic materials themselves): specifies identification tests and physicochemical tests for plastic materials, such as melt index, density, ash and heavy metals. These are requirements on the "material formulation."
  • USP <661.2> (plastic packaging systems): specifies extraction testing of the entire packaging system (including the processed bag). These are requirements on the "finished component."

Together, these two chapters form a systematic chemical evaluation framework for SUS bag materials and connectors.

Core concept: USP <381>, elastomeric closures

USP <381> sets test requirements specifically for elastomeric closures for injections (rubber/silicone stoppers), including appearance, transparency, functionality (self-sealing), biological tests (cytotoxicity, systemic toxicity) and chemical tests (reducing substances, heavy metals, etc.).

In SUS, components such as silicone tubing connectors and rubber septa generally need to meet USP <381>, and this is one of the test items a supplier's CoC (certificate of conformance) should include.

Analogy: extractables vs. leachables

USP <1663> and <1664> address Extractables and Leachables respectively, two terms that are often confused:

  • Extractables: all compounds that could possibly be released from the plastic, forced out under extreme conditions (strong solvents, high temperature). This is the upper boundary of potential risk and is generated by the supplier.
  • Leachables: substances that actually migrate into the drug product under real conditions of use. This is the actual risk and is determined by the drug manufacturer (the end user).

Think of a tea bag. Its "extractables" are everything you would pull out by dropping it into boiling acid; its "leachables" are what you actually drink after steeping it in normal-temperature water for 3 minutes. Regulators expect you to manage the part you actually drink.

Key point: particulate standards USP <787> vs. <788>

SUS can generate particulates during use (especially film fragments), so particulate standards are critical:

  • USP <788>: applies to general injectables, with wider limits (no more than 6,000 particles per container at ≥10 μm)
  • USP <787>: designed for protein injections of biologics, with stricter limits
  • USP <1787>: provides methodological guidance for subvisible particles in protein injections

If a SUS process involves filling a biologic, USP <787> is the stricter standard that applies.

CDMO application: who actually performs the USP testing

In CDMO practice, responsibility for USP testing is usually divided as follows:

  • Supplier responsibility: provides USP <661.1> material test data, USP <87>/<88> biological reactivity data, and USP <381> closure test data (included in the DMF or CoC)
  • Drug manufacturer/CDMO responsibility: performs the USP <1663>/<1664> E&L studies (or outsources them), USP <788> finished product particulate testing, and USP <1207> container closure integrity assessment
  • Shared responsibility: USP <85> endotoxin testing. The supplier provides the BET limit specification for the SUS, and the drug manufacturer performs finished product testing.

Practice Questions

  1. Does your supplier qualification (SQA) questionnaire include items asking for USP <661.1>/<661.2> test data? If not, how do you think it should be revised?
  2. If your CDMO client asks for an E&L study report for a process that uses SUS, which USP chapters will you need to draw data from?
5.4 European Standards and Regulations

Original Text

5.5 European Pharmacopoeia (EP)

5.6 Other International Regulatory and Pharmacopeial Organizations

Tutorial Commentary

Core concept: EU GMP Annex 1 and SUS

After its major revision in 2022, EU GMP Annex 1 (Manufacture of Sterile Medicinal Products) formally includes explicit requirements for SUS. The main impacts are:

  • SUS suppliers must be treated as critical material suppliers and audited on site on a regular basis
  • Integrity testing requirements for SUS: testing must be performed after receipt and cannot rely on the supplier's CoC alone
  • The contamination control strategy (CCS) must explicitly assess the microbial and particulate risks introduced by SUS
  • The use of SUS must be supported by a documented risk assessment that covers the impact of leachables on the product

Core concept: comparing the EP 3.1 series with the USP <661> series

The EP 3.1 series and the USP <661> series are both pharmacopeial standards for plastic container materials, but their methodologies differ:

  • EP 3.1: specifies tests separately by polymer class (PE, PP, EVA, silicone rubber, etc.), which favors management by "material category"
  • USP <661>: does not classify by material but uses one systematic approach to evaluate all plastic packaging materials, which favors management by "performance"

Products exported to the EU must meet EP 3.1; products exported to the US must meet USP <661>. To cover both, you must either run both sets of tests or confirm that the two are mutually recognizable.

Key point: the impact of REACH on SUS

REACH (EC No. 1907/2006) is the EU chemicals regulation. It makes manufacturers and importers responsible for the safe use of chemical substances. Its impact on SUS:

  • SUS materials must not contain more than 0.1 wt% of any SVHC (substance of very high concern); otherwise customers must be notified proactively
  • Suppliers must provide a REACH compliance statement (SDS/Declaration of Conformity)
  • When auditing a SUS supplier, REACH compliance documents are one of the items that must be checked

Key point: EP 3.1.9 silicone elastomers, the most commonly used elastomer in SUS

EP 3.1.9 sets requirements for silicone elastomers used in stoppers and tubing, including appearance, chemical tests (pH, oxidizable substances, foaming ability) and biological tests (acute systemic toxicity test). Silicone tubing is the most widely used component in SUS, and its EP 3.1.9 compliance data should be included in the supplier's DMF or technical document package.

Analogy: the global regulatory map

Imagine a SUS supplier as a multinational restaurant chain that must comply with US FDA (menu labeling law), EU REACH (ingredient safety law), EU GMP Annex 1 (kitchen hygiene standards) and national pharmacopeias (recipe requirements) all at once. Every market has its own rules, but the core principles are the same: safe materials, controlled processes, and documentation you can check. When selecting a SUS supplier, a CDMO needs to confirm that the supplier can provide a compliance data package covering the main target markets.
