CDMO application: multi-market regulatory compliance strategy

For a CDMO serving US, EU and Japanese clients at the same time, the recommended SUS regulatory compliance strategy is:

  • Require the supplier to provide a global regulatory compliance statement covering the FDA (21 CFR), the EU (REACH + EP) and the Japanese Pharmacopoeia
  • Build a SUS materials database that records the regulatory coverage of each component
  • State explicitly in SUS purchase contracts that the supplier must notify you proactively when regulations change, and bring this into the change control procedure
Appendix 2 — Example Training Procedures for Inspections

Original Text

SUSs are typically delivered in cardboard boxes. Individual SUS units are usually double packed under vacuum, in plastic transparent over-pouches as secondary packaging. Protective foams are frequently used to safeguard the film from sharp components such as clamps, connectors or filters.

Storage and Handling Recommendations

  • Follow shelf life and storage recommendations (environment, temperature, etc.) provided by the suppliers to minimize risk to the SUSs.
  • Store the SUSs in their original box in the warehouse to minimize the risk of damage (as the number of products in each box is typically validated by the supplier with regard to specific design, weight, and constraints).
  • When using cutters to open the cardboard box, take special precautions to avoid damaging the SUS.
  • Organize a storage area in (or near) the production room, equipped with adapted shelves (with flat surfaces and no sharp angles), and use ergonomic transport carts for transfers. When SUSs are stacked on a shelf, care should be taken to the maximum quantity specified by the supplier to minimize potential damage due to product/component weight.
  • Pay special attention to single-use mixers and single-use bioreactors which may include impellers, stirrers, and sensors.

General Inspection Procedures

In creating the inspection procedures, the supplier can offer key recommendations. General inspection procedures may include:

  • As a first step for all SUSs, check the cardboard box integrity and the certificate of release upon receipt.
  • Before unpacking the SUS, check the irradiation label and evaluate the over-pouch integrity to ensure it is under slight vacuum and free from damage.
  • Due to differences between SUS types, the procedures for the SUS itself should be based on component groups:
    • Packaging: check the over-pouches for creases, scratches, or tears.
    • Bag: check the bag for film damage (scratches or tears) and inspect the ports and sensors.
    • Container system: check suitability for bag installation and that the interior is clean, dry, and has no sharp areas. Suppliers should provide recommendation for hardware cleaning.
    • Tubing/connections: check the tubing for damage and missing clamps or cable ties. Ensure connections are properly tightened.
    • Filters and sensors: check for damage or improper installation.

Tutorial Commentary

Core concept: why do SUS training procedures need to be standardized?

Visual inspection of SUS is different from a routine check of conventional equipment. It is a one-time assessment performed before every use, and every SUS is brand new. This means that:

  • Operators perform the inspection on a "new" item each time, with no baseline of in-use condition to compare against, as there would be with conventional equipment
  • SUS defects may already exist before unpacking (transport damage) or may occur during installation
  • If a defect goes undetected, the consequence may be process contamination or batch loss, and in aseptic filling it may even lead to a failed sterile batch

Training procedures must therefore enable operators to systematically recognize the difference between normal and abnormal conditions.

Analogy

The SUS receiving inspection procedure is like the "pre-flight checklist" a pilot runs before every takeoff. Even for the same aircraft, the check must be repeated before every flight. The same applies to SUS: products from the same lot share the same design, but each individual unit may have been damaged in transit. A standardized procedure ensures that no step is skipped, even by experienced operators.

Key point: the importance of the irradiation label

The irradiation label is one of the most critical visual checks for SUS. After exposure to the correct dose of gamma or electron beam radiation, the label changes color (usually from yellow to red/orange). If the label has not changed color, the SUS may not have been properly sterilized and must not be used. Another item to confirm is whether the over-pouch still holds a slight vacuum. This is a real-time indicator of packaging integrity, and if the bag no longer shows a vacuum, the packaging may have been breached.

Core concept: inspection logic by component group

The ISPE SUT guide recommends inspecting by "component group" rather than by "the SUS as a whole", for these reasons:

  • Different components have different failure modes (film: tears; tubing: kinks; filters: housing damage)
  • Once components are grouped, specific accept/reject criteria can be set for each type
  • It aids traceability: if an abnormality is found later, the search can quickly be narrowed to a specific component group

The five component groups: outer packaging → bag → container hardware system → tubing/connectors → filters/sensors.

Key point: the regulatory implications of storage conditions

SUS storage conditions are not just a matter of "don't crush it." The storage recommendations from the supplier (temperature, humidity, light protection, etc.) are part of the validation basis. If actual storage conditions go outside the supplier's specified range (for example, a warehouse that gets too hot in summer), the supplier's shelf-life commitment may no longer hold, undermining the quality assurance basis for the whole lot of SUS. Storage condition monitoring records are therefore an important GMP document in SUS management.

CDMO application: guidance for building a SUS visual inspection SOP

When creating or updating a SUS visual inspection SOP, we recommend including the following elements:

  • Scope: list the SUS models covered (components differ greatly between models, so each needs its own specification)
  • Reference documents: the supplier's installation and operation manual (IFU) and supplier training materials
  • Acceptance criteria: pass/fail criteria for each component group, supported by photographs (normal vs. rejected examples)
  • Handling of nonconforming product: quarantine, recording and complaint procedures when a nonconforming unit is found (see the defective product handling process in Appendix 3)
  • Training records: operators must complete OJT (on-the-job training) and be able to demonstrate the assessment, and training records must be retained

Practice Questions

  1. At your site, who performs SUS unpacking and visual inspection (QC, operators, or both)? If the irradiation label has not changed color, what follow-up steps does your current SOP specify?
  2. Is the SUS staging area monitored for temperature and humidity? If a warehouse temperature excursion occurs, how do you assess whether the affected SUS are still usable?
Appendix 3 — Defective Products and Failure Risks

Original Text

Complaint and Investigation Procedure

In an occurrence of failure, it is recommended to inform the supplier even when an official complaint or report is not required. The supplier can investigate the root cause, make recommendations, and develop a corrective action plan. In addition, the failure can be entered into the supplier CAPA system to support continuous quality improvement for the single-use product.

The supplier should provide to the end-user the procedure for complaint registration and the list of requested documentation. Product reference, batch number, and process descriptions should always be submitted to the supplier. Safety information about fluid contact and a certificate of decontamination are often requested before returning any defective products. The defective product should be evaluated and tested for damage or deviation that will support root cause analysis.

It is preferable to send the entire defective SUS (not only the part where the defect is located), arranged back to the originally received configuration with the original set of packaging (including over-pouches), to provide more information on where the failure may have occurred. For example, if damage/demarcation on the packaging and SUS correlate to the same location, this may indicate that damage occurred prior to installation.

If the defective product cannot be sent (e.g., due to contact with toxin or live virus), the complaint investigation and report would only be based on a batch documentation review. In this case, submitting pictures or videos with a thorough explanation can help support the investigation.

Common Failure Modes and Mitigation

The remainder of this appendix provides a general overview of common factors that lead to SUS failures during use. Due to the broad variety of SUSs, the associated risks should be identified by the supplier and end-user and assessed in the context of the application; an adapted troubleshooting procedure can then be defined.

Detection of supplier defects (missing components, incorrect assembly, foreign particulates within the packaging, etc.) is part of the inspection procedure. Such observations should lead directly to a formal complaint.

During use, SUS failures are mostly related to leakages. The main factors that contribute to leakage are listed below, with suggestions for how to mitigate these risks:

  • Improper handling during storage, transfer, and setup
    • Optimize storage conditions following the supplier's recommendation
    • Use ergonomic storage bin and transfer cart
    • Provide operational training
  • Mechanical stress on film or welds
    • Optimize bag deployment method, minimizing manipulations and folds/creases
    • Follow supplier recommendations for pressure and flow rate
    • Optimize shipping condition

Tutorial Commentary

Core concept: the link between the supplier CAPA system and the end user

Handling a defective product is not as simple as "return it and get a replacement." At its core it is a closed loop of knowledge management and quality improvement. The ISPE SUT guide stresses that even when no formal complaint is required, notifying the supplier is recommended, for three reasons:

  • Root cause analysis: only when the supplier receives enough failure information can it trace the source of the manufacturing defect at the plant level (raw material lot, production equipment, packaging procedure, etc.)
  • Support for the CAPA system: the supplier's CAPA system is the guardrail for manufacturing quality, and end-user feedback is an important input that drives it
  • Trend analysis: a single event may be a one-off, but when several end users report the same problem, a systemic defect can be identified

Analogy

The SUS defective product complaint process is like the aviation industry's pilot safety reporting system (ASRS). Pilots do not encounter a major accident every time, but they report anything abnormal, which lets airlines and manufacturers identify systemic problems and correct them before a major accident occurs. The same holds for SUS: each complaint report you file contributes to the quality improvement of the whole industry.

Key point: steps to complete before returning a defective product

Before returning a defective product to the supplier, you will usually need to provide:

  • Certificate of Decontamination: confirms that the SUS has been safely separated from hazardous substances (toxins, live virus, cell culture) to protect the supplier's receiving and investigation staff
  • Restored original packaging: return the SUS as close as possible to the condition in which it was received, including the outer packaging, because the correlation between packaging damage and SUS damage locations is an important clue in root cause analysis
  • Batch documentation: provide the SUS lot number, usage records, process conditions and other documents for the supplier's investigation

Core concept: the most common SUS failure mode, leakage

The source text states explicitly that SUS failures in use are mostly related to leakages. The main sources of leakage include:

  • Improper handling: film damage or stress on welds during storage, transfer or installation
  • Mechanical stress on film or welds: excessive folding, overpressure operation, and improper pressure or flow rate settings

What these two failure modes have in common is that both can be prevented by good operator training and standardized procedures, without relying on after-the-fact detection.

Key point: investigation strategy when the defective product cannot be returned

When a SUS has contacted toxins or live biological agents (live virus vectors) and cannot be physically returned, the investigation can only rely on documents. In this case, we recommend the following measures:

  • Take photos/videos immediately when the defect is found (damage location, condition, packaging condition)
  • Keep the pre-use visual inspection records (to confirm whether the damage occurred before or during use)
  • Keep the batch process data (pressure, flow rate and time records) as objective evidence of operating conditions
  • Ask the supplier to conduct a "desktop investigation" based on the documents, which is an acceptable form of investigation from a regulatory standpoint

Core concept: distinguishing supplier defects from handling defects

The first step in root cause analysis is to determine whether the defect is a "supplier manufacturing defect" or an "end-user handling defect":

  • Signs of a supplier defect: missing components, assembly errors, foreign matter inside the packaging, the same defect appearing in several units from the same lot
  • Signs of a handling defect: damage location correlates with the operating sequence, higher incidence when new staff are involved, higher damage rates at particular workstations

The correlation between packaging damage and SUS damage locations is the key clue for telling the two apart. If the damage locations match, the damage probably occurred before arrival at the site (transport damage, which is the supplier's responsibility).

CDMO application: building a SUS defective product management procedure

When building a SUS defective product management procedure, a CDMO needs to consider the following special situations:

  • Customer notification obligation: if the defective SUS has contacted a customer's drug substance, you must assess whether to open a deviation investigation and notify the customer
  • Batch impact assessment: a defective SUS may cause batch contamination (particulates, leakage) or batch loss, so a clear batch impact assessment procedure is needed
  • Clarifying supplier responsibility: the complaint process should preserve a clear timeline (SUS receipt date, storage conditions, time of use) to help establish where responsibility lies
  • Trend reports: set up a tracking database for SUS defective products and submit trend reports to the supplier regularly to drive its continual improvement

Practice Questions

  1. Does your site currently have a formal complaint procedure for defective SUS? When a defective SUS has already contacted process materials, what safety information must your complaint record include?
  2. If three SUS of the same model leak at the same location (for example, the same side of a tubing weld), which hypotheses will your root cause analysis consider first? How would you design the investigation plan?
  3. When the defective product cannot be returned (for example, a SUS that has contacted live virus), which specific documents and data does your desktop investigation need to collect?

Appendix 1–3 Section Summary

  • Building the regulatory map: SUS falls under the combined jurisdiction of ICH (quality system/risk management), ISO 10993 (biocompatibility), ISO 11137/11135 (sterilization validation), 21 CFR Part 211 (US GMP), EU GMP Annex 1 (sterile manufacturing) and national pharmacopeias (USP, EP). Building a systematic regulatory compliance matrix is a necessary first step in adopting SUS.
  • The USP dual-track standards: USP <661>/<661.1>/<661.2> (chemical evaluation of plastic packaging materials) and USP <381> (elastomeric closures) are the core pharmacopeial requirements. Together with USP <1663>/<1664> (E&L studies), they form a complete framework for material safety assessment.
  • ASTM E3051 is the backbone of SUS validation: as the first SUS-specific ASTM standard, it gives a unified methodological basis for specification, design validation and application assessment of pharmaceutical SUS.
  • The core of the training procedure is systematic inspection: visual inspection of SUS should follow the five component groups (outer packaging, bag, container hardware, tubing/connectors, filters/sensors), with particular attention to confirming the irradiation label and the over-pouch vacuum. This is the last quality gate before each use.
  • Defective product management is a proactive quality tool: even where a complaint is not mandatory, notify the supplier proactively and use its CAPA system to drive continual improvement. Complete defect documentation (lot number, process records, photos, certificate of decontamination) is the basis for root cause analysis, and for defective products that cannot be returned, a desktop investigation is an acceptable alternative.
  • Leakage is the most common SUS failure mode: the main sources are improper handling (storage, transport, installation) and mechanical stress (excessive folding, overpressure). Both can be significantly reduced through standardized SOPs and operator training, and prevention is better than tracing causes after the fact.
5

Section 5: App 4: Risk Qualification Attributes

p133-p146

ISPE Good Practice Guide: Single-Use Technology | p133 - p146

Learning Objectives

  • Master the test purpose, standard methods and acceptance criteria for the 15 risk-based validation attributes of single-use systems (SUS), including biocompatibility, mechanical properties, gas transmission, compendial physicochemical testing and animal origin control
  • Understand the design space concept for extractables/leachables (E&L) studies, the combined test strategy of polar/non-polar solvents, pH range and temperature conditions, and how to use supplier documentation for risk assessment
  • Distinguish the role of TOC, pH and conductivity as non-specific consistency tests: understand that their value lies in establishing baseline values during validation, not in serving as release criteria
  • Understand the technical requirements for sterility assurance level (SAL) validation and container closure integrity (CCI) testing, as well as practical considerations for particulate testing, protein adsorption studies and calibration of embedded instrumentation
  • Build an integrated mindset of "attribute = material risk + application conditions + regulatory requirements" that can be applied flexibly in supplier qualification and in writing the user requirements specification (URS)
8.1 Attribute 1: Biocompatibility Testing

Original Text

Biocompatibility testing is a core requirement for polymeric materials having product contact. Testing should follow USP <87> and USP <88> as follows:

  • Documentation should be provided to show testing by a recognized laboratory meeting the requirements of 21 CFR Part 58 Good Laboratory Practices (GLP) for Nonclinical Laboratory Studies.
  • Results should be reported with actual reactivity scores, rather than with a simple "Pass". The criteria for a passing result could be less than Grade 2 (mild sensitivity), where Grade 0 (no reactivity) is the desired result.

Results for the following tests are expected:

  • Elution and agar diffusion tests per USP <87>:
    • The elution test involves extraction of polymeric material using minimum essential media and placed on confluent monolayers of mouse fibroblast cells and examined after 48 hours for changes in cell morphology.
    • The agar diffusion test involves placing portions of material on an agarose surface directly overlaying confluent monolayers of mouse fibroblast cells and examining after 24 hours for changes in cell morphology.
  • Systemic injection, intracutaneous, and implantation tests per USP <88>:
    • Studies should be performed as Class VI plastics and with 70°C for the temperature of extraction.
  • Suppliers may choose to use alternative testing, such as ISO 10993-1; however, scientific justification for equivalency in testing results should be provided.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Regulatory Framework for Biocompatibility

Biocompatibility testing confirms that a polymer material will not cause adverse reactions in living organisms. For SUS components with product contact, it is the most fundamental safety threshold.

There are two main testing routes:

  • USP <87> / <88>: the US Pharmacopeia route, combining in vitro and in vivo testing
  • ISO 10993-1: the international standard route, which is risk-assessment driven and requires scientific justification of equivalence

Important: the supplier must provide the actual reactivity scores (e.g., Grade 0, 1, 2) and not merely write "Pass"; regulators expect raw data that can be reviewed.

Test Methods

USP <87> in vitro cytotoxicity:
  Elution test:
    Extraction medium → mouse fibroblast monolayer
    Observation time: 48 hours

  Agar diffusion test:
    Material placed directly on the agar surface
    Observation time: 24 hours

USP <88> in vivo biological reactivity:
  Class VI plastics (most stringent class)
  Extraction temperature: 70°C
  Includes systemic injection, intracutaneous injection and implantation tests

Acceptance criteria:
  Grade 0 = no reaction (ideal result)
  Grade <2 = mild sensitization (acceptable upper limit)

Analogy

Biocompatibility testing is like toxicity testing for food additives: before a new packaging material can be approved for food contact, you must show that it will not leach harmful substances into the food. Class VI is the highest safety class for plastics, much like a "food grade" certification for food contact materials, and means the material can safely contact living organisms under the harshest conditions.

Key Notes

The ISO 10993-1 methodology is not identical to USP <87>/<88>. If a supplier chooses the ISO route, it must provide a written scientific justification that the two are equivalent. This is often overlooked in regulatory reviews and can easily lead to a Warning Letter or a Form 483 observation.

Application

When evaluating a new supplier's biocompatibility documentation, request the following:

  • Complete test reports (not summaries) from a GLP-accredited laboratory
  • The actual score for each test (Grade number)
  • Whether the test samples match the as-supplied state (e.g., whether they were gamma irradiated)
  • If ISO 10993 is used, a documented equivalence justification
8.2 Attribute 2: Mechanical Properties

Original Text

For mechanical properties, the expectation is that the supplier will supply the documented test results for the applicable properties of each single-use product. Refer to:

  • ASTM F2097-16 Standard Guide for Design and Evaluation of Primary Flexible Packaging for Medical Products
  • BPSA 2015 Single-Use Manufacturing Component Quality Test Matrices Guide for best practices and specific test references

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Why Mechanical Property Testing Matters

Mechanical properties testing ensures that a single-use system maintains its physical integrity throughout its life cycle (storage, transport and use). For a biopharmaceutical process, this means that bags do not rupture during mixing or shipping, tubing does not burst under pressure, and connectors remain sealed after repeated connection and disconnection.

Key mechanical tests typically include: tensile strength, seal strength, impact/puncture resistance, flex-crack resistance and film thickness uniformity.

Analogy

Imagine buying a batch of infusion bags: you need to confirm not only that the bags do not leak today, but also whether they become brittle after storage at -20°C, and whether they crack after being flexed 1,000 times by an infusion pump. Mechanical property testing simulates these "worst-case use scenarios" so that SUS components do not become a source of contamination under any realistic operating condition.

Key Notes

ASTM F2097 is a guide for the design and evaluation of flexible packaging, while the BPSA test matrix guide targets bioprocess SUS components and gives a more specific list of tests and industry best practice. Suppliers should provide the mechanical test data package that corresponds to each product type (bags, tubing, connectors, etc.).

Practice Questions

  1. In your facility, when a SUS bag develops a small crack during processing, how would you trace afterwards whether it was a material defect or an operational problem? How would baseline mechanical property data help the root cause analysis?
  2. Mechanical test data from suppliers are usually generated under "standard" conditions. If your process conditions (temperature, pressure, contact time) fall outside the supplier's tested range, how should you handle it?
8.3 Attribute 3: Gas Transmission Properties

Original Text

The gas transmission properties of containers constructed of plastics should be provided according to the appropriate ASTM methods:

  • ASTM F1249-13 for water vapor at dry conditions
  • ASTM D3985-17 for oxygen at dry conditions
  • ASTM F1927-14 for oxygen at controlled relative humidity

The conditions of the testing should be stated for the reported result(s), including thickness of the film tested, temperature of the test, oxygen partial pressure used on both sides of the film during the test, and relative humidity on the upstream side of the film during the test.

If the testing completed to provide reported values for the oxygen and water vapor transmission rates were not completely in accordance with the appropriate ASTM method, then the differences between the test method and the ASTM method should be clearly stated.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Gas Transmission and Product Stability

Gas transmission is a key indicator of whether a plastic film can effectively block oxygen and water vapor. For biopharmaceutical processes:

  • Oxygen Transmission Rate (OTR): directly affects the stability of oxygen-sensitive biological products (e.g., proteins, vaccines)
  • Water Vapor Transmission Rate (WVTR): affects concentration changes and container integrity during long-term storage

Test Condition Requirements

When reporting gas transmission data, the following must be stated explicitly:
  - Film thickness (µm or mil)
  - Test temperature (°C)
  - Oxygen partial pressure (on each side of the film)
  - Relative humidity on the upstream side (%RH)

If the ASTM standard method is deviated from:
  - The deviations must be clearly described
  - Values must not be reported without their conditions

Analogy

Gas transmission testing is like testing how well a food storage bag keeps food fresh: the same film lets oxygen through at very different rates in a 4°C refrigerator and in a 40°C tropical climate. That is why the test conditions must be reported; without them the number is meaningless. A multilayer film structure (e.g., EVA/EVOH/PE) is like the multiple layers of a thermos flask, where each layer plays a different barrier role.

Application

In long-term storage studies (e.g., 24-month stability), if a SUS bag is used to store an oxidation-sensitive mAb product, you must confirm that the bag OTR meets requirements across the whole storage temperature range. If the supplier gas transmission data were generated at 23°C/0%RH and your storage condition is 2-8°C, you need to confirm that the data are applicable or request supplementary testing.

8.4 Attribute 4: Compendial Physicochemical Testing

Original Text

Physicochemical testing should be performed for components constructed of plastics, such as high-density PE, low-density PE, PP, PET, PETG, and poly(ethylene-co-vinyl acetate). Testing should follow USP <661> or EP 3.1.3 requirements set forth for these plastics. Testing should be applied to containers, connectors, tubing, fitting, etc. as appropriate.

Plastic container requirements of USP <661> for polymeric containers are listed below, with additional specific tests according to polymer type:

  • Infrared spectroscopy (IR) with multiple internal reflectance spectrum, range is specific to the plastic type
  • Differential scanning calorimetry, range is specific to the plastic type
  • Heavy metals
  • Non-volatile residue
  • Residue on ignition
  • In vitro biological reactivity tests per USP <87>
  • Total terephthaloyl moieties
  • Ethylene glycol

Table 8.1 details the plastic container property requirements of the EP according to polymer type.

Table 8.1: Summary of European Pharmacopoeia Testing Requirements

* If used in the composition of the material. Polyolefins apply to PE, PP, and copolymers with up to 25% higher homologues (C4–C10) or carboxylic acids/esters.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Purpose of Compendial Physicochemical Testing

Compendial physicochemical testing is a standardized characterization specific to each polymer type, confirming that the material meets pharmacopeial specifications.

USP <661> and the EP 3.1.x series are the two main frameworks; their test items partly overlap, but each has unique requirements.

  • IR spectroscopy: confirms polymer identity and prevents material substitution
  • DSC (differential scanning calorimetry): confirms the melting point range and ensures material consistency
  • Heavy metals and non-volatile residue: confirm the absence of harmful impurities

Key Notes

The EP test matrix (Table 8.1) varies by polymer type. For example, PP with additives (EP 3.1.6) requires testing for extractable chromium and vanadium, whereas PE without additives (EP 3.1.4) does not, because these metals usually come from catalyst residues and the latter is made with a different catalyst system.

Practical tip: the supplier CoA (certificate of analysis) must correspond to the polymer type of the component you are buying; a test report for a different material cannot be used.

Analogy

Compendial physicochemical testing is like the factory quality inspection of a car: different models (PP, PE, PETG) have different inspection checklists. You cannot use the factory inspection report of a Toyota Camry in place of the report for a Toyota RAV4, even though both are Toyotas. Likewise, a test report for EP 3.1.5 (PE with additives) cannot replace the requirements of EP 3.1.6 (PP with additives).

Practice Questions

  1. Your supplier provides a USP <661> test report, but your purchasing specification requires compliance with EP 3.1.x. What steps should you take to confirm whether supplementary testing is needed?
  2. Why is the "non-volatile residue" indicator particularly important for materials in contact with injectable products? What does it mean if the limit is exceeded?
8.5 Attribute 5: Animal Origin Control

Original Text

Animal origin control is a core requirement for polymeric materials having product contact. The single-use product should not contain or be derived from specified risk material as defined in EU Commission Decision 97/534/EC. Any bovine material must be product originated from a BSE-free country as defined by 9 CFR Part 94, Section 94.18.

If it does contain a stearate or other ingredient of animal origin, the processing conditions of the ingredient should meet the requirements of the EP 5.2.8 and EMA "Note for guidance on minimizing the risk of transmitting animal spongiform encephalopathy agents via human and veterinary medicinal products (EMA/410/01 rev.3)".

Relevant animal species within the scope of these requirements are cattle, sheep, goat, animals that are susceptible to infection with TSE agents, or susceptible to infection through the oral route other than humans and non-human primates.

The materials of concern associated with the animal species listed above are those used for the preparation of:

  • Active substances
  • Excipients and adjuvants
  • Raw and starting materials and reagents used in the production (bovine serum albumin, enzymes, and culture media)
  • Materials that come into direct contact with the equipment used in the manufacture of medicinal product – potential for cross-contamination
  • Materials used in the qualification of plant and equipment where there is a risk of cross-contamination, e.g., media fills
  • Other materials such as cleaning agents, softeners, and lubricants that come into contact with the medicinal product during routine manufacture

The following points should be established by the supplier:

  • If the product, or the starting materials for the product, contain any material of animal origin
  • If there are any animal-based products used at the facility that may come into contact with the products during the manufacturing process (e.g., lubricants)
  • If any of the cleaning materials used are derived from animal origin

If "yes" to any above — supplier should provide a signed and dated Certificate of Suitability (CEP) issued by EDQM.

The following BSE risk categorization (per EP) should be identified:

  • Highly unlikely
  • Unlikely but not excluded
  • Likely but not confirmed or confirmed at a lower level
  • Confirmed at a high level

Infectivity categories of animal parts:

  • High Infectivity (e.g., CNS tissues): High titre in later stages of TSE
  • Lower Infectivity Peripheral Tissues: Tested positive for infectivity and/or PrPsc
  • No Detectable Infectivity (e.g., cleaning materials, lubricants): No infectivity detected

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The TSE/BSE Risk Control Framework

The core concern of animal origin control is transmissible spongiform encephalopathy (TSE), in particular bovine spongiform encephalopathy (BSE, commonly known as "mad cow disease").

Key terms:

  • TSE: prion diseases including BSE (cattle) and scrapie (sheep)
  • PrPsc (scrapie-type prion protein): the pathogenic prion protein, resistant to heat and radiation and extremely difficult to inactivate
  • EDQM CEP (European Directorate for the Quality of Medicines Certificate of Suitability): the compliance document the supplier needs to provide

Analogy

Animal origin control is like food allergen management: the issue is not only the product itself but the whole manufacturing environment. Even if your SUS bag contains no bovine-derived ingredients, there is a cross-contamination risk if the plant uses a tallow-containing lubricant to maintain a machine that then contacts your bag. The scope of inquiry therefore needs to cover raw materials, processing aids, equipment lubricants, cleaning agents, and the substrates used for media fill.

Key Notes

The unique danger of TSE prions is their extreme stability: even autoclaving at 134°C for 18 minutes does not fully eliminate them. This is why regulations require "source control" (not using materials with TSE risk) rather than "elimination by terminal sterilization". A CEP document is the only written form of assurance accepted by regulators.

Application

During supplier audits, the animal origin statement needs to cover the following four levels:

  • Raw materials of the component itself (polymers, additives)
  • Processing aids used during manufacturing (lubricants, mold release agents)
  • Equipment cleaning agents (those containing animal fatty acid derivatives)
  • Materials used for media fill (APS) validation

Practice Questions

  1. Your SUS bag supplier is based in the United States, which the EU considers a country with a BSE risk of "Unlikely but not excluded". What additional documentation should you ask the supplier to provide?
  2. A supplier claims that its lubricant contains no animal-derived components but has no CEP document, only a self-declaration. Is this sufficient in an EU regulatory audit?
8.6 Attribute 6: Total Organic Carbon Analysis

Original Text

Although TOC is a non-specific test, it is a means for testing consistency in a process. Performed during qualification, this value can serve as a baseline for subsequent testing which may include incoming acceptance testing. As part of qualification, there may not be an acceptance criterion.

TOC analysis should be conducted consistent with USP <643>, but other methods may be utilized if the methods are described. TOC should be reported in mg/L units.

As with the other qualification tests, the test material used should be representative of the component supplied; for instance, if the component is supplied gamma irradiated, then the test material should be gamma irradiated.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Role of TOC as a Non-Specific Consistency Indicator

Total organic carbon (TOC) measures the total amount of organic carbon in an aqueous solution. It is a non-specific indicator: it tells you "how much organic material has leached out" but not "what the organic material is".

Three roles of TOC in SUS validation:

  • Establishing a validation baseline: the primary purpose at the validation stage is to establish a baseline value for comparison between batches
  • Monitoring process consistency: in later incoming testing, an abnormally high TOC can trigger an investigation
  • Verifying cleaning effectiveness: where applicable, it can be used to verify the cleaning of components

Test Specifications

Standard method: USP <643>
Reporting unit: mg/L (or ppm)

Sample representativeness requirements:
  As-supplied state = gamma irradiated → test samples must be gamma irradiated
  As-supplied state = non-irradiated → test samples need not be irradiated

Note: there is usually no acceptance criterion at the validation stage;
the values serve only as baseline reference values for later monitoring

Analogy

TOC is like a "turbidity meter" for kitchen drain water: it only tells you how dirty the water is (total organic content), not whether it is grease, vegetable scraps or detergent. Although the information is not precise, long-term trend analysis is very useful: if the TOC of a batch of bags is suddenly three times the usual level, even without knowing what the substance is, it is already a signal that needs investigation.

Key Notes

Gamma irradiation changes the chemical structure of polymers and may increase the types and amounts of leachable organic substances. The sample treatment state must therefore be exactly the same as the actual as-supplied state; this principle runs through the test requirements of all 15 attributes.

8.7 Attribute 7: pH and Conductivity

Original Text

Although pH and conductivity are non-specific tests, they are a means for testing consistency in a process. Performed during qualification, this value can serve as a baseline for subsequent testing which may include incoming acceptance testing.

Specific applications may have a pH range requirement, but for most products when rinsed with carbon dioxide free water, the pH should be no less than 5 and no higher than 8.

Specific applications may have a conductivity requirement, but for most products when rinsed with pharmacopeial grade WFI, the conductivity should not be more than double the conductivity of the water quality being used for testing.

As with the other qualification tests, the test material used should be representative of the component supplied; for instance, if the component is supplied gamma irradiated, then the test material should be gamma irradiated.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: pH and Conductivity as Consistency Indicators

pH and conductivity are both non-specific tests. Like TOC, their main function is process consistency monitoring, not the precise identification of the leached species.

A low pH may indicate leaching of organic acidic substances, while a high pH may indicate release of basic additives. High conductivity usually reflects leaching of ionic substances (e.g., degradation products of antioxidants and stabilizers).

General Acceptance Ranges

pH (general applications):
  Test solution: carbon dioxide free water
  Acceptable range: 5.0 ≤ pH ≤ 8.0

Conductivity (general applications):
  Test solution: pharmacopeial-grade WFI
  Acceptable upper limit: ≤ 2 × WFI baseline conductivity

Note:
  - Special applications (e.g., contact with strong acid/base buffers) may have different ranges
  - Baseline values are established at the validation stage; later incoming inspection can use them as screening criteria

Analogy

pH and conductivity are like the "routine biochemistry panel" in a blood test: pH corresponds to blood acidity and conductivity to electrolyte concentration. A single value of either is not enough to diagnose a specific disease, but when trends are tracked over time, if the rinse water pH of a batch of components suddenly drops from the normal 6.5 to 4.0, it means you need to investigate in depth which acidic substance is leaching in large amounts.

Application

When setting incoming quality control specifications for a SUS supplier, the pH and conductivity data from the validation period can be used to set control limits. For example, if the rinse water pH of 10 batches during validation was all within 6.2–6.8, an incoming upper limit of pH 7.0 can be set, with an investigation triggered when it is exceeded. This is more sensitive to the process than relying solely on the pharmacopeial range of 5-8.

8.8 Attribute 8: Extractables and Leachables

Original Text

The extractables testing approach described in this section is intended to create a design space for use of the intended materials. This approach does not rely on typical testing per USP <381> and USP <661>. As such, the exaggerated conditions used for any extractable testing done by the supplier would define the conditions within which the intended material could be used.

The design space would be defined by such variables as pH range, temperature range, contact time, and/or solvent. The components used for an E&L study should include pre and post sample treatment. The post-treated sample should use the same treatment steps, including gamma irradiation for sterilization as intended to be supplied as components to the end-user.

To support an E&L evaluation, the supplier would be requested to perform and/or provide:

  • Extractable testing supporting the design space:
    • Polar (e.g., WFI, water) and non-polar (organic) solvents (e.g., isopropanol, hexane)
    • Media/buffers with low and high pH
    • Media/solvents of varying temperatures (e.g., 20°C or ambient and 80°C or reflux temperature)
    • Media/solvents of varying ionic strengths (e.g., NaF/KCl)
    • Varying extraction times (e.g., 2, 4, and 8 months)
  • Leachable testing supporting the design space:
    • In-use exposure times of up to thirty days at ambient and elevated temperature (e.g., 40°C)
    • Analysis at minimum with GC-MS, HPLC/UV-Visible (or MS), and ICP-MS (or other heavy metal analysis)

Copies of actual extractables/leachables study reports should be provided by the supplier. All data should be expressed in ppm and/or mg/in² (bag), mg/in length (tubing) or mg/unit (filter).

Any extractables that exceed pharmacopeial limits (such as ICH Q3C(R6) Class 1 solvents and USP <467>) should be identified.

Table 8.2: Examples of Extractables (50 kGy Irradiation)

Note: From polypropylene filter capsule with modified PVDF membrane after 50 kGy irradiation.

Toxicity of E&L: Some chemicals may be unacceptable at almost any concentration (e.g., ICH Q3C Class 1 solvents). Determination of toxicity is a function of actual concentration in the final drug product, dose size and regimen, mode of delivery, patient population, and risk/benefit assessment.

A leachable also may affect functional elements of the process. For example, residual silicone from silicone tubing has been reported to significantly suppress post-use bubble points of sterilizing grade filters, resulting in false integrity test failures.

Using Supplier Documentation for E&L: Supplier extractables data is a starting point for general information on E&L. An assessment should be performed to confirm if data is representative of the actual process. Supplier documentation may be sufficient for certain applications where risk is low.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Fundamental Difference in E&L

Extractables vs leachables is the most important and most frequently confused pair of concepts in SUS risk assessment:

  • Extractables: all compounds forcibly extracted from the material under "exaggerated conditions" (polar/non-polar solvents, extreme pH, high temperature). They represent the maximum possible release and are used to define the boundaries of the "design space"
  • Leachables: compounds that migrate from the material into the product under "actual use conditions". They represent the real risk and are the direct basis for patient safety assessment

Relationship: Extractables ⊇ Leachables (extractables cover all potential leachables, but actual leached amounts are usually far lower than extracted amounts)

The Three Core E&L Analytical Methods

Minimum required combination of analytical methods:
  1. GC-MS (gas chromatography/mass spectrometry)
     → volatile and semi-volatile organic compounds

  2. HPLC-UV/Vis or HPLC-MS
     → non-volatile organic compounds
     (e.g., antioxidants, UV absorbers)

  3. ICP-MS (inductively coupled plasma/mass spectrometry)
     → trace metal elements

Data units:
  Bag-type components: ppm or mg/in²
  Tubing-type components: mg/in length
  Filter-type components: mg/unit

Analogy

E&L is like a leaching test on a newly bought kettle under different conditions: soak the kettle in boiling water, coffee and fruit juice for a week each (these are the exaggerated conditions of extractables), then analyze how much substance leaches out in each case. Leachables, by contrast, are the amounts that actually enter your drinking water when you use the kettle normally every day. You need the former to understand the worst case, but what you really care about is the health impact of the latter.

Key Notes

Special risk of silicone tubing: the source document specifically notes that residual silicone from silicone tubing can significantly suppress the bubble point test of filters, leading to false integrity test failures. This shows that the harm from leachables is not limited to toxicity; they can also undermine the reliability of critical process control tests. This is an interaction that requires special attention when selecting SUS components.

Application

Decision logic when evaluating supplier E&L documentation:

  • Low-risk applications (short contact time, no direct product contact, upstream processing) → the supplier standard E&L documentation is usually sufficient
  • Medium-risk applications (buffer contact, storage bags) → confirm the overlap between the supplier test conditions and your process conditions
  • High-risk applications (direct final product contact, long-term storage, high-temperature processes) → a user-level leachables confirmation study is required

Practice Questions

  1. Your process uses pH 8.0 phosphate buffer in contact with a SUS bag at 37°C for up to 14 days. The supplier E&L study was performed at pH 4 and pH 8, but only up to 40°C and only up to 8 days of contact time. Do you think this documentation is sufficient to support your application?
  2. If 2,4-Di-tert-butylphenol (DTBP, a common antioxidant degradation product) is found in the leachables study of your DS (drug substance) storage bag, what elements should your next risk assessment include?
8.9 Attribute 9: Chemical Compatibility

Original Text

Chemical compatibility is the measure of resistance exhibited by a specific contact material (plastics, valves, connectors, etc.) to a specific chemical (e.g., acids/bases) or contact with heavy metals (e.g., carbon steel, 316 stainless steel). Chemical compatibility charts provide supplier-based recommendations for the use of the product contact material with specific tested chemicals/heavy metals.

Testing by the supplier covers the evaluation of the contact material's physical properties (weight, dimension, appearance) for resistance to the chemical reagents. The physical characteristics of thermoplastics and elastomers are sensitive to temperature, therefore recommendations for chemical compatibility should indicate temperature limits or temperature test setting for chemicals tested. Pressure and chemical concentrations should also be included in the compatibility charts since they affect chemical compatibility.

Testing methods for chemical compatibility testing are driven by ASTM D543-95 and ISO 175.

Note: In-house evaluation should be performed if chemicals used with the contact materials are not provided on the chemical compatibility charts or if the contact environment (temperature, pressure, etc.) during manufacturing is outside of the test limits.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: A Three-Dimensional Assessment of Chemical Compatibility

Chemical compatibility assesses the physical resistance of contact materials in a specific chemical environment. The assessment must consider the combination of three dimensions:

  • Chemicals: acids, bases, organic solvents, cleaning agents, heavy metal ions
  • Temperature: the compatibility of thermoplastic elastomers can drop sharply at high temperature
  • Pressure and concentration: high pressure or high concentration can accelerate material degradation

Analogy

Chemical compatibility is like choosing the material of kitchen gloves: rubber gloves can handle diluted detergent, but if used with acetone they may swell and fail within minutes. The same gloves that are fine at 20°C may soften and deform immediately in a solution at 80°C. The chemical compatibility chart for a SUS component is the reference you must consult before "choosing the gloves".

Key Notes

A supplier chemical compatibility chart covers only the chemicals they have tested. If your process uses chemicals not listed in the chart, or your operating temperature/pressure exceeds the tested range, you must perform your own compatibility verification. This cannot be skipped, because the consequence of failure is that the component degrades during processing and directly contaminates the product.

This is also why 316L stainless steel tubing fittings need special confirmation in some SUS systems: leaching of metal ions (iron, nickel, chromium) from stainless steel may affect metal-sensitive biological products.

Application

When selecting SUS components, build a chemical compatibility assessment matrix: list all contact chemicals (including CIP/SIP cleaning agents) × component materials × operating temperature range, and check each one against the supplier compatibility chart. Incompatible or untested combinations require supplementary in-house testing or a change of material.

8.10 Attribute 10: Protein Adsorption Studies

Original Text

Currently, there are no broadly accepted standards for protein adsorption studies; however, such testing is necessary for understanding the impact that a polymeric system may have on the biochemical processes they are in contact with. This section intends to define an expectation rather than defining specifications.

Consideration should be given to utilizing one of two large molecular weight commercially available products for testing, insulin and heparin. These compounds are readily available, as are methods for detecting these compounds.

Alternate but equivalent methods could also be used, such as the use of bovine immunoglobulin or bovine serum albumin measured by direct quantitative amino acid analysis. In such studies, more than one temperature for storage and multiple time points should be used. It is also crucial that the surface area to volume ratio is presented in the data.

For the test results to be meaningful, the testing should be performed on components that are assembled in the manner suitable for delivery and sterilized in the manner used for components delivered for use.

Understanding that protein adsorption will occur (such as adsorption onto borosilicate glass), studies are expected using model proteins at more than one temperature point and at multiple time points to understand the kinetics of the adsorption. The adsorption is not only a function of the material (such as ultralow density PE) but also the physical characteristics of the surface, which could be altered by the sterilization technique. Comparison studies should be performed side by side with stainless steel and glass systems to form a baseline for understanding variations from laboratory to laboratory.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Mechanisms and Impact of Protein Adsorption

Protein adsorption is the physical/chemical interaction between protein molecules and a contact surface, causing the protein to "disappear" from solution onto the surface. Its impacts on biopharmaceutical processes include:

  • Product loss: valuable mAb or therapeutic protein is adsorbed onto bag walls and the inner walls of tubing, lowering the effective dose
  • Aggregation: adsorption-desorption cycles may induce conformational changes in the protein and promote the formation of aggregates
  • Immunogenicity: protein released after adsorption may have a different immunogenicity profile

Test Design Requirements

Recommended model proteins:
  - Insulin: small molecule, easy to detect
  - Heparin: large molecule, charged

Alternatives:
  - Bovine immunoglobulin (bIgG)
  - Bovine serum albumin (BSA)
  - Quantification by amino acid analysis

Key points of study design:
  ≥2 storage temperatures
  Multiple time points (kinetic data)
  The surface area to volume ratio (S/A:V) must be reported

Controls:
  Stainless steel system + glass system (borosilicate glass)
  → establishes a baseline for cross-laboratory comparison

Analogy

Protein adsorption is like grease sticking in an oven: different pan materials (non-stick coating vs. plain steel) show different levels of sticking, and the higher the temperature and the longer the contact time, the more sticks. A pan with a large surface area (high S/A:V) also collects more in total than a small one. The surface properties of SUS materials (e.g., ultra-low-density PE) may change after gamma irradiation, just as the non-stick coating of a pan gets damaged after several uses and sticking increases.

Key Notes

There is currently no widely accepted standard method, which means the assessment of this attribute relies more on scientific judgment than on mechanical compliance. When designing the study, you must justify that the chosen model protein is relevant to your target drug in terms of adsorption behavior. High-concentration protein drugs (e.g., mAb formulations at >100 mg/mL) are often sensitive to adsorption in a completely different way from low-concentration ones and need special attention.

8.11 Attribute 11: Endotoxin Testing

Original Text

Endotoxin testing per USP <85> and EP 2.6.14 is a requirement for single-use products with product contact which need to be sterile and pyrogen free, per the intended application. USP <85> and EP 2.6.14 utilize Limulus Amebocyte Lysate (LAL) testing to demonstrate the absence of pyrogens (for the purpose of this Guide, endotoxins and pyrogens are considered equivalent terms).

For the test results to be meaningful, the testing should be performed on components that are assembled in the manner suitable for delivery and sterilized in the manner used for components delivered for use.

The supplier can also provide a statement indicating the systems and processes in place to reduce or control endotoxins. The statement should indicate that assembly occurs in a classified environment with appropriately gowned personnel, or that a visual inspection process is used for particulates. Periodic testing should be performed to demonstrate that the process successfully produces material that meets the endotoxin requirements. The document should indicate the frequency of the testing.

In the absence of other requirements, the material should pass USP WFI endotoxin standards when tested with USP WFI.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Relationship Between Endotoxin and Pyrogen

Endotoxin is the lipopolysaccharide (LPS) component of the cell wall of Gram-negative bacteria and is the most important pyrogenic substance. The guide states explicitly that, in the context of SUS evaluation, endotoxin and pyrogen are treated as synonyms.

The LAL test (Limulus amebocyte lysate test) is currently the most sensitive pharmacopeia-recognized method for detecting endotoxin, with a sensitivity of down to 0.005 EU/mL.

Hazard of endotoxin: even at trace concentrations (ng/kg body weight), intravenous administration can cause a severe systemic inflammatory response (SIRS) that is life-threatening.

Testing and Process Control Requirements

Basic requirements:
  Test standard: USP <85> / EP 2.6.14 (LAL method)
  Test samples: consistent with the as-supplied state (including irradiation status)
  General acceptance criterion: ≤ USP WFI endotoxin limit

Process controls the supplier must declare:
  1. Component assembly environment: cleanroom grade + personnel gowning control
  2. Visual particulate inspection procedure
  3. Documented periodic testing frequency

Important:
  One-off test reports without a stated frequency are not accepted
  Ongoing monitoring data are required, not only validation data

Analogy

Endotoxin control is like Salmonella testing in food manufacturing: you cannot run a test once when the plant is set up and then assume the problem will never appear. Every batch needs to be tested, and the production environment (controlled environment + gowning rules) must be continuously maintained to control the source of endotoxin contamination (Gram-negative bacteria). The endotoxin control logic for SUS components is exactly the same.

Key Notes

Endotoxin is not eliminated by common sterilization methods (e.g., 25 kGy gamma irradiation): irradiation kills bacteria, but endotoxin consists of cell wall fragments released after the bacteria die, and it can only be degraded by dry heat at 250°C or a dedicated depyrogenation treatment. The assembly environment and water quality control of the components are therefore the key to endotoxin control, not terminal irradiation sterilization.

8.12 Attribute 12: Sterilization (Irradiation)

Original Text

Robust evidence/data should be provided by the supplier to support the sterility claim (SAL) for the single-use product. Sterilization validation should be based on ANSI/AAMI/ISO 11137.

The sterilization validation package should include:

  • Validation performed per ANSI/AAMI/ISO 11137 methods
  • Method used to establish minimum sterilization dose (e.g., Method 1, VDmax25, etc.)
  • Demonstration of adherence to method used
  • Dose setting/dose substantiation
  • Pre-sterilization bioburden determination (per ISO 11737-1)
  • Verification dose experiment (per ISO 11737-2 for sterility testing)
  • Auditing of sterilization dose (demonstration of continued process effectiveness)
  • Frequency each testing is performed

For selection of the single-use product used for validation:

  • Product family definition/matrixing
  • Product that represents the product family (e.g., master, equivalent, or simulated product)
  • Criteria for defining the product family
  • Plan for maintaining product family
  • Determination and adequacy of SIP selection

Bacteriostasis/fungistasis testing is required by USP <71> for sterility testing, in order to:

  • Validate the sterility test to be used for verification dose experiments
  • Demonstrate that the product material itself does not inhibit growth
  • Demonstrate that product bioburden can be detected in the sterility test

Validation testing to support sterility shelf life (per ANSI/AAMI/ISO 11607-1) includes:

  • Real time aging
  • Accelerated aging (refer to ASTM F1980)
  • Physical and functional testing and/or sterility testing
  • Leak testing: Pressure decay (ASTM F2338-09), Seal integrity-peel (ASTM F88/F88-M-15), Transportation/shipping integrity, Visual inspection (ASTM F1886-98), Dye penetration (ASTM F1929-98)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: SAL and Irradiation Dose Setting

Sterility assurance level (SAL) is the indicator that quantitatively describes sterilization effectiveness. Medical devices and drug-contact components generally require SAL = 10⁻⁶ (no more than 1 in a million units may contain a surviving microorganism).

Gamma sterilization dose setting methods (ISO 11137):

  • Method 1: a full statistical method based on measured microbial resistance data
  • VDmax25: a simplified substantiation method for components with low bioburden, confirming that 25 kGy achieves SAL 10⁻⁶

Elements of Sterile Shelf-Life Validation

ANSI/AAMI/ISO 11607-1 framework:

Sterile barrier system:
  → the minimum package that prevents microbial ingress
  → validation: microbial barrier test + seal strength

Protective packaging:
  → the outer layer that protects the sterile barrier system
  → validation: transport integrity testing

Aging tests (either one or both in parallel):
  Real-time aging: most direct, but time-consuming
  Accelerated aging: per ASTM F1980
  → temperature coefficient Q10 = 2 (reaction rate doubles for every 10°C rise)

Seal integrity test methods:
  Pressure decay, dye penetration, helium leak, tensile strength

Analogy

Irradiation sterilization validation is like shelf-life testing of food: you need to confirm not only that the food is safe now (when just produced), but also that it is still safe (sterile) at the end of its labeled shelf life (say, 2 years later). Product family matrixing is like the "representative product" concept in the food industry: you do not need to run a full test on every flavor of cookie, only to select the most difficult-to-sterilize representative for full validation, and the others in the same family can be covered by a matrix approach.

Key Notes

Bacteriostasis/fungistasis testing (USP <71>) is a prerequisite for confirming the validity of the sterility test itself: if the material of a SUS component inhibits microbial growth in the culture medium, a "no growth" result cannot be used as proof of sterility. This is the method suitability test of USP <71> and must be completed before the verification dose experiment.

Practice Questions

  1. Your supplier validated using the VDmax25 method. They later added a new component assembly room to the plant, where the cleanliness of the assembly environment is somewhat lower, which may increase the pre-sterilization bioburden. Does this change require the irradiation dose to be re-substantiated?
  2. In accelerated aging testing, storage at 40°C for 12 weeks is claimed to be equivalent to storage at 25°C for 2 years. Explain the assumptions behind this calculation and the circumstances in which these assumptions may not hold.
8.13 Attribute 13: Container Closure Integrity

Original Text

Container closure integrity (CCI) validation demonstrates the effectiveness of the sterile boundary or closure system against microbial ingress by means of a specific challenge. At a minimum, the closure system should be qualified by at least one of the following test methods (as appropriate for the container design):

  • Helium leak test: Helium is used as a tracer gas for detection and measurement of leakage across a package seal, detected by mass spectrometry (ASTM E498-95, ASTM E499-95).
  • Liquid ingress test: A liquid tracer (dye, radionucleotide ions) is exposed to the test seal. Migration of the tracer across the seal is determined visually (with dyes) or by other analytical means (ASTM E165-02).
  • Vacuum decay test: Containers are placed inside a chamber where a vacuum is applied. Once steady vacuum is reached, it is monitored to detect any leak from the test container (ASTM F2338-09).
  • Pressure retention test: Container pressure retention is measured over time inside the container using a pressure measuring device.
  • Tensile test: Used for seal quality including connection strength, closure coring, IV bag port connection strength, and residual seal force. Test samples are stretched at a constant rate to a predefined load (ASTM F88/F88-M-15).
  • Compression test: Used to test plastic tube or bag seal quality and strength by compressing at a constant rate to a predefined load.
  • Microbial challenge tests: Per liquid immersion or aerobiology methods (USP <71>).
  • Residual seal force: Established for specific vial/stopper combinations, confirming compression levels on the closure. Required in addition to component closure integrity qualification for all final finished products.

Test sample sizes should be representative of the lot sizes being requested. For additional information, refer to USP <1207>, USP <1>, and ASTM E432-91.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Purpose of CCI and the Logic of Test Selection

The core purpose of container closure integrity (CCI) validation is to demonstrate that the closure system can resist microbial ingress, that is, maintain sterility. Different CCI test methods are suited to different types of leaks and container designs:

  • Deterministic methods: helium leak, vacuum decay, pressure hold; based on physical measurement, highly sensitive, and requiring no dyes or microorganisms
  • Probabilistic methods: dye ingress, microbial challenge; they rely on chemical/biological indicators and are statistical in nature

The industry trend is moving from probabilistic to deterministic methods (the FDA 2016 guidance prefers them).

CCI Test Method Selection Guide

Rigid containers (vials, cartridges):
  → Vacuum decay (ASTM F2338)
  → Helium mass spectrometry leak test
  → Residual seal force (applicable only to stopper/vial combinations)

Flexible containers (SUS bags):
  → Pressure hold test
  → Dye ingress (for validation)
  → Tensile test (seal strength)

Tubing connections:
  → Compression test (bag closure)
  → Tensile test (connector joint strength)

High-sensitivity needs:
  → Helium leak (lowest detectable ~10⁻⁹ cc He/s)

Analogy

CCI testing is like an airtightness test of a car tire: you can use soapy water to see whether bubbles form (equivalent to the dye method, probabilistic), or use a precision instrument to measure the rate of pressure decay (equivalent to vacuum decay, deterministic). For sterile products, what we need is not only "no leak now" but "no bacterial ingress throughout the whole shelf life", which calls for the more rigorous deterministic methods.

Key Notes

USP <1207> is an important reference chapter for container closure integrity testing, covering the classification and selection logic of deterministic and probabilistic methods. Residual seal force is a requirement specific to glass vial/rubber stopper systems; it does not apply to SUS bags, but may be relevant to finished products that use SUS fittings to connect to stoppers and vials.

Practice Questions

  1. The SUS process bag you use has a volume of 50 L and the batch size is 200 bags. How many samples should your CCI test plan use to represent this batch? What does USP <1207> recommend in this regard?
  2. Your SUS bags passed the dye ingress CCI test, but in the microbial challenge test 1 bag showed microbial ingress. How do you explain the difference between these two results? What does it say about the limitations of the dye ingress test?
8.14 Attribute 14: Particulate Testing

Original Text

Particulates, both loose visible and non-visible to the extent defined by USP, can contaminate or adulterate the product if there are no intervening steps between use of the component and fill/finish. Therefore, polymeric materials should be free from particulate matter.

Particulate testing should be performed for polymeric materials having product contact per USP <788>, EP 2.9.19 and EP 2.9.20, as a starting point. The potential to shed particles into the process due to the intended application will require additional qualification, including additional information concerning sub-visible particles and the composition of any particulates found.

USP <788> utilizes light obscuration particle testing, and the methodology is appropriate for large volume parenterals for single dose infusion.

EP 2.9.19 allows for the use of both light obscuration and the microscopic methods.

For the test results to be meaningful, the testing should be performed on components that are assembled in the manner suitable for delivery and sterilized in the manner used for components delivered for use. Results should be presented as actual data, not simply as "Passed".

In addition, a statement should be provided by the supplier indicating the systems and processes in place to reduce or control particulates. The statement should indicate that assembly occurs in a classified environment with appropriately gowned personnel, or that a visual inspection process is used for particulates. Periodic testing should be performed to demonstrate that the process successfully produces material that meets the particulate requirements. The statement should indicate the frequency of the testing.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Three Sources and Classes of SUS Particulates

Particulate testing targets particles that may be shed from SUS component materials into the product. Particulates are classified by size:

  • Visible particulates (>100 µm): visible to the naked eye and an immediate non-compliance
  • Subvisible particulates (1-100 µm): detected by light obscuration, the main target of USP <788>
  • Submicron particulates (<1 µm): in the nanoparticle range, requiring special techniques (NTA, DLS, etc.); there are currently no widely applied pharmacopeial requirements

Pharmacopeial Limits

USP <788> injectable products (large-volume injections, LVP):
  ≥10 µm: ≤25 particles/mL
  ≥25 µm: ≤3 particles/mL

EP 2.9.19 injectable products (same as USP <788>):
  ≥10 µm: ≤25 particles/mL
  ≥25 µm: ≤3 particles/mL

EP 2.9.20 (for ophthalmic preparations):
  ≥10 µm: ≤50 particles/mL

Note: rinse solution testing of SUS components must meet the limits above
Actual data (particles/mL) must be reported; "Pass" alone is not accepted

Analogy

SUS particulate shedding is like color bleeding from new clothes: you cannot simply confirm there is no dye on a new garment on the shelf; you also need to simulate actual use conditions (washing, rubbing) to assess whether the dye transfers to other clothing. Similarly, a SUS bag may show few particulates at rest, but in actual use (mixing, peristaltic pump operation, temperature cycling) it may shed more particulates due to mechanical stress.

Key Notes

"Using the pharmacopeial limits as a starting point" means that the pharmacopeial limits are only the minimum requirement. For applications where final filling is done directly in the SUS (with no subsequent filtration step), the particulate criteria may need to be stricter than the pharmacopeia. In addition, if particles are found in particulate testing, they must be identified (e.g., by SEM-EDX or FTIR) to confirm whether they are shed from the SUS material; this is critical for root cause analysis.

Application

When evaluating a new SUS bag, in addition to static particulate testing, dynamic particulate testing should also be considered: simulate actual process conditions (mixing RPM, duration, temperature) and then measure the particle count of the liquid in the bag. This better reflects real use risk than a static rinse test, especially for bioreactor bags used in long-duration agitated cultures.

8.15 Attribute 15: Calibration of Embedded Instrumentation

Original Text

For single-use products that have embedded instrumentation providing data utilized for controlling and monitoring processes, documentation should include:

  • Traceable calibration certificates (per instrument) that comply with international standards such as NIST (National Institutes of Standards and Technology) and UKAS (United Kingdom Accreditation Service) and include the calibration expiration date
  • Verification that the instruments can be independently calibrated pre and post-use
  • Calibration procedures, including a specific calibration range and loop tolerance details of the integrated instrument (element, indicator, etc.)
  • Data regarding the shelf life and whether the instrument can be recycled
  • Data regarding the stability of the embedded instrumentation to withstand cleaning and sterilization processes
  • If required, technical verification regarding connectivity of the instrument to end-user control systems

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Special Challenges of Embedded SUS Instrumentation

Modern SUS components increasingly integrate embedded sensors, such as:

  • Single-use pH sensors (integrated in the bag or line)
  • Dissolved oxygen (DO) sensors (bioreactor bags)
  • Pressure/temperature sensors
  • Turbidity sensors (for cell culture monitoring)

The challenge with these sensors is that they are irradiation-sterilized together with the bag and discarded with it after use (single use), so the traditional instrument management framework of "pre-use/post-use calibration" needs to be rethought.

Documentation Requirements for Embedded Instruments

Required documentation list:
  1. Traceable calibration certificate
     → conforming to NIST or UKAS standards
     → includes the calibration expiry date

  2. Pre-use/post-use independent calibration verification method
     → e.g., a 1-point verification using a known pH standard solution

  3. Detailed calibration procedure
     → calibration range (e.g., pH 2-12)
     → loop tolerance

  4. Shelf-life data
     → effect of storage conditions on sensor accuracy
     → whether reuse is permitted or prohibited

  5. Resistance data
     → irradiation sterilization (typically 25-40 kGy)
     → resistance to cleaning/disinfection

Analogy

An embedded SUS sensor is like a disposable blood glucose test strip: the strip itself has been calibrated at the factory, you run a quick verification with a standard solution before each use, and you discard it afterwards without sending it back to the factory for recalibration. But you need to make sure that the strip is within its expiry date (shelf life), stored correctly, and that the manufacturer has provided traceable batch calibration data. If the strip gets damp (equivalent to a sensor stored under unsuitable conditions), the reading may be biased even within the expiry date.

Key Notes

NIST (US) and UKAS (UK) are the most widely recognized metrology standards bodies, and calibration traceability must be linked back to these national standards. In a GMP environment, an embedded sensor whose calibration has expired must not be used, even if it is physically intact. In PAT (process analytical technology) applications, the calibration status of embedded sensors directly affects process acceptability and is a high-focus item in FDA/EMA regulatory audits.

Application

When introducing SUS components containing embedded sensors, the following SOP framework needs to be established:

  • Incoming calibration verification procedure (integrated with the existing calibration management system)
  • Electronic recording of calibration data (including lot number, calibration date, expiry date)
  • Failure handling procedure (disposition path for expired calibration or abnormal readings)
  • Connection verification with DCS/SCADA systems (IQ/OQ documentation)
  • Waste management (some sensors contain special materials that require special disposal)

Practice Questions

  1. Your SUS bioreactor bag has a built-in dissolved oxygen sensor, but the supplier provides only lot-level factory calibration data and no individual calibration certificate for each sensor. Does this meet GMP requirements? What should you ask the supplier to provide?
  2. You find a lot of SUS pH sensor bags in your plant inventory whose calibration validity has expired, although the bags look physically intact. What is your QA decision? Is there any flexible way to "extend" the validity?

Appendix 4 Section Summary

  • Biocompatibility (Attr.1): USP <87>/<88> or ISO 10993-1, with actual scores required (not just "Pass"); Class VI plastics with extraction at 70°C; the supplier must provide a complete report from a GLP-accredited laboratory.
  • Mechanical properties & gas transmission (Attr.2-3): documented test results per ASTM F2097 and the BPSA matrix; gas transmission data (OTR, WVTR) must include full test conditions (film thickness, temperature, pressure, RH), and any deviation from the ASTM method must be clearly explained.
  • Compendial physicochemical testing (Attr.4): select USP <661> or the EP 3.1.x series by polymer type; the test matrix differs between polymers (see Table 8.1); IR spectroscopy confirms material identity and prevents material substitution.
  • Animal origin control (Attr.5): TSE/BSE risk control is the core; any component of animal origin (including lubricants and cleaning agents) requires an EDQM CEP document; the geographical BSE risk category and the infectivity tissue category must be assessed.
  • TOC, pH, conductivity (Attr.6-7): all three are non-specific consistency indicators whose main function is to establish baseline values during validation and then serve as screening indicators for triggering investigations in later incoming monitoring; the test sample state must match the as-supplied state.
  • E&L (Attr.8): a design space-oriented approach (not the traditional USP <381>/<661> route); combined studies of polar/non-polar solvents, low and high pH, different temperatures and different contact times; the combination of GC-MS + HPLC-MS + ICP-MS is the minimum requirement; substances exceeding the ICH Q3C Class 1 limits must be identified; silicone residues can suppress filter bubble point.
  • Chemical compatibility & protein adsorption (Attr.9-10): chemical compatibility must cover the three dimensions of chemicals × temperature × pressure; contact conditions outside the chart range must be evaluated by the user. There is currently no standard method for protein adsorption studies; they need multiple temperatures + multiple time points + a reported S/A:V ratio, with side-by-side comparison against stainless steel/glass systems to establish a baseline.
  • Endotoxin (Attr.11): LAL method (USP <85>/EP 2.6.14); control of the assembly environment is more critical than terminal sterilization (irradiation does not eliminate endotoxin); periodic testing with a documented frequency is required.
  • Irradiation sterilization validation (Attr.12): per ISO 11137; includes dose setting method (Method 1 or VDmax25), bioburden determination, product family matrix design, bacteriostasis/fungistasis testing, and shelf-life validation (real-time + accelerated aging).
  • Container closure integrity (Attr.13): validate with at least one CCI test method; deterministic methods (helium leak, vacuum decay) are preferred over probabilistic methods (dye ingress); select the appropriate method by container type; refer to USP <1207>.
