  • Particulate testing (Attr.14): USP <788>/EP 2.9.19 as a starting point; actual data (particles/mL) must be reported and "Pass" alone is not accepted; test samples must match the as-supplied state; direct-fill applications need to consider stricter criteria.
  • Calibration of embedded instrumentation (Attr.15): a calibration certificate traceable to NIST/UKAS + expiry date; the ability to verify pre-use/post-use independent calibration; irradiation/cleaning resistance data; technical verification documentation for the connection to the end-user control system.
6

Section 6: App 5: Case Study – 2000L SUB

p147-p158

ISPE Good Practice Guide: Single-Use Technology | p147 - p158

Learning Objectives for This Chapter

  • Understand the technology selection process for a 2000 L-scale SUB (single-use bioreactor), including qualitative assessment, quantitative assessment (blend time, kLa, CO₂ stripping, cell performance) and the weighted-scoring decision method
  • Understand the engineering challenges of installing a large SUB in an existing GMP facility, including space constraints, construction access management, and the hybrid design strategy combining stainless steel and single-use systems
  • Understand the new risks introduced by the move to single-use technology (leaks, supply chain dependency, inventory management) and their mitigations (FMEA, training batches, process flow map)
  • Learn how a two-phase renovation project can systematically capture and apply lessons learned (AAR), quantifying communication, documentation and installation issues and acting on them
  • Build a decision framework for adopting an SUB platform at a large-scale biologics CDMO: a complete view from vendor selection and facility modification to validation and supply chain management
9.1 Summary

Original Text

GlaxoSmithKline's biopharmaceuticals clinical manufacturing facility in Upper Merion, PA was renovated to install single-use bioreactor (SUB) systems and establish a new global manufacturing platform strategy for scale-out at 2000 L. The latest advances in 2000 L SUB technologies were assessed through a comprehensive selection process based on qualitative and quantitative attributes of three suppliers. Quantitative results were compared to performance of an existing 1200 L scale stainless steel bioreactor.

GlaxoSmithKline first installed the selected 2000 L SUB technology in one of two similar existing cell culture suites. This approach minimized plant downtime and provided the opportunity to apply lessons learned in a subsequent phase for installation of two 2000 L SUBs in the second cell culture suite. The design included a hybrid approach using stainless steel and single-use systems for large-scale media prep and transfer operations.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Strategic Background to Adopting a 2000 L SUB

The GSK Upper Merion case represents a typical industry milestone in the shift from stainless steel (SS) to single-use technology (SUT). At the time (around 2018), 2000 L was the technical boundary for commercial SUBs: larger than the 1000 L pilot scale, but not yet at the scale of traditional 10,000–20,000 L stainless steel fermenters.

The core logic of the scale-out strategy: rather than using one very large bioreactor (scale-up), replicate several 2000 L SUBs, which lowers the risk of batch failure and improves scheduling flexibility.

Executing in two phases was an important risk management decision: pilot in Suite 1 first, then renovate Suite 2 once the lessons were learned, so the organization could "learn while doing" rather than bet everything at once.

Analogy

Imagine upgrading your company's phone system from traditional landlines to cloud VoIP. You would not replace every phone in the company on the same day (too risky); you would trial it in one department first and roll it out company-wide once it proved stable. GSK's two-phase SUB adoption follows the same logic: Suite 1 is the test field and Suite 2 is the harvest field.

CDMO Application Scenario

For a CDMO, the strategic significance of a 2000 L SUB platform is:

  • Customer flexibility: different molecules can use the same platform, which reduces cross-contamination risk and speeds up product changeover
  • Bridge to commercialization: a smooth transition from clinical scale (500 L) to commercial scale (multiple 2000 L units) without building a new plant
  • Capital efficiency: the capital expenditure of an SUB is far lower than that of a stainless steel system of the same scale, which suits the multi-client business model of a CDMO
9.2 SUB Technology Selection

Original Text

Initial qualitative evaluation determined which SUB vendors would be selected for a more extensive quantitative evaluation. Results of comprehensive qualitative and quantitative evaluations were summarized in a weighted scoring system for a decision on the selected supplier.

9.2.1 Qualitative Evaluation

The SUB selection team implemented the following qualitative evaluation criteria for the 2000 L SUBs:

  • Ergonomics and ease of operation, including the bag installation.
  • Flexibility and options available in the bag design, including agitation and sparging choices.
  • Industry and commercial experience of each vendor, including gathering third party experiences and references.
  • Scale down-representation of different SUB sizes available to allow process development and transfer to production scale.
  • Vendor supply chain robustness, including lead times for standard and custom bags.
  • Vendor support and experience at the local level and at other sites in GlaxoSmithKline's international network.

9.2.2 Quantitative Evaluation

GlaxoSmithKline evaluated SUBs from three established vendors at capacities ranging from 200 L to 1000 L scale and compared the results to their existing 1200 L stainless steel bioreactor.

Quantitative evaluations involved hands-on testing in non-GMP areas at the Upper Merion site. Testing included measurement of blend time at full and partial volumes, as well as mass transfer capabilities. Cell growth and product titers were compared to the existing 1200 L stainless steel bioreactor using a platform cell line.

A third-party consultant modeled the SUBs at the 2000 L scale using computational fluid dynamics.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: A Two-Stage Vendor Selection Framework

GSK used a two-stage selection of "qualitative screening, then quantitative in-depth evaluation", which is standard industry practice for vendor assessment:

  • Qualitative screening: quickly eliminates vendors that do not meet basic requirements (ergonomics, supply chain capability, representativeness of the scale-down model)
  • Quantitative in-depth evaluation: quantifiable engineering tests on the shortlisted vendors, reducing subjective judgment
  • Weighted scoring: combines the qualitative and quantitative results into a comparable total score, so the decision is transparent and auditable

Note: the quantitative evaluation was performed in a non-GMP area. This was a key decision: no GMP resources are tied up before purchase, which lowers cost and disruption.

Key Point: The Special Importance of Supply Chain Assessment

Among the qualitative criteria, Vendor supply chain robustness was listed as an evaluation item. This began to receive attention around 2018; after COVID-19, the risk of supply chain disruption became the number one barrier to SUB adoption.

When assessing the supply chain, look at: Is there a single bag film supplier? Does a backup manufacturing site exist? How many irradiation sites are there?

Analogy: Why Scale-Down Matters

Scale-down representativeness is like a "practice track" in running training. The real race is 2000 L, but you first need to practice and optimize parameters on a "scaled-down track" of 200 L or 500 L. If the vendor has no reliable scale-down model, you cannot effectively develop and transfer the process before production scale goes live.

CDMO Application Scenario: Platform Standardization

When selecting an SUB, a CDMO should also consider the following, beyond the criteria GSK listed:

  • Multi-client compatibility: can the same SUB platform support different MAb, recombinant protein, gene therapy and other processes?
  • Regulatory support documentation: can the vendor provide an adequate E&L (extractables and leachables) data package to support multiple INDs?
  • Consistency of global supply: if the CDMO has sites in Taiwan or elsewhere in Asia, the vendor's local support capability is critical
9.2.2.1–9.2.2.3 Blend Time, kLa, and CO₂ Stripping

Original Text

9.2.2.1 Bioreactor Blend Time

Bioreactor blend times were tested to collect empirical data. The testing was determined by measuring the time required for a bolus of acid to be dispersed throughout the vessel. Replicate tests were conducted over the range of operating speeds. Measurements were made both at the lower tangent line and just below the liquid surface.

[Figure 9.1🔍: Example Blend Time Based on Whole Volume – Volume Uniformity of 1 Means 100% Mixing]

[Figure 9.2🔍: Blend Time Results – Reactor A Shows the Best Performance and Reactor C the Lowest]

9.2.2.2 Oxygen Mass Transfer Coefficient (kLa)

kLa was determined by monitoring the increase in dissolved oxygen in the vessel contents while sparging in air. The tests were conducted over a range of operating conditions by varying the agitator power input and gas sparge rate. Dissolved oxygen was measured both at the lower tangent line and just below the liquid surface level to assess spatial variation.

9.2.2.3 Carbon Dioxide (CO₂) Stripping

CO₂ stripping experiments were conducted over the range of gas rates (superficial gas velocity) and agitator speed (energy dissipation rate). The pH was monitored and its change versus time analyzed as a CO₂ saturated solution was stripped via air sparging. These measurements were also made both at the lower tangent line and just below the liquid surface level.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Three Key Fluid Dynamics Tests

These three tests form the core triangle of the physical performance assessment of a bioreactor:

  • Blend time: evaluates mixing efficiency, that is, whether pH adjusting agents, antifoam and glucose feed in the medium can be distributed quickly and uniformly. It is measured by tracking the dispersion time of an acid tracer.
  • kLa (oxygen mass transfer coefficient): describes how efficiently oxygen moves from the gas bubbles into the liquid phase. The higher the kLa, the less likely the cells are to become oxygen-limited in high-density culture.
  • CO₂ stripping efficiency: if the CO₂ produced by cell metabolism is not removed effectively, the culture pH goes out of control and cytotoxicity rises. This is one of the known weaknesses of SUBs, especially at large scale.

Key Point: The Two-Point Measurement Design

All tests were measured at two locations, "the tangent at the tank bottom" and "below the liquid surface", in order to assess spatial uniformity. Dead zones are a bigger problem in large bioreactors (especially 2000 L) than in small ones. A large difference between the two readings indicates uneven mixing, which affects the accuracy of both pH probe and DO probe readings.

Analogy: An Intuitive View of kLa

kLa is like a city's "logistics efficiency index": oxygen is the cargo, cells are the factory and bubbles are the transport vehicles. A high kLa means fast logistics and a factory that never runs short of raw material; a low kLa means congested logistics and a constrained factory. The challenge for a large SUB is that as volume increases, bubbles need a longer rise path and kLa may fall, so the sparger design must compensate.

Practice Questions

  1. When scaling up from 200 L to 2000 L, how does kLa usually change? How would you adjust operating conditions to compensate?
  2. How does insufficient CO₂ stripping affect pH probe readings? What are the direct consequences for cell metabolism?
9.2.2.4–9.2.2.6 Results, CFD, and Cell Performance

Original Text

9.2.2.4 kLa and CO₂ Results

The results were obtained and correlated for the tested volume. These correlations were used to extrapolate to the 2000 L scale. Significant differences were observed between the different systems tested. The greater differences were seen for the oxygen mass transfer, lesser differences for the CO₂ stripping. The sparger design was determined to be a contributing factor for the differences observed. One system tested outperformed the stainless-steel control system.

9.2.2.5 Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD)

CFD modeling was performed to predict the performance of the three mixing vessel designs at the 2000 L scale. This involved breaking each SUB into 3–5 million units with a mesh size of 1–2 cm and then modeled. This solved for the time accurate mixing behavior of each vessel by introducing a virtual tracer into the mesh at a location comparable with the experimental data and then comparing tracer concentrations and mixing times with the experimental data.

9.2.2.6 Cell Performance Testing

Cell performance was tested in the three SUBs using an established CHO cell process. All three SUBs used the same media lot and seed pool to eliminate these factors as potential variables. The testing evaluated cell growth and viability, titer, metabolic profile and product quality in each SUB. Results were compared to historic performance in the 1200 L stainless-steel bioreactor. Reactor A was comparable or better than the stainless-steel system. Reactor C consistently underperformed. Reactor B underperformed during one run due to a pH probe failure.

[Figure 9.3🔍: Viable Cell Count and Titer Performance versus the Stainless Steel Control]

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Methodology for Extrapolating to 2000 L

Because testing directly at 2000 L is very costly, GSK used a combined strategy of "small-scale testing plus CFD extrapolation":

  • Experimental data (200–1000 L) establish the correlation equations for kLa and CO₂
  • The CFD model (3–5 million mesh cells) predicts 2000 L behavior, and its accuracy is verified against the small-scale experimental data
  • The two verify each other, lowering the risk of full-scale testing

Sparger design is the main cause of kLa differences: pore size, number of pores and placement determine bubble size and mass transfer area. This directly informs SUB purchasing decisions.

Key Point: The Systemic Implication of the pH Probe Failure

In Reactor B, a pH probe failure made the data from one run unusable. This is not just an equipment fault; it reveals a systemic risk in the reliability of single-use probes in SUBs. In a stainless steel system, a probe can be calibrated and replaced many times; in an SUB, the probe is usually pre-installed with the bag, so a failure means replacing the whole assembly.

This also explains why validation testing of the pH probe and DO probe is critical in the IQ/OQ of an SUB.

Analogy: CFD Is Like a Flight Simulator

Before flying a real aircraft, pilots practice various scenarios in a simulator. CFD plays the same role for a 2000 L SUB: before the real equipment is purchased and installed, a computer simulates the fluid behavior to foresee possible dead zones and mixing problems. The finer the mesh (1–2 cm), the more accurate the simulation, but the longer the computing time.

CDMO Application Scenario: Regulatory Defensibility of the Selection Decision

GSK's weighted scoring system design is worth borrowing for a CDMO:

  • The decision process is documented, so in a client audit or regulatory inspection you can show "how we came to select this vendor"
  • Cell performance data are compared directly with the existing SS system, providing bridging data for technology transfer
  • The CFD extrapolation method can serve as the scientific basis of a scale-up validation strategy, reducing regulatory questions
9.2.3 Vendor Comparison Overview

Original Text

The score card below illustrates the weighted ranking system that was used to objectively evaluate each SUB platform. Three factors receive the highest weight factor: cell performance, mixing/kLa and vendor supply chain. Vendor support had the lowest weight factor.

[Figure 9.4🔍: Assessment of Vendors Using a Weighted Scoring System]

In some cases, a reactor received multiple scores for the same criteria. Reactor A vendor support, for example, received a 5 for US support but a 1 for UK support. Total weighted score for Reactor A was considerably higher than the scores for the other two SUB systems, and was chosen as the technology for installation in the Upper Merion clinical manufacturing facility.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Three Highest Weights in the Weighted Scoring

There is clear business logic behind the choice of the three highest-weighted items:

  • Cell performance: ultimately determines product quality and yield, and directly decides business success or failure
  • Mixing/kLa: the engineering foundation of cell performance; insufficient kLa = oxygen-starved cells = reduced performance, a predictable path to failure
  • Vendor supply chain: SUB bags are proprietary consumables, and a supply interruption = the whole platform stops, a very high business risk

"Vendor support" carries the lowest weight because it can be compensated for through contracts and training, whereas cell performance and supply chain are fundamental and hard to remedy after the fact.

Key Point: The Asymmetry of Regional Support

Reactor A scored 5 in the US but 1 in the UK. This shows that a multinational company or global CDMO must consider the consistency of the global support network when selecting equipment, rather than looking only at the service level in the main market. The vendor's local agent capability, warehouse location and technical support response time in each region should all be part of the assessment.

Practice Questions

  1. If you were a CDMO in Taiwan introducing a 2000 L SUB, which weights would you add or adjust in GSK's scoring criteria, and why?
  2. How do you ensure the objectivity of a weighted scoring system and prevent evaluators with a pre-existing preference from influencing the result?
9.3 Installation in an Existing GMP Facility

Original Text

9.3.1 Overview of the Existing Facility

GlaxoSmithKline's first 2000 L SUB was installed in Building 38 (UM-38), a multi-product clinical manufacturing facility in Upper Merion PA.

UM38 has GMP clinical operations on three floors, with process utilities at the basement level. The first floor includes microbial fermentation, with purification, media and buffer prep, wash area, laboratories and offices. The second floor includes two independent but similar mammalian cell culture suites, including a dedicated seed scale-up lab for each suite. Cell culture operations share a media prep area and wash area. Clarified harvest from the cell culture operations is transferred to the third floor, which includes two independent purification suites, supported by a shared buffer prep and wash area.

The floor-to-floor height of each production level is 16 ft., with 2 ft. of supporting steel, providing a clear height of 14 ft. below steel. Process operations on these floors typically have a 9 or 10 ft. ceiling, with distribution of process piping, utilities and ductwork in the remaining space above the ceiling. High hat areas provide higher ceiling elevations in specific locations that need to accommodate larger equipment.

A 2000 L SUB was installed in one of the two cell culture suites. The media prep area was also renovated to install single-use mixing systems.

9.3.2 Construction Access

Renovation of Cell Culture I and Media Prep was completed while maintaining existing operations in Cell Culture II. The return corridor was used during this renovation for construction access. Entry airlocks from the clean corridor were sealed with plastic sheathing. These soft barriers deterred construction personnel from entry into the clean corridor, but still allowed emergency egress in this direction.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Space Constraints in Renovating an Existing Facility

The building structure of UM-38 presents the typical challenges of an existing facility:

  • Effective clear height: nominal floor-to-floor height is 16 ft; deducting 2 ft of structural steel leaves 14 ft usable; deducting the process ceiling (9–10 ft) leaves only 4–5 ft of interstitial space for piping. A 2000 L SUB is usually taller than 10 ft and needs a special space (a high hat area)
  • High hat area: a locally raised space reserved for large equipment, and a key design asset when retrofitting an existing plant
  • Unidirectional flow: operators and materials enter from the clean corridor, and waste and personnel leave via the return corridor. This logic must be maintained during the renovation

Key Point: GMP Controls During Renovation

During construction, the entrance to the clean corridor was closed off with plastic sheathing, a typical "soft segregation" measure. The core principle is that the construction flow (construction workers, waste building materials) and the production flow (operators, materials) must be strictly separated, while emergency egress is preserved. In a real GMP environment, this kind of concurrent renovation requires a detailed CCR (construction compliance review) and continuous environmental monitoring.

CDMO Application Scenario: Assessing an Existing Plant for Retrofit

When assessing whether an existing plant is suitable for installing a large SUB, first confirm:

  • Whether a high hat area is available, or whether the cost of creating one is within budget
  • Whether the return corridor has enough space to serve as a construction route without affecting adjacent production areas
  • Whether the WFI and clean steam piping has the capacity margin to support the additional equipment
  • Whether the BAS (Building Automation System) has spare control points to integrate the new equipment
9.3.3–9.3.4 Suite 1 Renovation and Hybrid Approach

Original Text

9.3.3 Suite 1 Renovation

The existing cell culture suite had 100 L, 750 L, and 1200 L stainless steel bioreactors, and a 1500 L stainless steel harvest tank. The 1200 L bioreactor remained for ongoing comparisons of performance in a stainless steel and single-use bioreactors. The harvest tank was replaced with a larger 2500 L system to accommodate the new 2000 L SUB.

The 2000 L SUB was installed in the previous location of the 750 L bioreactor. This location was chosen because the existing high hat area was able to accommodate the larger SUB with minimal modifications to the ceiling and no impact to existing mechanical systems above the ceiling. The new 2500 L harvest tank aspect ratio was chosen to work within the existing high hat area elevation. The existing high hat width was expanded to accommodate a wider tank.

Renovations to the media prep area included removal of the 640 L and 1100 L stainless steel mix tanks and installation of single-use mixing systems for 50 L, 200 L, 650 L, and 1500 L media prep.

9.3.4 Stainless Steel and Single-Use Hybrid Approach

A hybrid approach was used for the 1500 L media prep operation. Powder additions to the top of the 1500 L mixing station would have required significant modifications to install a high hat in the ceiling, as well as additional footprint for platform access. A floor mounted stainless steel inline powder-liquid mixing system, typically used for much larger scale operations, was integrated with the 1500 L single-use bag. This eliminated the need for ceiling modifications, minimized floor space requirements and provided a more ergonomic design for powder additions.

Transfers of media from the 1500 L media prep to the 2000 L SUB were designed to use the existing stainless steel media transfer line, with minimal modifications to the existing system. Existing systems for cleaning and sterilization of equipment and piping made this hybrid approach possible with minimal impact to the facility.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Space Optimization Decisions in the Renovation

Several key design decisions in the Suite 1 renovation merit closer analysis:

  • Installing the 2000 L SUB in the 750 L position: uses the existing high hat and avoids major ceiling changes. This is a "minimum disruption, maximum use" design approach
  • Retaining the 1200 L SS bioreactor: serves as a benchmark (control) for long-term performance comparison, and also provides a business continuity safety net
  • Harvest tank from 1500 L to 2500 L: downstream equipment must match the upstream working volume, a detail that is easily overlooked in equipment integration design

Core Concept: The Stainless Steel and Single-Use Hybrid Strategy

The core logic of the hybrid approach: when a single-use system would require excessive facility modification cost for a particular operation (powder addition), keeping the stainless steel system is a rational choice.

The hybrid strategy depends on one precondition: the existing stainless steel system has CIP/SIP capability, so the interface with the new single-use system can be cleaned and sterilized. This allows the two systems to be integrated safely.

This case shows that "going single-use" does not mean "replacing everything", but "using the right technology in the right place".

Analogy: The Hybrid Strategy Is Like a Kitchen Refit

Imagine refitting a commercial kitchen. You do not need to replace all the equipment; you replace the workstations that most need flexibility (single-use technology) but keep the large fixed equipment (industrial ovens, central drainage). The key is that old and new equipment connect seamlessly so ingredients flow smoothly from one station to the next. The media transfer piping is the kitchen's "order pass-through system".

CDMO Application Scenario: GMP Impact of a Hybrid System

A hybrid system brings additional challenges to GMP documentation and validation:

  • The stainless steel-single-use interface needs a clearly defined "cleaning boundary" and "sterile boundary"
  • Integrity testing of the single-use system must be included in the batch record
  • Is the CIP validation of the existing SS piping still valid? The impact of the modification on cleaning effectiveness needs to be assessed
  • Scoping the IQ/OQ of a hybrid system: which parts fall under new equipment qualification, and which under requalification of the existing system?
9.3.5–9.3.7 Project Schedule, Support, and Phase II

Original Text

9.3.5 Project Schedule

Design and installation of single-use systems in the two areas was completed twenty-four months after the single-use technology was selected. Equipment was specified and procured early to minimize risk of construction delays. Equipment for the hybrid system was set up and tested in an existing non-GMP space prior to installation in the media prep area to minimize risk of delays in system start-up. Media prep construction was completed first to allow this area to support the existing operations in Cell Culture II.

9.3.6 Project Support

GSK internal support for this project included personnel from engineering, technology, manufacturing operations, quality, validation and calibration departments. Staffing peaked at twenty-five people during construction and validation. Generation of new protocols for validation of single-use systems created greater demands for personnel than normally expected with stainless steel systems, where existing protocols could be leveraged.

9.3.7 Phase II Renovations

A second phase of renovations was completed to install two 2000 L SUBs in Cell Culture II. This renovation also required a second renovation to the Media Prep area to install a second 1500 L media mixing station to accommodate the increased bioreactor capacity.

Unlike the first renovation, which focused on minimal renovations to the facility, this phase required significant modification to Cell Culture II to expand the area into the return corridor.

A platform was provided around the two 2000 L SUBs based on lessons learned from Phase I. This provided greater access to the top of the bioreactors for installation of filters and media transfer connections. The platform provided a clear height of 6 ft. 8 in. below the platform, which enabled operator access around the back of the bioreactors and also provided the ability to install control systems under the platform. Overall height of the area above the 2000 L SUBs is 13 ft. 9 in., just below the 14 ft. elevation at the bottom of building steel.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Managing a 24-Month Schedule

From technology selection to completion of installation took 24 months, a figure that is a useful reference for anyone planning a similar project:

  • Early procurement of equipment is the key to shortening the schedule: SUB equipment lead times can reach 6–12 months, and any delay becomes a critical-path bottleneck
  • Pre-testing the mixing system in a non-GMP area: do a "dry run" first and install afterwards, avoiding costly rework if problems are found in the GMP area
  • Completing the media preparation area first: ensures continued production support for Suite II, a decision that puts business continuity ahead of construction convenience

Key Point: The Hidden Cost of Validation Manpower

The project peak required 25 people of internal support. Note that the source specifically states that developing validation protocols for a single-use system required more manpower than for a stainless steel system, because there were no ready-made SOPs and protocols that could be reused directly.

This is a hidden cost that many organizations underestimate when assessing the cost of SUB adoption: developing validation documents from scratch, writing and reviewing new operating SOPs, and designing training programs all require substantial input from Quality, Engineering and Production.

Core Concept: Phase II Design Improvements - The Birth of the Platform

Phase I had no operating platform; Phase II added an operating platform around the 2000 L SUB based on lessons learned:

  • Function: provides access to the top of the bioreactor (installing filters, media transfer connections)
  • Dimensional design: clear height beneath the platform is 6 ft 8 in (allowing operators to pass behind the bioreactor); the height from the platform to the building steel is 13 ft 9 in (just within the 14 ft limit)
  • Additional benefit: the space beneath the platform can house the control system, making full use of the vertical space

This improvement shows that "the first build cannot always foresee every operating need", and that leaving room for improvement and an AAR mechanism to record issues is what matters.

CDMO Application Scenario: Manpower Planning and Schedule Estimation

Recommendations for CDMO management:

  • The validation manpower required to introduce a new technology is 30–50% higher than for an equipment replacement, so plan manpower early
  • Consider hiring external consultants with SUB validation experience (such as the role of Hargrove Life Sciences in this case) to speed up document development
  • The 24-month baseline schedule should be treated as the most optimistic estimate; the parallel management required in a complex CDMO environment (multiple clients, multiple products) may extend the schedule to 30–36 months
9.4 Project Risks and Mitigation - New Technology

Original Text

Changing from stainless steel operation to disposable technology introduced new risks such as potential leaks due to manual connections and improper handling of bags. In addition, new disposable systems needed to be created to move product within the plant. Working with disposable technology is a long-term relationship with your suppliers and relies on the robustness of their supply chain in addition to increased inventory management.

To mitigate these risks, water and engineering batches were conducted to train equipment operators in unpacking and loading the bags and in making the aseptic connections between disposables. A Failure Mode and Effects Analysis (FMEA) was conducted to assess equipment and operational SOPs, identify risks and generate a mitigation plan.

A detailed process flow map was created to show both major pieces of process equipment and the disposable systems needed to connect the equipment. The map showed tubing types, sizes and connectors needed and helped organize and consolidate the inventory of unique items required. The goal was to not have a specific tubing set for each process step but instead common ones that could be used in multiple areas.

[Figure 9.14🔍: Media Process Flow Map Indicating All Hose Requirements]

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Three New Risk Categories of Single-Use Technology

Moving from stainless steel to single-use technology fundamentally changes the risk pattern:

  • Operational risk: manual (aseptic) connections have a high skill threshold, and differences in operator skill translate directly into quality risk. CIP/SIP in stainless steel systems is relatively standardized, whereas single-use systems depend more on human judgment and technique.
  • Supply chain risk: SUB bags are proprietary consumables, and a supplier discontinuing production, a quality problem or a natural disaster can stop production directly. Stainless steel "equipment" can last 20 years, while bag "inventory" lasts only a few months.
  • Inventory management risk: bags have expiry dates and are scrapped once expired; bags are bulky, so storage costs are high; inaccurate demand forecasting leads to batch delays or waste.

Key Point: When to Perform the FMEA

In an SUT implementation, the FMEA should be completed before engineering qualification (EQ) and used as the basis for designing the qualification tests: the most critical failure modes (highest RPN) map to the corresponding qualification test items. The FMEA scope in the source covers both "equipment" and "operating SOPs", which is a complete view: equipment failure and human error must both be included.

Core Concept: The Design Philosophy of Standardized Tubing Sets

The design goal of the process flow map is "shared tubing sets" rather than "dedicated to each step", a key strategy for reducing SKU complexity:

  • Reduces the number of inventory items and lowers the risk of misuse errors
  • Improves purchasing negotiating power (larger order quantities)
  • Simplifies operator training (with the same tubing configuration, operators need to learn only one set of skills)

This standardization approach is especially important in a CDMO environment: a CDMO serves multiple clients, and if every client has a different tubing specification, inventory management gets out of control.

Practice Questions

  1. If you were to introduce a single-use transfer system into your current process, what do you think the failure mode with the highest RPN in the FMEA would be? How would you mitigate it?
  2. How would the "shared tubing set" strategy be implemented in a multi-client CDMO? How would you balance customer-specific requirements against standardization?
9.5 Vendor Supply Chain

Original Text

The vendor relationship and supply chain are critical to a successful conversion to single-use technology. Tubing sets can be generic and can be sourced from multiple vendors to ensure continuity of supply. Bags for SUBs are proprietary and the SUB vendor supply chain is critical to business success. Items to consider for vendor supply chain include: bag film source, any bag component and connectors, back-up bag manufacturing and irradiation sites and bag shipping protocols. Any single points of failure should be identified jointly with the vendor and a mitigation plan prepared.

In addition to the vendor supply chain, the internal supply chain needs to be able to forecast bag usage to plan appropriate inventory levels, taking into consideration bag expiry and space required for storage.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Layers of the Supply Chain

The source distinguishes two supply chain layers, each with different risks:

  • Generic consumables (tubing): can be sourced from multiple suppliers, low risk; this is a commoditized market
  • SUB bags (proprietary consumables): vendor lock-in, requiring in-depth management:
    • Bag film raw material: does the film have a sole-source supplier?
    • Backup manufacturing site: if the main site has a problem, has the backup site already been qualified?
    • Irradiation sites: gamma irradiation of bags is usually outsourced; does a backup irradiation site exist?
    • Shipping agreement: packaging, temperature control and transit time for large bags, especially for regions such as Taiwan that must import consumables

Key Point: Joint Identification of Supply Chain Risks

The source specifically emphasizes that single points of failure should be identified jointly with the vendor, rather than assessed unilaterally. This practice matters: the vendor knows its own supply chain far better than the customer, and many risk points are revealed only through deep collaboration. This is also why SUT vendor management is closer to a "partnership" than a purely "purchasing relationship", compared with traditional equipment vendor management.

Analogy: Bag Inventory Management Is Like Blood Bank Management

A hospital blood bank must keep enough blood in stock, but blood has a shelf life: too much is wasted, and too little means a shortage during emergency surgery. SUB bag inventory management follows the same logic: forecast usage from the production plan, set safety stock allowing for lead time, and rotate stock regularly to avoid expiry. The difference is that there are only a limited number of blood types, whereas a CDMO may manage bags of different sizes for different clients at the same time, so the complexity is higher.

CDMO Application Scenario: Designing Supply Chain Resilience

Practical steps for a CDMO to build SUB supply chain resilience:

  • Supplier qualification audit: require the SUB vendor to submit a Business Continuity Plan, and state the contingency arrangements in the supply agreement
  • Dual-supplier strategy: if the technology platform allows, consider qualifying bags from a second supplier (E&L and cell compatibility testing may need to be repeated)
  • ERP integration: track bag lot numbers, expiry dates and stock levels automatically in the ERP system, and set safety stock alerts
  • Customer communication: specify in the CMO contract the notification obligations and agreed mechanisms for a bag supply interruption
9.6 Application of Lessons Learned

Original Text

As this project was conducted in two phases, the second phase provided an opportunity to apply learnings from the first phase to the second so as not to have the same issues. After Action Reviews (AARs) were conducted with the various groups involved in the project (Operations, Validation, Engineering, Quality and the equipment vendor). The feedback included what went well and what did not along with ideas for improvement. We found the results could be divided into three high level buckets:

  • Communication (60%) — majority of issues
  • Documentation (30%)
  • Installation (10%) — including site acceptance testing and validation

These results were then turned into measurable actions and applied to the next phase.

Specific AAR themes:

  • Project schedule — get buy-in from all parties involved, communicate details early and often in the project and make sure the schedule is easy to follow. Keep everyone involved and engaged throughout the project.
  • Documentation — projects of this nature require extensive amount of both internal documentation and exchange of documents between vendors. A common portal site was used to keep track of external documents. Internal documents need to be carefully staggered during the project to ensure both timely delivery and not overwhelm reviewers and approvers.
  • Installation — initial estimates for disposable supplies needed for start-up and validation activities were low, substantially more supplies were needed than planned.

In addition to the AAR, a retrospective safety review of the first phase was conducted and updates to the equipment and installation plans were prospectively made for the second phase.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: AAR as a Structured Learning Mechanism

After Action Review (AAR) is a structured reflection tool that originated in military training and is increasingly common in pharmaceutical project management. GSK's AAR results reveal an important insight:

  • 60% communication issues: very typical of large cross-functional projects; technical problems are usually a minority, and people coordination is the biggest challenge
  • 30% documentation issues: the documentation density of an SUT project is far higher than that of a traditional equipment replacement, so managing the pace of documents (staggering) is critical
  • 10% installation issues: including a serious underestimate of consumables at the outset, a rookie trap of single-use technology

This 60/30/10 split is a strong guide for planning similar projects: the priority for resources should be communication > documentation > technology.

Key Point: The "First-Time Effect" in Estimating Validation Consumables

"Single-use consumable demand during installation far exceeds expectations" is a common lesson for SUT newcomers. The reasons:

  • Every validation test (IQ/OQ/PQ) requires new bags and tubing sets
  • Failed test runs must be repeated, doubling consumable use
  • Operator training needs practice consumables, which cannot all be counted in the validation plan

Recommendation: for a first SUB implementation, the quantity of consumables purchased should be 50–100% higher than calculated, and purchasing flexibility should be maintained.

Analogy: Document "Pacing" Is Like a Publication Deadline

Think of the editorial work on a monthly magazine: if all manuscripts arrive at once, the editors are overwhelmed; if they arrive in batches by section, the work proceeds in an orderly way. GSK's document pacing (staggering) follows the same logic: keep the workload of reviewers and approvers manageable throughout the project, rather than letting it pile up in a mountain before the deadline.

Practice Questions

  1. In a large project you have taken part in, what were the main categories of problems? Did they also fit the "communication > documentation > technology" pattern?
  2. If you were designing an AAR mechanism for an SUB implementation project, at what points would you run the AAR: at the end of each phase, or after the whole project is completed? What are the advantages and disadvantages of each approach?
9.7 Conclusions

Original Text

GlaxoSmithKline has successfully installed 2000 L SUBs in their existing clinical manufacturing facility and has observed cell titer and product yields that are comparable to their 1200 L stainless steel bioreactors. Single-use technologies, though appearing inherently simpler than stainless steel systems, present new challenges with operator training and a long-term dependence on the SUB vendor and their extended supply chain.

Installation in an existing facility presented some challenges with limitations in height and room adjacencies, but use of stainless steel and single-use hybrid process design enabled the design team to overcome these challenges. Existing infrastructure with ability for steam in place and clean in place made the hybrid approach possible.

9.8 Acknowledgements

  • GSK Team: SUB Selection Team, Operations, Process Development, Validation, Engineering, Quality
  • Hargrove Team: Process and Facility Design, Project Management and Planning
  • ABEC Inc for mixing and CFD support

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Trap of "Seemingly Simpler"

The most noteworthy insight in the conclusions: "Single-use technologies, though appearing inherently simpler than stainless steel systems, present new challenges..."

This statement breaks the common misconception that "single-use = simple". The complexity of stainless steel is concentrated in equipment maintenance and CIP/SIP systems; the complexity of single-use is spread across personnel training, inventory management and vendor relationship management. The former is an "engineering problem", the latter an "organizational problem", and many plants underestimate the difficulty of the latter when transitioning.

Core Concept: The Key Enabler of Hybrid Design

The source states explicitly the precondition for the success of the hybrid strategy: the existing facility has SIP (steam-in-place) and CIP (clean-in-place) capability. This means:

  • A newly built facility may not have this capability, so the applicability of the hybrid strategy needs to be reassessed
  • One advantage of retrofitting an existing plant: existing utility infrastructure can be used instead of starting from scratch
  • The capacity and layout of the CIP/SIP systems must be confirmed at the start of the facility assessment

CDMO Application Scenario: The Final Takeaways from the Case

Overall takeaways of the GSK UM-38 case for a CDMO:

  • Technology selection: use a structured qualitative + quantitative + weighted scoring system to make the decision defensible
  • Facility modification: minimize disruption to existing systems, execute in phases, and make full use of existing assets such as the high hat
  • Validation: allow sufficient manpower and consumables budget; the documentation development cost of a new technology is far higher than that of an equipment replacement
  • Supply chain: treat bags as "business-critical consumables" rather than "office supplies", and establish a formal supplier risk management framework
  • Continuous improvement: the AAR mechanism ensures that the learning from each project is not lost, and phased execution lets the learning be applied

Key Point: The Meaning of Performance Equivalence

The cell titer and yield of the 2000 L SUB were comparable to those of the 1200 L SS system. Note that this is "comparable", not "better". This result is sufficient for a commercial decision: achieving comparable product quality at larger scale, lower infrastructure cost and greater flexibility is the core of the SUB platform's value proposition.

Appendix 5 Section Summary

  • GSK's two-phase 2000 L SUB implementation is the most representative industry example of a large-scale single-use bioreactor implementation, and provides a complete decision framework from technology selection through facility modification to supply chain management.
  • Technology selection followed a three-step process of qualitative screening, then quantitative evaluation (blend time, kLa, CO₂ stripping, cell performance), then weighted scoring. The three highest weights were cell performance, Mixing/kLa and vendor supply chain.
  • The core strategies of facility modification were to make full use of the existing high hat space, adopt a hybrid SS + SUT design, and construct in phases to maintain business continuity; the operating platform added in Phase II based on Phase I experience reflects the improvement mechanism of a "learning organization".
  • Risk management tools included water/engineering batch operator training, FMEA, and the process flow map (used to design standardized tubing sets). Supply chain management must address single-point-of-failure risks such as bag film source, backup manufacturing site, irradiation sites and shipping agreements.
  • The AAR results showed the sources of problems: communication 60%, documentation 30%, installation 10%. This indicates that the biggest challenge in a large SUT implementation project is organizational coordination rather than the technology itself, and that the initial consumables estimate should include a 50–100% buffer.
  • The final conclusion: the 2000 L SUB achieved cell titer and yield comparable to the 1200 L SS system; single-use technology is "seemingly simpler" but brings new organizational challenges in operator training and vendor dependency; existing CIP/SIP infrastructure is the key enabler that makes the hybrid design strategy feasible.

ISPE Good Practice Guide: Single-Use Technology | p159 – p174

Learning Objectives for This Chapter

  • Understand the complete body of reference standards in the SUT field, and the positioning and weight of organizations such as ICH, ISO, USP, EP, ASTM, BPSA and BPOG within the regulatory framework for single-use technology
  • Become familiar with SUT-specific abbreviations and acronyms, building a common language for communicating with manufacturers, suppliers and regulators
  • Understand the formal definitions of key terms such as Extractables & Leachables, Single-Use System, Container Closure System and Functionally Closed Process, and the limits of their application in practice
  • Be able to locate the original source of a specific technical requirement quickly from the standards system, supporting vendor audits, regulatory submissions and internal risk assessments

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Five Pillars of SUT Reference Standards

The 129 references in this appendix can be grouped into five categories, which together make up the complete regulatory architecture for single-use technology:

  • Industry bodies: BPSA, BPOG, PDA - develop industry best-practice guidance, especially on E&L testing and standardization
  • Regulatory documents: FDA Aseptic Guidance (2004), EU Annex 1, ICH Q8/Q9/Q10 - the legal basis for regulatory compliance
  • Pharmacopoeias: USP, EP, JP - technical specifications for material test methods and acceptance criteria
  • International standards (ISO/ASTM): ISO 10993 (biocompatibility), ISO 11137 (radiation sterilization), the ASTM series - test methods and acceptance criteria
  • ISPE publications: Baseline Guides (Vol. 1–6), GAMP 5, the GEP Guide - professional guidance for pharmaceutical engineering

BPSA vs. BPOG: The Positioning of the Two Major SUT Industry Bodies

BPSA (Bio-Process Systems Alliance) is an alliance of single-use equipment manufacturers. Its technical guides represent the supplier's perspective and cover topics such as particulate testing and waste disposal.

BPOG (BioPhorum Operations Group) is a collaborative alliance of biopharmaceutical manufacturers (the end-users). Its guides focus more on issues of concern to users, such as E&L risk assessment, supplier management and change notification.

The "BPOG/BPSA Industry Proposal for Change Notification Practices" (Ref. 81), published jointly by the two, is an important industry consensus document.

Key Point: How the ICH Trio Applies to SUT

  • ICH Q8(R2) Pharmaceutical Development: supports the introduction of QbD for SUT, especially defining the design space for compatibility and E&L testing
  • ICH Q9 Risk Management: the core framework for SUT risk assessment (such as FMEA), referenced throughout the whole guide
  • ICH Q3D Elemental Impurities: the governing basis for metal leachables testing; metal residues from single-use bags are a focus of E&L review

Analogy

Think of the SUT regulatory standards system as a pyramid: the top layer is FDA/EU GMP (legally the most binding), the middle layer is ICH guidelines and ISO standards (international consensus), and the base layer is the BPSA/BPOG industry guidance (voluntary best practice). All three layers need to be cited in a regulatory submission, but the higher up the pyramid, the greater the legal weight of the document.

The Three Pharmacopoeias: The Roles of USP, EP and JP in SUT

USP (United States Pharmacopeia): <87>/<88> biological reactivity tests, <661> plastic packaging systems, <85> bacterial endotoxins test - the standard reference for US regulatory submissions

EP (European Pharmacopoeia): the 3.1.x series (polyethylene, polypropylene, etc.), 2.6.14 bacterial endotoxins, 3.2.9 rubber closures - material specifications for the EU market

JP (Japanese Pharmacopoeia): Section 7.03 test for rubber closure - must be met for submissions to the Japanese market

For simultaneous multi-region submissions, the requirements of all three pharmacopoeias must be met together, which is a complex challenge when designing the material selection and test plan.

Tutorial Commentary

Milestones in the Standardization of E&L Testing

Ref. 31 (Ding et al., 2014) is a landmark paper on a standardized E&L test protocol for single-use systems. It was developed jointly by several major pharmaceutical companies (Amgen, Biogen, etc.), established the industry consensus on test methods, and remains a core reference for E&L studies today.

Ref. 70 (Hammond et al., 2013) presents the well-known DMPP case (a harmful leachable compound from single-use containers), showing why an E&L assessment must include cytotoxicity testing and cannot rely on chemical analysis alone.

ASTM E3051 vs. E2500: An SUT-Specific Standard

ASTM E2500 (Ref. 40) is the general standard for specification, design and verification of pharmaceutical manufacturing systems and equipment, applicable to all pharmaceutical manufacturing systems.

ASTM E3051 (Ref. 41) is a 2016 standard written specifically for single-use systems. It explicitly covers guidance on the specification, design, verification and application of SUS, and is the first ASTM standard dedicated to SUT in the industry.

When auditing an SUT supplier or qualifying a system, E3051 is the more directly relevant reference document.

Radiation Sterilization Standard: The Importance of ISO 11137

Ref. 51 (ANSI/AAMI/ISO 11137-1) is the core standard for gamma sterilization validation of single-use systems, covering sterilization dose setting, dose distribution verification and routine control requirements. Single-use components from SUT suppliers are usually delivered pre-sterilized by gamma irradiation, so this standard is critical in supplier audits and sterilization qualification.

The Regulatory Framework for Particulate Control

Particulate control for injectables spans several levels of regulatory documents, and all of them must be met:

  • USP <788> (Ref. 49): numerical limits for sub-visible particles
  • USP <790> (Ref. 67): visual inspection for visible particles
  • EP 2.9.19: sub-visible particles (European Pharmacopoeia version)
  • EP 2.9.20: visible particles (European Pharmacopoeia version)
  • BPSA 2014 particulate guide (Ref. 50): recommendations for identifying and controlling the particulate sources specific to SUT components

Analogy: Why ISO/IEC 17025 Matters

Ref. 38 (ISO/IEC 17025) is the international standard for laboratory competence accreditation. Think of it as a "laboratory license": just as a medical license confirms that a physician is competent to practice, ISO 17025 accreditation confirms that a laboratory's test results are reliable, repeatable and comparable. When you ask an SUT supplier for an E&L test report, checking whether the laboratory holds ISO 17025 accreditation is the first step in judging the reliability of the data.

Practice Questions

  1. When commissioning an SUT supplier to perform E&L testing, which standards should you require the supplier to cite? What roles do the standardized protocol of Ref. 31 and the BPOG guide (Ref. 71) each play in the test plan?
  2. How do ASTM E3051 (Ref. 41) and ASTM E2500 (Ref. 40) differ in scope of application? Which standard should be cited in the qualification documents for a single-use bioreactor?
