Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Strategic Importance of USP <665>

USP <665> (Ref. 66, covering polymeric materials for SUT components) is the most forward-looking pharmacopoeial reference in this guide. This chapter is aimed specifically at polymeric components used in biopharmaceutical manufacturing and covers a complete test framework for E&L analysis, filling the gap that the traditional packaging pharmacopoeial chapters (<661>, <661.1>) cannot fully cover for SUT scenarios.

The Regulatory Framework for TSE/BSE Control

For SUT components that use animal-derived materials (such as animal-derived additives in rubber stoppers), TSE risk control must satisfy all of the following:

  • EU 97/534/EC (Ref. 27): prohibition on the use of certain animal-derived materials
  • EP 5.2.8 (Ref. 94): European Pharmacopoeia requirements for minimizing the risk of TSE transmission
  • EMA/410/01 rev.3 (Ref. 95): the EMA's specific TSE guidance
  • 9 CFR Part 94 (Ref. 93): USDA import controls to prevent the spread of animal disease in the US

The supplier's TSE/BSE compliance statement (CoC) is a must-check item in a supplier audit.

A Range of Methods for Package Integrity Testing

Refs 111–120 provide a complete matrix of ASTM standards for seal integrity testing of SUT:

  • ASTM F2338 (Ref. 112): vacuum decay method (non-destructive, most commonly used)
  • ASTM F88 (Ref. 113): seal strength test (destructive)
  • ASTM F1929 (Ref. 115): dye penetration method (visual confirmation)
  • ASTM E498/E499 (Refs. 116–117): mass spectrometer leak detection (most sensitive)
  • ASTM E432 (Ref. 120): guide for selecting a leak test method (a decision-tree tool)

According to USP <1207> (Ref. 63), the choice of integrity test method should be based on risk assessment, preferring deterministic methods such as vacuum decay over probabilistic methods such as the microbial challenge.

The Industry Consensus on BPOG/BPSA Change Notification

Ref. 81 (BPOG/BPSA Change Notification Proposal) addresses the biggest headache for SUT users: when a supplier changes a raw material or process, how can downstream customers be notified in time? This document sets an industry standard of "proactive change notification", requiring suppliers to notify customers of major changes 12 months before the effective date, giving drug manufacturers time to assess the impact and update their regulatory filings.

Practical Application

Scenario: the supplier of your single-use storage bags notifies you of a minor change in material specification. You need to decide whether it affects existing regulatory filings. Which standards should you cite for the assessment?

Suggested assessment framework:

  • Change management basis: ICH Q10 (Ref. 42) + ISPE PQLI Part 3 (Ref. 80)
  • E&L impact assessment: BPOG guide (Ref. 71) + the standardized protocol of Ref. 31
  • Material compatibility testing: USP <661.1> (Ref. 64) + the EP 3.1.x series (Refs. 73, 89–92)
  • Integrity confirmation: USP <1207> (Ref. 63) + ASTM F2338 (Ref. 112)
  • Judging whether the change is reportable: 21 CFR Part 820.100 (Ref. 123) + ICH Q6B (Ref. 124)
Appendix 7 – Glossary
7.1 Acronyms and Abbreviations

Original Text

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: A Functional Classification of SUT Abbreviations

These abbreviations can be divided by function into several groups to help you locate them quickly:

  • E&L analytical techniques: AET, GC-MS, HPLC, ICP-AES, ICP-MS, LC-MS, LC-UV, FTIR, IC, MS, NVR - the analytical toolchain for leachable identification and quantification
  • Single-use system body: SUS, SUB, SUT, CCS, PPC, PCM, EPDM, TPE, PE, PP, PET - identifying equipment, materials and components
  • Toxicological thresholds: AET, ADI, TDI, TTC, SCT, QT - quantitative decision tools in E&L risk assessment
  • Regulatory bodies: FDA/CDER, EMA, ICH, EDQM, ISO, BPSA, BPOG, PDA, ELSIE - the multi-region regulatory landscape
  • Process and quality: GMP, GLP, CAPA, CPP, QbD, QRM, FMEA, DOE, FAT, SAT, SOP, URS - the common language of manufacturing and quality management

Key Point: The Hierarchy of E&L Thresholds

Understanding how the E&L thresholds relate to each other is a core skill for SUT practitioners:

  • AET (analytical evaluation threshold): the lowest concentration that triggers identification and quantification. Below this value, no reporting is needed (except for PAHs, MBT and N-nitrosamines)
  • SCT (safety concern threshold): compounds below this value need no toxicological assessment
  • TTC (threshold of toxicological concern): the basis for assessing toxic potential, based on the Cramer classification
  • QT (qualification threshold): the concentration level at which toxicological qualification is required
  • ADI/TDI (acceptable/tolerable daily intake): the final safety assessment metric, based on human exposure

Analogy

Think of the E&L abbreviation group as the layers of air quality monitoring: the AET is the "detection limit" (can the instrument measure it?), the SCT is the "no-action line" (it was measured but raises no safety concern), the TTC is the "attention line" (more assessment is needed), and the ADI/TDI is the "safety standard" (an upper limit that must not be exceeded). In an SUT E&L assessment, each threshold corresponds to a different follow-up action requirement.

The Most Frequent SUT Abbreviations: Essential on the CDMO Floor

In the day-to-day work of an aseptic fill CDMO, the following abbreviations appear most often:

  1. SUS/SUB - single-use system / single-use bioreactor
  2. E&L - extractables and leachables
  3. AET - analytical evaluation threshold
  4. BPSA/BPOG - the two major industry bodies
  5. COA - certificate of analysis
  6. URS - user requirements specification
  7. FAT/SAT - factory/site acceptance testing
  8. CAPA - corrective and preventive action
  9. TSE/BSE - transmissible spongiform encephalopathy / bovine spongiform encephalopathy
  10. SAL - sterility assurance level
7.2 Definitions Part 1 (Absorption – Genotoxic)

Original Text

Absorption

Assimilation of molecules or other substances into the physical structure of a liquid or solid without chemical reaction.

Acceptance Criteria

The criteria that a system or component must satisfy in order to be accepted by a user, customer or other authorized entity.

Active Ingredient

Any component that is intended to furnish pharmacological activity or other direct effect in the diagnosis, cure, mitigation, treatment, or prevention of disease, or to affect the structure or any function of the body of man or other animals.

Adsorption

Adherence of molecules in solution or suspension to cells or other molecules – or to solid surfaces, such as chromatography media.

Analytical Evaluation Threshold (AET)

An upper limit, at or above which, identification and quantification of an unknown extractables and leachables should be performed and reported for potential toxicological assessment. This is not applicable to special case compounds such as Polyaromatic Hydrocarbon (PAH), Mercaptobenzothiazole (MBT) and N-nitrosamine, which should be evaluated individually.

Antioxidant

Compound that slows the rate of oxidation reactions.

Aseptic

Free of pathogenic (causing or capable of causing disease) microorganisms.

Autoclave

An apparatus into which moist heat (steam) under pressure is introduced to sterilize or decontaminate materials placed within (e.g. filter assemblies, glassware, etc.).

Bioburden

The concentration of microbial matter per unit volume. Microbial matter includes viruses, bacteria, yeast, mold, and parts thereof.

Bioreactor

A closed system (flask, roller bottle, tank, vessel, or other container) capable of supporting the growth of cells, mammalian or bacterial, in a culture medium in which a biological transformation takes place.

Bovine

Of, relating to, or from a cow: such as Bovine Blood: blood from a cow.

Calibration

The set of operations which establish, under specified conditions, the relationship between values indicated by a measuring instrument or measuring system, or values represented by a material measure or a reference material, and the corresponding values of a quantity realized by a reference standard.

Certificate of Analysis (COA)

A batch-specific document that is used to list test methods and results, including applicable specifications, acceptance criteria, and a final batch disposition.

Change Management (ICH Q10)

A systematic approach to proposing, evaluating, approving, implementing and reviewing changes.

Chromatography

Method of highly selective molecule separation using columns to purify proteins and other chemical products.

Closed Process

A process system that is designed and operated such that the product is never exposed to the surrounding environment. Additions to and draws from closed systems need to be performed in a completely closed fashion. (ISPE Baseline® Guide: Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities, Second Edition)

Compatibility

A measure of the extent to which a Primary Packaging Component (PPC), Process Contact Material (PCM), and/or proximal material will interact with a dosage form. Such interaction should not cause unacceptable changes in quality of either the dosage form or the packaging component. Such interactions may include (ab)adsorption of the active drug substance, leachable-induced degradation, precipitation, changes in drug product pH, discoloration, etc.

Conductivity

A measure of flow of electrical current through water.

Container Closure System (CCS)

The sum of packaging components that together contain, protect, and deliver the dosage form. This includes primary packaging components and secondary packaging components if the latter are intended to provide additional protection relative to product stability (e.g., foil pouch).

Containers

A receptacle for holding and/or transferring material.

Corrective and Preventive Action (CAPA)

A quality system defined by 21 CFR Part 820.100; the policies, procedures, and support systems that enable a firm to assure that exceptions are followed up with appropriate actions to correct a defined situation, and with continuous improvement tasks to prevent recurrence and eliminate the cause of potential nonconforming product and other quality problems.

Decontamination

A process that eliminates viable bioburden via use of sporicidal chemical agents. (FDA 2004 Aseptic Processing Guidance)

Dilution

Lowering the concentration of a solution by adding more solvent.

Elution

Washing out; removing adsorbed material with a solvent or buffering agent.

Endotoxins

Cell wall debris (lipopolysaccharide) from Gram-negative bacteria.

End-User

The pharmaceutical customer or user organization contracting a supplier to provide a product. In the context of this document: The pharmaceutical organization applying the single-use assemblies or components to produce a pharmaceutical drug substance. The end-user typically purchases the single-use assemblies from a supplier but can also build the single-use assemblies by purchasing single-use components and building its own assemblies.

Enzyme

A protein capable of producing chemical reactions (biocatalyst). Enzymes are involved in practically all biochemical reactions.

Excipient (ICH Q6B)

An ingredient added intentionally to the drug substance which should not have pharmacological properties in the quantity used.

Extractables

Chemical compounds that are removed from a material by exertion of an artificial, exaggerated force (e.g., solvent, temperature, or time). This is a material specific characteristic and is independent of the drug product with which the material is used.

Failure Modes and Effects Analysis (FMEA)

Method of reliability analysis intended to identify failures, at the basic component level, which have significant consequences affecting the system performance in the application considered.

Functionally Closed Process

Process systems that may be opened but are rendered closed by a cleaning, sanitization, and/or sterilization process that is appropriate or consistent with the process requirements, whether sterile, aseptic or low bioburden. These systems remain closed during production within the system. (ISPE Baseline® Guide: Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities, Second Edition)

Gamma Irradiation

A physical means of sterilization or decontamination also known as "cold process" that uses electromagnetic radiation of very short wavelengths. Gamma irradiation kills bacteria by breaking down bacterial DNA and inhibiting bacterial division. The most common source of gamma rays for irradiation processing comes from the radioactive isotope Cobalt 60.

Genotoxic

Substances which damage or modify deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA).

Glycosylation

Adding one or more carbohydrate molecules onto a protein (a glycoprotein) after it has been built by the ribosome; a posttranslational modification.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Fundamental Difference Between Extractables and Leachables

E&L is the most central safety assessment topic in SUT, and the essential difference between the two is as follows:

  • Extractables: compounds extracted from a material under deliberately aggressive conditions (strong solvents, high temperature, long duration). This is an inherent property of the material and has nothing to do with the actual drug product used. Think of it as a "list of potential threats"
  • Leachables: compounds that migrate from a material into the drug product or process fluid under normal or near-normal conditions of use. This is a result specific to the combination of drug product and material. Think of it as the "threats that actually occur"

Logical relationship: extractables testing is used to predict the worst case, and leachables testing is used to confirm actual exposure. Do E first and then L; the E results guide the L monitoring plan.

Closed vs. Functionally Closed Process: A Core Concept of SUT Design

Closed process: the product never contacts the environment. In SUT, a sealed single-use bag system is a typical example. It needs the fewest aseptic containment measures but places the highest demands on system integrity.

Functionally closed process: the system may be open, but is cleaned/sanitized/sterilized appropriately before use and remains closed during production. This is the more common scenario in SUT; for example, a bioreactor is open before inoculation but remains functionally closed during the culture.

These two definitions directly determine the GMP grade required for the room environment.

End-User vs. Supplier: The Terminology Behind the Division of Responsibility

The definition of End-User is especially important in this guide because it makes the division of responsibility explicit: the End-User is the pharmaceutical manufacturer applying SUT, and the Supplier is the manufacturer of the single-use components. Much of what the guide discusses, such as supplier management, the division of E&L testing responsibilities and COA review, rests on this definition.

It is worth noting that an End-User may purchase individual components and assemble them into a single-use system itself (that is, build-your-own). In this case the End-User also takes on part of the Supplier's role, and the E&L testing responsibility shifts accordingly.

Key Point: The AET Exception Clause

The AET definition contains an important exception: PAHs (polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons), MBT (mercaptobenzothiazole) and N-nitrosamines. These three classes of compounds must each be assessed individually however low their concentration, and the principle of not reporting below the AET does not apply. The reason is that they have known carcinogenic potential, so even trace exposure may raise a safety concern. When writing an E&L test plan, the specific monitoring arrangements for these three classes must be stated explicitly.

Analogy: Bioburden and SAL

Bioburden is the microbial load on an item before sterilization, like the "number of enemy troops" waiting to be eliminated. SAL (sterility assurance level) is the upper limit on the probability that any single sterilized item still carries a viable microorganism (usually 10-6, one in a million), like a "guarantee of mission success". The lower the bioburden, the lower the sterilization dose needed to reach the same SAL, and the less radiation damage to the material. This is the fundamental reason why supplier control of raw material bioburden is so important.

FMEA in SUT Risk Assessment

FMEA is the core risk tool cited repeatedly in this guide (Ref. 72 also gives a detailed reference). In an SUT context, typical applications of FMEA include:

  • Failure mode analysis of single-use components (leaks, material degradation, connection failure)
  • Systematic identification of E&L pathways (which component is most likely to leach at which process step)
  • Risk gap analysis when switching between SUS and stainless steel systems
7.2 Definitions Part 2 (Hydrophilic – Parenteral Drug)

Original Text

Hydrophilic

Having a strong affinity for water; attracting, dissolving in, or absorbing water; readily absorbing moisture; having strong polar groups that readily interact with water. Its opposite, hydrophobic.

Hydrophobic

The extent of insolubility; not readily absorbing water; resisting or repelling water, wetting, or hydration; or being adversely affected by water. Hydrophobic bonding is an attraction between the hydrophobic or non-polar portions of molecules, causing them to aggregate and exclude water from between them.

Integrity Test

In the context of this document: A test primarily done by the supplier as part of the release criteria. The end-user may also conduct the test prior to the use of the product. The test can be applied to single-use assemblies or to filters.

For single-use assemblies: The test is done to check the structural and mechanical integrity of the assembly. The test confirms that there are no leaks in the single-use assembly. The end-user should consider doing the test prior to use in critical applications.

For filters: It is typically a non-destructive and non-contaminating test used to determine if a filter can retain material of a specified size.

Intermediate

A material produced during steps of the synthesis of a new drug substance that undergoes further chemical transformation before it becomes a new drug substance.

Leachables

Chemical species that migrate from or through a contact surface into a drug product or process stream during storage or normal use conditions. These are specific to the combination of material and drug product with which the material comes in contact.

Migration

Release of substances (leachables) from the plastic component into the content of the container under conditions which reproduce those of the intended use.

Moiety

One of the portions into which something is divided; a component, part, or fraction. In chemistry, a specific section of a molecule, usually complex, that has a characteristic chemical effect or pharmacological property.

Monomer

The basic subunit from which, by repetition of a single reaction, polymers are made. For example, amino acids (monomers) condense to yield polypeptides or proteins (polymers).

Open Process

A process that is exposed to the environment and therefore requires environmental conditions to mitigate the risk of contamination from the environment. (ISPE Baseline® Guide: Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities, Second Edition)

Packaging

All operations, including filling and labeling, which a bulk product has to undergo in order to become a finished product.

Parenteral Drug

A parenteral drug is defined as one intended for injection through the skin or other external boundary tissue, rather than through the alimentary canal, so that active substances they contain are administered, using gravity or force, directly into a blood vessel, organ, tissue, or lesion.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Dual Meaning of Integrity Test

"Integrity testing" in the SUT context has two entirely different objects, which must be distinguished:

  • Single-use component integrity: tests whether a bag, connector or component has a structural leak. In essence this is a "is there a hole?" test, a physical assessment of seal integrity.
  • Filter integrity: tests whether a filter can retain particles of a specified size (such as the bubble point test). In essence this confirms that the filter's pore size meets specification.

The purpose, method and acceptance criteria of the two are completely different, so when "integrity test" is used in technical documents, the object should be specified explicitly.

Leachables vs. Migration: A Subtle Difference in Terminology

Leachables is the broader definition (any compound that migrates into the drug product or process fluid). It has a highly specific regulatory meaning in the pharmaceutical field and is the core term of E&L assessment.

Migration is defined in the European Pharmacopoeia and emphasizes release under simulated conditions of intended use; it is mainly used to describe migration from plastic container materials.

The two terms are often rendered by the same word in translation ("leaching" or "migration"), but in a regulatory context the subtle difference in the meaning of the original terms should be kept in mind.

Key Point: Open / Closed / Functionally Closed in Plant Applications

When designing an aseptic manufacturing plant, the process classification directly affects the facility grade requirements:

  • Open process: the product is exposed to the environment and needs a local protective environment of ISO 5 / Grade A. Typical example: traditional open aseptic filling.
  • Closed process: fully closed, and can be operated in a lower-grade environment (for example an ISO 8 / Grade C background). Typical example: closed isolator filling.
  • Functionally closed process: the typical scenario for biopharmaceutical upstream processing (media preparation, bioreactor culture). Based on risk assessment, it can be operated in a Grade C or Grade D environment.

Analogy: Hydrophilic vs. Hydrophobic in SUT Material Selection

When selecting a single-use filter, hydrophilicity and hydrophobicity are key material considerations:

  • Hydrophilic filters (such as PES, hydrophilic PVDF): suitable for aqueous filtration, need no pre-wetting, and are commonly used for buffer and drug solution filtration
  • Hydrophobic filters (such as PTFE, hydrophobic PVDF): not wetted by water, suitable for sterilizing-grade gas filtration (vent/air filters) and for applications that need to prevent water backflow

The hydrophilicity or hydrophobicity of a material also affects drug adsorption (proteins adsorb more readily onto hydrophobic surfaces), which is one of the focus points of SUT compatibility assessment.

The Definition of Parenteral Drug and Its Relevance to SUT

The definition of "parenteral drug" makes clear why safety standards for SUT in pharmaceutical manufacturing are so strict. Injectables go directly into blood vessels, organs or tissues and bypass the body's natural barriers (skin, gastrointestinal tract) entirely, so the safety assessment standards for any single-use material that may contact the drug product are far higher than for oral dosage forms. This is also the fundamental reason why injectables carry the strictest toxicological threshold requirements in E&L assessment.

Practice Questions

  1. If a minor leak is found in a single-use bioreactor bag during culture, which type of integrity test should be used for the root cause investigation? Which test method would you choose (see Refs 112–120)?
  2. A new drug's bioreactor culture process is switched from conventional stainless steel to SUS. Is this process closed, functionally closed or open? How does this classification affect the GMP grade design of the facility?
  3. The AET definition excludes PAHs, MBT and N-nitrosamines. Explain why these three classes of compounds need individual assessment, and how they should be handled specifically in the test plan.

Section Summary

  • The five pillars of the SUT standards system: the 129 references in this appendix span industry bodies (BPSA/BPOG), regulatory documents (FDA/EU GMP/ICH), pharmacopoeias (USP/EP/JP), international standards (ISO/ASTM) and ISPE guides. Knowing the legal status of each category and when to cite it is a foundational skill for SUT practitioners
  • Key E&L concepts: extractables (an inherent property of the material, extracted under forced conditions) vs. leachables (a result specific to the combination of material and drug product, migrating under normal conditions of use) are the core concepts of SUT safety assessment, and neither can be omitted
  • Regulatory impact of process classification: the classification of open / closed / functionally closed process directly determines the facility environment grade requirements, and is the starting point of GMP design when a CDMO designs a new facility or switches to SUT
  • The AET exception clause: the three special compound classes PAHs, MBT and N-nitrosamines are not subject to the AET screening rule and must each be fully assessed individually; this is an essential point when designing an E&L test plan
  • The contractual basis of supplier management: the BPOG/BPSA change notification consensus document (Ref. 81) and ASTM E3051 (Ref. 41) are the standards most directly relevant to SUT supplier audits and contract negotiation, and their core requirements should be familiar
  • Memorizing abbreviations by group: memorizing by function (analytical techniques, toxicological thresholds, component materials, regulatory bodies, process and quality) is more effective than rote memorization one by one. The most frequent SUT abbreviations in daily work: SUS, E&L, AET, BPSA/BPOG, COA, URS, FAT/SAT, SAL
  • The dual meaning of integrity test: physical integrity testing of single-use components (leak or no leak) and functional integrity testing of filters (whether pore size meets specification) use completely different methods, and the object must be specified explicitly in technical documents
